Preface

This book is written by a research laboratory rather than by a single author. The laboratory — a small team of cooperating AI research roles (an Inventor, an Explorer, a Formal Verifier, a Skeptic, an Application Researcher, a Historian and an Author) — set out to explore mathematical systems built from deliberately minimal assumptions: distinguishable objects, equality, and explicitly stated transformation rules. Numbers, positional notation, multiplication and even “value” were not assumed; where they appear in these pages, an earlier chapter shows how they emerge.

The book has one rule that overrides every other consideration:

Nothing is presented as established unless it has been proved, and nothing is presented as new unless we looked for it and failed to find it.

Every theorem, conjecture and refuted hypothesis carries a label:

Label Meaning
PROVEN (Lean) machine-checked in Lean 4 with Mathlib, standard axioms only
PROVEN (computer-assisted) a finite certificate checked by a program; the trust base is stated
PROVEN (written) a complete written proof, reviewed by the lab’s independent Skeptic
COMPUTATIONALLY SUPPORTED experiments support the claim; no proof
CONJECTURE a precise claim awaiting proof
REFUTED a counterexample is given — and kept, because failures teach
REDISCOVERY the result is already in the literature (references given)
POTENTIALLY NOVEL no equivalent found in the sources searched; not a priority claim

Most of what the laboratory found is, honestly, rediscovery: the commutative monoid of counts, chip-firing, rational base numeration, the Zeckendorf game, Knuth’s Fibonacci multiplication. We regard this as a success of the method — independent rediscovery from minimal assumptions is evidence that the structures are natural — and we document the literature carefully. Where something appears to be new (for example, the “glider” of borderline exchange rules, or the density of irreducibles for Knuth’s circle product), we say exactly how far our search went. We also report where a deeper, independent audit found our “novelty” already in the literature: Chapter 2 tells how a formula we first believed new turned out to follow, in most cases, from 2022 and 2026 papers on the homology of one-relator monoids.

How the book is organised. The ten volumes follow the plan with which the laboratory started, adjusted to what the research actually produced. Volumes 1–5 and 7–8 contain the research chapters, one per research cycle: foundations (quantity, exchange, termination, derivations), invented operations, emergent structures, alternative representations of quantities, transformations and relationships, computational experiments, and applications. Volumes 6, 9 and 10 are catalogues generated from the laboratory’s database: every verified theorem and open conjecture, every refuted hypothesis with its counterexample, and every open problem. They cannot drift from the evidence, because they are rebuilt from it each time the book is built. Volumes are unequal in size; no chapter was written to fill a volume.

Each chapter follows the same plan: motivation, definitions, examples, results with proofs or proof attempts, the computational experiments behind them (with experiment identifiers that can be re-run from the repository), counterexamples, applications, comparison with existing mathematics, limitations, and open questions.

How to verify anything in this book. Every experiment identifier (EXP-C01-05, …) names a deterministic script in the repository; python -m lab run EXP-C01-05 re-runs it and checks the recorded result hash. Every claim identifier (CLM-C08-01, …) has a record in db/claims/ with its full status history. Lean theorem names refer to proofs/lean/Infinity/.

Version 1.1. After the first edition the laboratory ran a hardening sprint instead of new research: an audit of the novelty labels of Chapters 12–21, Lean proofs of four theorems that had only written proofs, a re-run of every experiment, and a certified computation on two open spectrum questions of Chapter 6. Every change this caused to a claim’s status, label or statement is listed, with its reason, in the generated Corrections log at the end of the book.

1 How the laboratory works

1.1 Minimal assumptions, stated twice

Each research branch begins from a foundational system whose object-level axioms are written down explicitly (repository file research/systems/SYSTEMS.md):

  • System A — words over distinguishable symbols, juxtaposition, rewriting rules; no numbers.
  • System B — a carrier with one invented binary operation; no algebraic law is assumed, every law is a hypothesis to be tested.
  • System C — representations of quantities (digit strings with a valuation), admissible only after System A has derived counting.
  • System D — structures defined by relations only (game options, graphs).
  • System E — local-rule dynamics over invented algebras.

The meta level — the mathematics we use to prove things about these systems — is ordinary mathematics (formally, Lean’s type theory with Mathlib). When we say that a concept “emerges”, we mean that it is definable from the system’s primitives and forced by them up to isomorphism, as shown by a meta-level proof.

1.2 A research cycle

Every cycle runs ten steps, and the cycle report in the repository is organised by them:

  1. generate hypotheses — including at least one bold hypothesis that might fail;
  2. state them precisely;
  3. design experiments that could falsify them;
  4. implement the experiments (deterministic, seeded scripts);
  5. execute them (results are hashed; re-running checks the hash);
  6. analyse the results;
  7. attempt proofs or find counterexamples;
  8. compare with the literature (OEIS and web searches; references recorded);
  9. look for applications, with baselines and honest negatives;
  10. document everything in the journal and the database.

1.3 Kinds of proof

Lean proofs. Statements are formalised in Lean 4 with Mathlib; a file counts as verified only if it compiles with no sorry and #print axioms reports only propext, Classical.choice and Quot.sound.

Computer-assisted proofs. Some results are proved by finite certificates that a program checks: a completed rewriting system whose convergence is re-verified independently, an automaton whose count sequence obeys a recurrence of bounded order, a recurring window of a monotone map. For these the book states the trust base — the program code — and the cross-checks used to guard against implementation errors.

Written proofs are complete arguments reviewed by the lab’s Skeptic, who works independently from the authors and tries to break every claim.

1.4 Novelty

The Historian searches the OEIS (all integer sequences we produce are looked up, and the cached answers are kept in the repository) and the literature. Matches are read, not counted: in Chapter 9 a sequence agreed with an OEIS entry for 24 terms and then diverged; the agreement had a simple explanation and was recorded as a coincidence.

Volume 1 — Foundations and Alternative Mathematical Objects

The first volume builds quantity from nothing but symbols and rules. Chapter 2 shows that counting is the complete invariant of free rearrangement and that modular arithmetic is what a single relation does to counting. Chapter 3 lets place value emerge from local exchanges of tokens. Chapters 4 and 5 study the transformation systems themselves: when they stop, how long they take, and which games they define.

2 Quantity from transformation

Volume 1 — Foundations and alternative mathematical objects. Research cycle C01.

2.1 Motivation

Can numbers be derived rather than assumed? We start with nothing but distinguishable symbols, the empty word, juxtaposition, and rules that declare two words interchangeable. If quantity is real, it should appear as whatever survives the transformations — an invariant — and the invariant should be forced, not chosen.

2.2 Definitions

Let Σ\Sigma be a finite alphabet of qq symbols. A relation u↔vu \leftrightarrow v allows replacing a factor uu by vv (or back) anywhere in a word. The congruence generated by a set of relations identifies two words when one can be transformed into the other by finitely many such replacements. An invariant is a map that is constant on congruence classes; it is complete if it separates different classes.

A border of a word ww is a nonempty proper prefix of ww that is also a suffix; ww is unbordered if it has none. The autocorrelation polynomial of ww (|w|=k|w| = k) is cw(x)=1+∑bxk−|b|c_w(x) = 1 + \sum_{b} x^{k-|b|}, the sum over the borders bb of ww.

2.3 Counting emerges

Theorem 2.1 (counting is the complete invariant of commutation) [PROVEN — Lean: commEq_iff_count; REDISCOVERY]. Modulo the relations xy↔yxxy \leftrightarrow yx (x,y∈Σx, y \in \Sigma), two words are congruent if and only if every symbol occurs equally often in both. The projection of a word onto a symbol ss — delete all other symbols — is the unary word s⋯ss\cdots s.

So the only information that survives free rearrangement is a family of unary words, one per symbol. This is the laboratory’s justification for introducing the natural numbers: they are the classes of the one-symbol free monoid, and they are the invariant.

Theorem 2.2 (one symbol: a clock with a tail) [PROVEN — Lean: gen_iff, exactly_m_add_p_classes; REDISCOVERY]. On words over one symbol aa, the relation am=am+pa^m = a^{m+p} (p≥1p \ge 1) produces exactly m+pm + p classes: ax∼aya^x \sim a^y iff x=yx = y, or x,y≥mx, y \ge m and x≡y(modp)x \equiv y \pmod p.

Theorem 2.3 [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY]. Several relations ami=ania^{m_i} = a^{n_i} generate index M=minmiM = \min m_i and period P=gcd(ni−mi)P = \gcd(n_i - m_i). (Two independent algorithms agree with this on 6159 relation sets, EXP-C01-02.)

Modular arithmetic is therefore also emergent: it is what one relation does to counting.

Theorem 2.4 (multiplication is composition) [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY]. The substitutions a↦aka \mapsto a^k of the one-symbol free monoid, with pointwise juxtaposition as addition and composition as multiplication, form a commutative semiring isomorphic to (ℕ,+,×)(\mathbb{N}, +, \times). With two symbols modulo commutation, composition is matrix multiplication and is no longer commutative.

2.4 A bold conjecture fails

Conjecture (H-C01-4) [REFUTED]. Any single relation that only rearranges symbols — like aab↔abaaab \leftrightarrow aba — eventually identifies all words with the same symbol counts.

Counterexample. Under aab↔abaaab \leftrightarrow aba, the word baabaa contains neither side, so its class is {baa}\{baa\}, while aab,aba,baaaab, aba, baa have the same counts. All 33 rearranging relations of length 3 and 4 fail in the same way (EXP-C01-04).

2.5 An unexpected Fibonacci pattern

The failed experiment produced something better than a confirmation. For each relation, count the classes of words of length nn over {a,b}\{a, b\}:

relation n=1,2,3,…n = 1, 2, 3, \ldots
all six length-3 relations 2, 4, 7, 12, 20, 33, 54, 88, 143, 232, 376
25 of 27 length-4 relations 2, 4, 8, 15, 28, 52, 96, 177, 326, 600, 1104
aaba↔abaaaaba \leftrightarrow abaa (and complement) 2, 4, 8, 15, 28, 52, 97, 181, 338, 631, 1178

The first row is Fn+3−1F_{n+3} - 1 (partial sums of Fibonacci numbers), the second the partial sums of tribonacci numbers. The explanation is the self-overlap structure of the words involved.

Theorem 2.5 (border containment) [PROVEN — written, Skeptic-reviewed; the list identities of the critical-pair join are checked in Lean (common_border_joinable); REDISCOVERY: Kurth 1987]. Let u≠vu \neq v have length kk and suppose every border of uu is a border of vv. Then the rewriting system {u→v}\{u \to v\} terminates and is confluent; the classes of length-nn words correspond to the words avoiding uu; and ∑n≥0#{classes of length n}xn=cu(x)xk+(1−qx)cu(x).\sum_{n \ge 0} \#\{\text{classes of length } n\}\, x^n = \frac{c_u(x)}{x^k + (1 - q x)\, c_u(x)} . For unbordered uu this is 1/(1−qx+xk)1/(1 - qx + x^k); for q=2q = 2 the coefficients are partial sums of (k−1)(k-1)-bonacci numbers.

Proof sketch. Every step changes the word in the same lexicographic direction inside a finite set, so derivations terminate. Two overlapping occurrences of uu overlap along a border bb of uu; since bb is also a border of vv, writing u=pb=bsu = pb = bs and v=p′b=bs′v = p'b = bs' one checks vs=p′u→p′vvs = p'u \to p'v and pv=us′→vs′=p′vpv = us' \to vs' = p'v, so the critical pair joins in one step on each side. Newman’s lemma gives confluence, and the count of uu-avoiding words is the Guibas–Odlyzko formula. ∎

The exceptional relations are those whose two sides share a border. Testing that observation led to a precise conjecture.

Conjecture 2.6 (common borders) [COMPUTATIONALLY SUPPORTED; partly settled by the literature; POTENTIALLY NOVEL only in the case “common border without containment”]. For all u≠vu \neq v of length kk, the class-count series is c(x)/(xk+(1−qx)c(x))c(x)/(x^k + (1-qx)c(x)) with c(x)=1+∑bxk−|b|c(x) = 1 + \sum_{b} x^{k - |b|}, the sum over the words bb that are borders of both uu and vv.

Evidence. No counterexample among all 2159 relations with binary k≤6k \le 6 and ternary k≤4k \le 4 (up to n=12n = 12, resp. 77), including 1326 relations not covered by Theorem 2.5 (EXP-C01-06).

Theorem 2.7 [PROVEN — computer-assisted; REDISCOVERY for 1620 of the 1632 relations]. Conjecture 2.6 holds for all nn for 1632 of these relations. Method: Knuth–Bendix completion finds a finite convergent system (independently re-verified: rules decrease, all critical pairs join, uu and vv have equal normal forms); its irreducible words are counted by an automaton with ss states, so the count sequence obeys a recurrence of order ≤s\le s; the conjectured series obeys one of order kk; equality of the first s+k+5s + k + 5 terms therefore implies equality forever (EXP-C01-07). Every certificate is stored with the experiment, every completed rule is re-derived from u=vu = v by search, and the Skeptic re-verified all 1632 certificates with independently written code. The remaining 527 relations have infinite completions, e.g. aba↔bababa \leftrightarrow bab completes to bab→ababab \to aba together with bajba→abaabj−1b a^j b a \to a b a a b^{j-1} for all j≥2j \ge 2.

2.5.1 What the literature already knew

Our Historian’s independent audit found that much of this territory had been mapped by a different route — homological algebra of one-relator monoids:

  • Theorem 2.5 is the length-preserving case of Kurth’s (1987) one-rule confluence criterion, and Kurth’s criterion is an iff: u→vu \to v is confluent exactly when every border of uu is a border of vv (we confirmed this on 28,850 pairs, EXP-C01-08).

  • When uu and vv share no border, Conjecture 2.6 follows from a 2026 theorem of Gray and Steinberg together with Anick’s theory of strongly free relations:

    Theorem 2.8 [PROVEN — corollary of published theorems, every step checked by the Skeptic; REDISCOVERY]. If u≠vu \ne v of length kk have no common border, the class-count series is 1/(1−qx+xk)1/(1 - qx + x^k).

    Proof idea. No common border means the monoid is “incompressible”, so it has Hochschild cohomological dimension at most 2 (Gray–Steinberg 2026, Thm 8.1); the trivial module of K⟨Σ⟩/(u−v)K\langle\Sigma\rangle/(u - v) then has the resolution 0→A(−k)→A(−1)q→A→K→00 \to A(-k) \to A(-1)^q \to A \to K \to 0, whose Euler characteristic is the formula. ∎ Every one of the 527 relations that our completion method could not settle lies in this case, so all 2159 relations of EXP-C01-06 are now proved.

  • With exactly one common border, growth identities read off from Gray–Steinberg (2022) imply the conjectured formula provided a certain multiplication map on a left ideal is injective. The Skeptic found that this lemma was asserted but not proved; it holds in all 2591 cases tested, so the case is “proved modulo one lemma”.

What remains open is the case of two or more common borders without containment. The formula holds in every case tested: the Skeptic’s search covered 295,358 relation orbits and 1,491 targeted relations with one to four common borders, up to n=27n = 27, without a single failure. The episode is recorded as a lesson: two quick web searches were not enough to establish novelty.

2.6 Experiments

Experiment Question Result
EXP-C01-01 Is counting the complete invariant? 25 exact slices, no exception
EXP-C01-02 Index/period of one-symbol relations 6159 relation sets, 0 disagreements
EXP-C01-03 Do substitutions form (ℕ,+,×)(\mathbb N,+,\times)? all laws on a0..a12a^0..a^{12}; non-commutative with 2 symbols
EXP-C01-04 Does one rearrangement give counting? refuted 33/33; Fibonacci pattern found
EXP-C01-05 Unbordered-side hypothesis 756 relations, 0 failures
EXP-C01-06 Common-border conjecture 2159 relations, 0 failures
EXP-C01-07 Proofs for all nn 1632 proved, 527 open

2.7 Comparison with existing mathematics

Theorems 2.1–2.4 are classical: Parikh vectors (1966) and trace monoids (Cartier–Foata 1969), monogenic semigroups, the endomorphism semiring of ℕ\mathbb{N}. Theorem 2.5 is Kurth (1987) with Guibas–Odlyzko (1981). For Conjecture 2.6 see the previous section: the no-common-border case is a corollary of Gray–Steinberg (2026) and Anick (1982), and the homological counterpart of the whole common-border dichotomy is Gray–Steinberg (2022, Theorem B). The explicit growth formula with common borders was not found.

2.8 Applications and limitations

Complete invariants turn equivalence testing from a search into a computation (counting decides commutation-equivalence in linear time). Theorem 2.5 also measures how much information a single “invisible” local rewrite destroys: modulo aab=abaaab = aba, binary words carry log2φ≈0.694\log_2 \varphi \approx 0.694 bits per symbol instead of 1. These are observations, not benchmarked algorithms.

2.9 Open questions

  1. Prove the injectivity lemma that completes the one-common-border case, then the case of two or more common borders, perhaps by induction over Adjan–Oganesyan compression.
  2. Is there a common-border formula for two or more relations?
  3. Formalise Theorem 2.5 completely in Lean.

3 Numeration from exchange

Volume 1 — Foundations. Research cycle C02.

3.1 Motivation

Chapter 2 showed that a multiset of tokens is a number. Positional notation is usually introduced by fiat (“the digit in the second place is worth two”). Here we ask whether place value can be discovered: put tokens on levels 0,1,2,…0, 1, 2, \ldots and allow only local exchanges.

3.2 Definitions

A configuration is a finitely supported vector c=(c0,c1,…)c = (c_0, c_1, \ldots) of token counts. A carry rule m[k]→∑j=1Jbj[k+j]m[k] \to \sum_{j=1}^{J} b_j [k+j] removes mm tokens from a level holding at least mm and adds bjb_j tokens jj levels higher. A configuration is stable if every ck<mc_k < m. The carry polynomial is P(x)=∑jbjxj−mP(x) = \sum_j b_j x^j - m and the emergent base rr is its unique positive root. Nothing about value is assumed — but solving mwk=∑jbjwk+jm\,w_k = \sum_j b_j w_{k+j} for exchange-invariant weights forces wk=rkw_k = r^k: value is the invariant of the exchange rule, V(c)=∑kckrkV(c) = \sum_k c_k r^k.

3.3 Examples

  • 2[k]→[k+1]2[k] \to [k+1]: from nn tokens at level 0 one obtains the binary digits of nn. Base bb arises from b[k]→[k+1]b[k] \to [k+1].
  • 3[k]→2[k+1]3[k] \to 2[k+1]: base 3/23/2 (the rational base of Akiyama, Frougny and Sakarovitch): 1,2,20,21,22,210,…1, 2, 20, 21, 22, 210, \ldots (OEIS A024629).
  • 3[k]→[k+1]+[k+2]3[k] \to [k+1] + [k+2]: base r=(13−1)/2≈1.3028r = (\sqrt{13}-1)/2 \approx 1.3028, digits 0..20..2: 1,2,110,111,112,220,221,222,12100,…1, 2, 110, 111, 112, 220, 221, 222, 12100, \ldots (not in the OEIS).
  • Fibonacci exchanges [k]+[k+1]→[k+2][k]+[k+1] \to [k+2], 2[k]→[k+1]+[k−2]2[k] \to [k+1] + [k-2] (with boundary versions) produce Zeckendorf representations.

3.4 Results

Theorem 3.1 (abelian property) [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY: chip-firing]. For a carry rule, firings at different levels commute. Hence from any configuration either all maximal derivations are infinite, or all are finite, end at the same stable configuration, have the same length, and fire every level the same number of times.

Theorem 3.2 (integer bases) [PROVEN — written]. Under b[k]→[k+1]b[k] \to [k+1] every derivation from nn tokens ends at the base-bb expansion after exactly (n−sb(n))/(b−1)(n - s_b(n))/(b-1) exchanges (sbs_b = digit sum).

Theorem 3.3 (termination dichotomy) [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY (folklore)]. If ∑bj<m\sum b_j < m, every derivation terminates. If ∑bj>m\sum b_j > m, then r<1r < 1 and no derivation from n>(m−1)/(1−r)n > (m-1)/(1-r) tokens terminates, because every stable configuration has value at most (m−1)/(1−r)(m-1)/(1-r).

The borderline ∑bj=m\sum b_j = m is the interesting case: the rule 2[k]→[k+1]+[k+2]2[k] \to [k+1] + [k+2] started from 6 tokens leaves a pattern (2,1)(2, 1) that glides upward forever. In the upward sweep the “window” of the next JJ levels evolves by a monotone map whose fixed point is the tail-sum vector T=(∑j≥1bj,∑j≥2bj,…)T = (\sum_{j\ge 1} b_j, \sum_{j \ge 2} b_j, \ldots): every level fires exactly once.

Theorem 3.4 (critical rules) [PROVEN — two written proofs; POTENTIALLY NOVEL — Propp (2017) proposed this rule family without results]. If ∑bj=m\sum b_j = m, every derivation from nn tokens is infinite for all sufficiently large nn. Hence a carry rule terminates from every unary start if and only if ∑bj<m\sum b_j < m.

Proof idea. Write the window map as F(x)=Ax−φ(x)bF(x) = Ax - \varphi(x)\,b with φ∈[0,1)\varphi \in [0,1) the floor error. The eigenvalues of AA are 1/μ1/\mu for the roots of ∑jbjμj=m\sum_j b_j \mu^j = m; when the support of bb has gcd 1, μ=1\mu = 1 is a simple root and every other root satisfies |μ|>1|\mu| > 1. So AA has the simple eigenvalue 1 (eigenvector TT, left eigenvector (1,…,1)(1, \ldots, 1) — token conservation) and all other eigenvalues inside the unit disk. The floor errors stay bounded off the TT-direction, so after O(logn)O(\log n) levels the window is a large multiple of TT plus a bounded error, hence dominates TT, and the glider runs forever. ∎ For all 98 critical rules with m≤5m \le 5, J≤4J \le 4 every start beyond 124 is certified non-terminating (EXP-C02-07).

The lab’s Skeptic, asked to break this theorem, instead found a second proof that uses only positivity: the linearised window map is a primitive non-negative matrix (its cycles have lengths jj with bj>0b_j > 0, of gcd 1), so some power of it is strictly positive and a large enough start dominates TT after a bounded number of levels. This second proof was checked line by line by the laboratory’s Formal Verifier, so the theorem rests on a reviewed proof; the Skeptic also simulated 668,793 critical rules, all of which glide.

Theorem 3.5 (radix order) [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY in substance: Akiyama–Frougny–Sakarovitch 2008, Prop. 11]. For terminating carry rules, n<n′n < n' implies that the representation of nn precedes that of n′n' in radix order. (Add one token and stabilise. If HH is the highest level that fires, the levels H+jH + j with bj>0b_j > 0 gain tokens and never fire, so the highest level whose count changes lies above HH: it only gained tokens. An earlier version of this argument had a gap, found by the Skeptic.)

Theorem 3.6 (uniqueness) [PROVEN — Lean core: digits_unique; REDISCOVERY in substance: Akiyama–Frougny–Sakarovitch 2008, Lemma 5]. Let gg be the gcd of the levels reachable in one exchange and P̃\tilde P the carry polynomial in the variable y=xgy = x^g. If P̃\tilde P is primitive and irreducible over ℚ\mathbb{Q}, distinct stable configurations on the reachable levels have distinct values; in particular, when ∑bj<m\sum b_j < m, each integer has exactly one representation. If P̃\tilde P is not primitive and ∑bj≤m\sum b_j \le m, uniqueness fails. (Both side conditions are needed: the Skeptic’s counterexamples are 2[k]→4[k+1]2[k] \to 4[k+1], non-primitive yet without collisions, and 2[k]→3[k+1]2[k] \to 3[k+1], which has no stable configuration of value 2.)

Proof idea. A difference Δ\Delta of two digit strings with equal value vanishes at the root; an irreducible P̃\tilde P must then divide Δ\Delta over ℚ\mathbb{Q} (a Bézout identity evaluated at the root rules out coprimality), hence over ℤ\mathbb{Z} by Gauss’s lemma; but a nonzero multiple of P̃\tilde P has a trailing coefficient divisible by mm, while digit differences are smaller than mm. ∎

A refuted hypothesis. We first conjectured uniqueness for every terminating rule. The rule 4[k]→2[k+1]4[k] \to 2[k+1] (base 2 with digits 0..30..3) refutes it: “2” and “10” both have value 2. All failures among the 31 terminating small rules have a non-primitive reduced carry polynomial.

3.5 The Fibonacci exchanges are the Zeckendorf game

Theorem 3.7 [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY]. The Fibonacci exchanges terminate from every configuration at the Zeckendorf representation of its value, and the shortest derivation from nn tokens has n−z(n)n - z(n) steps (z(n)z(n) = number of Zeckendorf terms). Termination uses the potential (token count, ∑kckg(k)\sum_k c_k g(k)) with g(0)=−12g(0) = -\tfrac12, g(k)=kg(k) = k; the shortest derivation adds tokens one at a time, which needs only merges.

This system is exactly the Zeckendorf game of Baird-Smith, Epstein, Flint and Miller (2018), who proved termination and the shortest game n−Z(n)n - Z(n), and whose Figure 4 already lists the longest games for n≤49n \le 49 — identical to our exhaustive values for n≤40n \le 40 (0,1,2,3,5,6,8,10,…,780, 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 8, 10, \ldots, 78). Cusenza et al. (2022) proved the greedy strategy optimal; a closed form is known only for n=Fk−1n = F_k - 1. The sequence is not yet in the OEIS.

3.6 Limitations and open questions

  1. An explicit bound n0n_0 for Theorem 3.4, and its Lean formalisation.
  2. Which carry rules give regular languages of representations?
  3. A closed form for the longest Zeckendorf game.

4 When does rewriting stop?

Volume 1 — Foundations. Research cycle C03.

4.1 Motivation

A transformation system is only a computation if it stops. For the simplest non-trivial systems — a single rule moving a block of 0s past a block of 1s while multiplying them, 0p1q→1r0s(p,q,r,s≥1),0^p 1^q \to 1^r 0^s \qquad (p, q, r, s \ge 1), we ask: from which rules does every sequence of transformations terminate?

4.2 Easy cases

Theorem 4.1 [PROVEN — Lean: terminating_of_count_lt]. If a rule decreases the number of some letter (s<ps < p or r<qr < q), it terminates.

Theorem 4.2 [PROVEN — written; swap case in Lean]. Rules that conserve a letter count (s=ps = p or r=qr = q) terminate.

Theorem 4.3 [PROVEN — Lean: not_terminating_of_loop, not_terminating_double]. A derivation u→+xuyu \to^+ x\,u\,y (a loop) proves non-termination, and every rule in the doubling region r≥2qr \ge 2q, s≥2ps \ge 2p has a two-step loop.

4.3 Two hypotheses that failed

H-C03-1 [REFUTED]: “a rule terminates iff some letter count never grows.” Zantema’s problem 0011→1110000011 \to 111000 grows both counts and yet terminates — a famous published result (Geser 1993), which our own search corroborates on all 3747 words of length ≤12\le 12 containing 00110011, but which we did not prove independently.

H-C03-2 [REFUTED, Lean-checked]: “a rule terminates iff r<2qr < 2q or s<2ps < 2p.” The rule 011→11100011 \to 11100 has r=3<4=2qr = 3 < 4 = 2q yet loops: 001111→411100111100100001111 \to^4 111\,001111\,00100 (Lean: not_terminating_0_11). There are 40 counterexamples with parameters ≤5\le 5.

4.4 Divisibility

The decisive surprise was a pair of almost identical rules: 0011→111000000011 \to 11100000 (s=5s = 5) appears to terminate, while 0011→1110000000011 \to 111000000 (s=6s = 6) loops. The difference is whether the produced block of zeros is a whole number of consumed blocks.

Lemma 4.4 (lifting) [PROVEN — Lean: step_map, loop_map]. The morphism 0↦0p0 \mapsto 0^p carries every loop of 01q→1r0k0\,1^q \to 1^r 0^k onto a loop of 0p1q→1r0kp0^p 1^q \to 1^r 0^{kp}.

One base loop thus certifies infinitely many rules. Together with exhaustive search this classifies the family almost completely:

Theorem 4.5 [PROVEN — computer-assisted; REDISCOVERY]. Of the 625 rules with p,q,r,s≤5p, q, r, s \le 5, 525 terminate (Theorems 4.1–4.2) and 76 do not (re-checked loop certificates); 24 remained open in our hands, all predicted to terminate, including Zantema’s problem.

Theorem 4.6 (Zantema–Geser) [PROVEN in the literature; REDISCOVERY — our conjecture is equivalent to it]. 0p1q→1r0s0^p1^q \to 1^r0^s is non-terminating iff (r≥2qr \ge 2q and s≥2ps \ge 2p), or (s≥2ps \ge 2p, q<r<2qq < r < 2q and p∣sp \mid s), or (r≥2qr \ge 2q, p<s<2pp < s < 2p and q∣rq \mid r).

We first stated this as a conjecture (zero contradictions on 625 rules). The laboratory’s Historian later located the 1994 technical report of Zantema and Geser, whose Theorem 1.1 says exactly this — rule terminates iff p≥sp \ge s, or q≥rq \ge r, or p<s<2pp < s < 2p and q∤rq \nmid r, or q<r<2qq < r < 2q and p∤sp \nmid s — and whose page 2 lists the three non-terminating cases in the same words. So the 24 open rules all terminate, and our intermediate hypothesis H-C03-3 (“non-terminating iff both counts grow and r≥2qr \ge 2q or s≥2ps \ge 2p”) is refuted by the published theorem: 0011→111000000011 \to 11100000 terminates.

Loops can be long: the rule 016→17060\,1^6 \to 1^7 0^6 needs 9,332 steps and grows a 56,035-letter word before its starting word reappears inside it.

4.5 Comparison with existing mathematics

The family was completely characterised by Zantema and Geser (tech. report UU-CS-1994-44; RTA 1995; AAECC 2000). Our first attempts to retrieve the theorem failed (the usual repository hosts refused the download); the Historian found the scanned report on an older archive host, and Conjecture 4.6 turned out to be equivalent to their theorem (Theorem 4.6 above): their Theorem 1.1 characterises termination, our conjecture listed the non-terminating cases, and the lists match case by case. Their Lemma 4.1 is our doubling loop, and their Lemma 4.3 reduces the divisible cases to q=1q = 1 exactly as our lifting lemma does. Hard terminating cases such as Zantema’s problem admit no simple matrix interpretations (none exist in the tiers we searched) and need the Zantema–Geser method. Hard terminating cases such as Zantema’s problem admit no simple matrix interpretations (none exist in the tiers we searched) and need match-bounds or the Zantema–Geser method.

4.6 Open questions

  1. Compare Conjecture 4.6 with the Zantema–Geser characterisation. Done: identical (Theorem 4.6).
  2. An independent, machine-checkable termination proof for the 24 rules (e.g. RFC match-bounds) — they terminate by the published theorem.
  3. Explain the kqk^q growth of loop lengths in the base family.

5 How long does rewriting take?

Volume 1 — Foundations: transformation systems. Research cycle C04.

5.1 Motivation

Chapter 4 asked whether a transformation system stops. Once it does, the length of a derivation is itself a quantity produced by the system, without any arithmetic having been put in. This chapter asks three questions about the smallest systems — a single rule l→rl \to r over the letters a,ba, b:

  1. how long can derivations from words of length nn be?
  2. when does the order in which rewrites are applied not matter?
  3. what games does a rule define if two players take turns rewriting?

5.2 Definitions

A step rewrites one occurrence: xly→xryx\,l\,y \to x\,r\,y. A normal form contains no occurrence of ll. D(w)D(w) is the maximal number of steps from ww to a normal form, and the derivational complexity is D(n)=max|w|=nD(w)D(n) = \max_{|w| = n} D(w). A rule has random descent (RD; van Oostrom) if from every word all maximal derivations have the same length and end in the same normal form. A border of ll is a nonempty proper prefix of ll that is also a suffix. In the rewriting game two players alternately make one step; a player who cannot move loses (normal play), and positions get Sprague–Grundy values.

5.3 Which rules terminate?

Theorem 5.1 [PROVEN — computer-assisted]. Of the 196 rules with 1≤|l|≤31 \le |l| \le 3, 0≤|r|≤30 \le |r| \le 3, exactly the 34 in which ll is a factor of rr are non-terminating. Each of the other 162 has a checked certificate: length decrease (70 rules), lexicographic monotonicity of length-preserving steps (70), a linear interpretation over ℕ\mathbb{N} (20; e.g. ab→bbaab \to bba with a↦3xa \mapsto 3x, b↦x+1b \mapsto x + 1), or a factor count that drops at every step (2).

The bold generalisation fails. Hypothesis H-C04-0 — “a rule with |l|≤3|l| \le 3 terminates iff ll is not a factor of rr” — is refuted as soon as |r|=4|r| = 4 is allowed: ab→bbaaab \to bbaa does not terminate although abab does not occur in bbaabbaa: aab→abbaa=abbaa→bbaabaa=bb⋅aab⋅aa.aab \to a\,bbaa = abbaa \to bbaa\,baa = bb\cdot aab\cdot aa . The extended census (|r|≤5|r| \le 5, 868 rules) has 18 such rules; two symmetry classes (aab→ababaaab \to ababa, aab→babaaaab \to babaa) were left undecided here (EXP-C04-02) and are settled — both terminate — in Chapter 21.

5.4 One rule, one invariant

The following elementary fact organises the rest of the chapter.

Theorem 5.2 (constant-step potential) [PROVEN — written; consequences in Lean: steps_length_unique, length_changing_unique]. For every rule l→rl \to r with l≠rl \ne r there are Φ:Σ*→ℤ\Phi : \Sigma^* \to \mathbb{Z} and δ≠0\delta \ne 0 such that every step u→vu \to v satisfies Φ(v)=Φ(u)+δ\Phi(v) = \Phi(u) + \delta. Consequently all derivations between two given words have the same length, and a terminating one-rule system is confluent if and only if it has random descent.

Proof idea. If |l|≠|r||l| \ne |r| take Φ(w)=|w|\Phi(w) = |w|. Otherwise pick a letter cc whose position sets PP in ll and QQ in rr differ; the power sums ∑j∈Pjm\sum_{j \in P} j^m and ∑j∈Qjm\sum_{j \in Q} j^m differ for some m<|l|m < |l| (Vandermonde), and for the least such KK the potential Φ(w)=∑i:wi=ciK\Phi(w) = \sum_{i : w_i = c} i^K changes by the same amount at every offset by the binomial theorem. ∎ Checked on 470,927 steps of 5,365 rules (EXP-C04-06), where K≤2K \le 2 always sufficed.

Theorem 5.3 (local criterion) [PROVEN — Lean: random_descent; REDISCOVERY: van Oostrom 2007]. If every local peak b←a→cb \leftarrow a \to c can be joined by two derivations of equal length, the system has random descent (from any word that has a normal form).

Theorem 5.4 [PROVEN — Lean: unbordered_diamond, unbordered_random_descent; REDISCOVERY]. If ll is unbordered, two different one-step reducts of a word have a common one-step reduct; hence l→rl \to r has random descent. (Two overlapping occurrences of ll would exhibit a border.)

A refuted converse. Hypothesis H-C04-1 claimed RD iff ll is unbordered. The rule aa→aaa \to a has a bordered left side but random descent: every step shortens one run of aa’s, so every derivation from ww has exactly ∑runs(length−1)\sum_{\text{runs}} (\text{length} - 1) steps and ends in the same word. Eighteen bordered rules with |l|,|r|≤3|l|, |r| \le 3 have RD (EXP-C04-05).

5.5 Derivational complexity

Theorem 5.5 [PROVEN — Lean: inv_step, normal_iff_inv_zero, derivation_length_eq_inv, p_positions; REDISCOVERY]. Under ab→baab \to ba each step lowers the number of inversions by one; every complete derivation from ww has exactly inv(w)\mathrm{inv}(w) steps, so D(n)=⌊n2/4⌋D(n) = \lfloor n^2/4 \rfloor; and in the rewriting game the losing positions for the player to move are the words with an even number of inversions.

This is the extremal case of Métivier’s conjecture, proved by Bertrand (1994): length-preserving one-rule systems never need more than n2/4n^2/4 steps.

Theorem 5.6 [PROVEN — written]. For ab→bbaab \to bba and ab→baaab \to baa, D(n)=2n−1−1D(n) = 2^{n-1} - 1. (Scanning ww from the right, an aa that meets BB letters bb crosses them in BB steps and leaves 2B2B behind it.)

Observation 5.7 [COMPUTATIONALLY SUPPORTED]. The 162 terminating rules fall into 55 symmetry classes with complexity linear (144 rules), quadratic (14: ⌊n2/4⌋\lfloor n^2/4 \rfloor, ⌊n(n−1)/6⌋\lfloor n(n-1)/6 \rfloor, ⌊n2/8⌋\lfloor n^2/8 \rfloor) or exponential (4: 2n−1−12^{n-1} - 1); 54 classes have a simple closed form on all computed terms (EXP-C04-03).

One class does not: the rule aab→baaab \to ba refutes the hypothesis (H-C04-G) that every complexity sequence satisfies a linear recurrence.

Theorem 5.8 [PROVEN — written; POTENTIALLY NOVEL (small)]. For aab→baaab \to ba, D(n)=n−1−κ(n),κ(n)=min{k≥0:2k+k≥n},D(n) = n - 1 - \kappa(n), \qquad \kappa(n) = \min\{k \ge 0 : 2^k + k \ge n\}, so D(n)=n−log2n+O(1)D(n) = n - \log_2 n + O(1); DD stalls exactly at n=2k+k+1n = 2^k + k + 1 (4,7,12,21,38,…4, 7, 12, 21, 38, \ldots), and ∑D(n)xn\sum D(n) x^n is not a rational function.

Proof idea. Write w=ax0bax1b⋯baxmw = a^{x_0} b\, a^{x_1} b \cdots b\, a^{x_m}. Rewriting aabaab moves two aa’s before the (k+1)(k{+}1)-th bb to one aa after it: this is the exchange 2[k]→[k+1]2[k] \to [k+1] of Chapter 3 — a binary counter — with the last level inert. The value ∑kxk2k\sum_k x_k 2^k is invariant, each step removes one token, and aabaab is unbordered, so every derivation has the same length, determined by the binary expansion of the value. Optimising over ww gives n−D(n)=minm(m+⌈(n−m)/2m⌉)=1+κ(n)n - D(n) = \min_m (m + \lceil (n-m)/2^m \rceil) = 1 + \kappa(n). Since the stall positions have unbounded gaps, no linear recurrence can produce them. ∎ (Checked for all words of length ≤21\le 21 and as an identity to n=20000n = 20000.)

The same numbers turned up somewhere unexpected. OEIS A247983 counts how many terms of the series log2=∑h≥11/(h2h)\log 2 = \sum_{h \ge 1} 1/(h\,2^h) are needed for nn bits of accuracy; the entry gives no formula.

Theorem 5.9 [PROVEN — written, Skeptic-reviewed; POTENTIALLY NOVEL (small)]. For every n≥1n \ge 1, A247983(n)(n) =n−max{j≥0:2j+j≤n+1}=D(n+2)= n - \max\{j \ge 0 : 2^j + j \le n + 1\} = D(n+2) for aab→baaab \to ba. (The Skeptic noted that the identity fails at n=0n = 0, outside the OEIS offset.)

Proof idea. The tail log2−∑h≤k1/(h2h)\log 2 - \sum_{h \le k} 1/(h 2^h) lies between 2−k/(k+2)2^{-k}/(k+2) (Jensen) and 2−k/(k+1)2^{-k}/(k+1), which pins down the first kk with tail below 2−n2^{-n}. ∎ (Checked with exact arithmetic for n≤1500n \le 1500, EXP-C04-10.) The coincidence has a simple reason: both quantities measure how far the counter 2k+k2^k + k has advanced.

5.6 Games from rewriting

Theorem 5.10 [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY]. If a rule has random descent, the Grundy value of its game at ww is D(w)mod2D(w) \bmod 2: such games are decided by parity alone.

Theorem 5.11 [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY: octal games as rows of pins]. For a terminating rule ak→ua^k \to u the game on ww is the disjoint sum of games on the maximal runs of aa’s, and each run game is a take-and-break game. In particular

rule game
aa→baa \to b Dawson’s Kayles (0.07) — period 34 from n=53n = 53
aa→abaa \to ab Officers (0.6) — periodicity unknown
aaa→baaa \to b Treblecross (0.007) — periodicity unknown
aaa→abaaa \to ab 0.06 — no period ≤300\le 300 up to n=3000n = 3000
aaa→abaaaa \to aba 0.4 — period 34

So the two-letter, one-rule system aa→abaa \to ab already encodes a famous open problem: whether the Grundy values of Officers are ultimately periodic (they have been computed to beyond 101410^{14} without a period appearing).

A bold hypothesis refuted. H-C04-6 guessed that Grundy values of these games never exceed 3 (true on all words of length ≤10\le 10). The single word a14a^{14} under aa→baa \to b has Grundy value 4.

5.7 Experiments

5.8 Comparison with existing mathematics

Random descent and its local criterion are due to van Oostrom (RTA 2007) and have been formalised in Isabelle; our Lean development is an independent re-formalisation. Quadratic and exponential one-rule complexities are standard examples (Kobayashi; Métivier’s conjecture, settled by Bertrand). The octal games are classical (Guy–Smith 1956; Winning Ways). A brief search found no prior statement of Theorem 5.2 or of the analysis of aab→baaab \to ba; Theorem 5.2 is elementary enough to be folklore and is labelled accordingly in the database (“not assessed”).

5.9 Applications and limitations

For one-rule systems the cost of normalisation is predictable without simulation (Theorem 5.2): any one derivation tells the length of all of them. Closed forms such as ⌊n2/4⌋\lfloor n^2/4 \rfloor, 2n−1−12^{n-1} - 1 and n−log2n+O(1)n - \log_2 n + O(1) are exact worst-case costs of bubble-sort-like swaps, doubling processes and binary-counter carries. These are observations; no practical improvement over existing tools is claimed.

5.10 Open questions

  1. Do aab→ababaaab \to ababa and aab→babaaaab \to babaa terminate? Yes — see Chapter 21, which also gives their exact (exponential) complexities.
  2. For |l|=4\lvert l\rvert = 4, do complexities other than polynomial, exponential and n−Θ(logn)n - \Theta(\log n) appear?
  3. Is the Grundy sequence of aaa→abaaa \to ab (octal game 0.06) ultimately periodic? (For aa→abaa \to ab this is the open Officers problem.)

Volume 2 — Invented Operations and Their Properties

Here a single binary operation is invented and nothing about it is assumed: every law is a hypothesis. Chapter 6 maps which laws have finite models of which sizes; Chapter 7 anchors an operation to a successor and finds that the sizes admitting a model form arithmetic sets — powers of two for left-distributivity (Laver’s tables), the odd numbers, and the moduli in which the golden ratio exists.

6 Which sizes can a single law allow? Spectra of magma identities

Volume 2 — Invented operations and their properties. Research cycle C05.

6.1 Motivation

The barest invented operation is a magma: a set and a multiplication table, with no laws at all. Impose a single equation, say x=y(x(xy))x = y(x(xy)), and most tables are cut away. This chapter asks the simplest question one can put to such a law: for which sizes nn does any table satisfy it? There are no numbers in the definition, yet the answers are pieces of arithmetic — congruences modulo 3 and 4, single forbidden sizes, long gaps, as in triple systems and Euler’s 36 officers. We found these patterns before learning that the Equational Theories Project (ETP) had studied such laws in 2024–25, so the census is a rediscovery; we then pushed into the sizes its notes leave open.

6.2 Definitions

A magma (M,*)(M, \ast) of order n=|M|n = |M| is given by its Cayley table; we write xyxy for x*yx \ast y. A law is an identity x=tx = t, where tt is a term in the variables x,yx, y; its order is the number of multiplications in tt. The spectrum of a law is the set of n≥1n \ge 1 for which some magma of order nn satisfies it for all values of the variables. Numbers such as “law 167” refer to the ETP’s list of equations.

A law is nontrivial if xx occurs in tt but is neither its leftmost nor its rightmost leaf. Otherwise only n=1n = 1 works (xx absent: every element equals t(y)t(y)), or a projection ab=aab = a or ab=bab = b is a model of every order. A law and its mirror (tt read right to left) have the same spectrum, via the opposite table a*′b=b*aa \ast' b = b \ast a. A quasigroup is a magma whose table is a Latin square. An affine model over ℤ/n\mathbb{Z}/n is a table xy=ax+by+cxy = ax + by + c. We write spectra on n=1,2,…n = 1, 2, \ldots as strings: 1 = a model exists, 0 = none, ? = undecided within the solver budget.

Model existence is decided by SAT: one variable per table entry and value, plus one per subterm, assignment of x,yx, y and value. The encoding is exact (proofs/paper/C05.md) and matches brute force on all magmas of orders 2, 3.

6.3 The census

Observation 6.1 [COMPUTATIONALLY SUPPORTED; REDISCOVERY: Equational Theories Project]. There are 115 nontrivial laws x=t(x,y)x = t(x, y) of order 2–4 (2, 15 and 98 of orders 2, 3 and 4). Of these, 52 have models of every order n≤6n \le 6; 2 have none of any order 2≤n≤62 \le n \le 6; and 61 have a restricted spectrum. Mirror pairs always agree. Extended to n≤8n \le 8, the 63 non-full laws fall into 15 patterns:

pattern, n=1..8n = 1..8 laws example (ETP number) what is known
10111011 16 x=y(x(xy))x = y(x(xy)) (63), x=y((xx)y)x = y((xx)y) (115) 115: conjectured all n≠2,6n \ne 2, 6
10110010 4 x=y(x(yy))x = y(x(yy)) (66) n≡0,1(mod3)n \equiv 0, 1 \pmod 3, n≠6n \ne 6 (Mendelsohn)
10011??1 1 x=(yx)(xy)x = (yx)(xy) (167) n≡0,1(mod4)n \equiv 0, 1 \pmod 4 (Theorem 6.2, which also settles the two ? as absent)
11011011 8 x=y((xy)(yy))x = y((xy)(yy)) (883) contains n≡1,2(mod3)n \equiv 1, 2 \pmod 3
10001011 8 x=y(x(x(yy)))x = y(x(x(yy))) (467) open
1000101? 4 x=y(x((yx)y))x = y(x((yx)y)) (677), x=y(((yx)x)y)x = y(((yx)x)y) (1313) open; up to 10 exactly the orders 1, 5, 7, 9 (Theorem 6.11), and 16 occurs (Theorem 6.10), so Conjecture 6.6 is refuted

10111111 4 x=(yx)((xy)y)x = (yx)((xy)y) (1685) all n≠2n \ne 2
1000001? 2 x=y(((xy)x)y)x = y(((xy)x)y) (1286) open; smallest nontrivial model has order 7

The last column comes from the ETP sources under Comparison; our data agree with every size stated there. The 16 Dupont-type laws have one model up to isomorphism at each of n=3,4,5n = 3, 4, 5, always a medial quasigroup; the order-3 model of law 907 is the Steiner quasigroup; law 167 has 58 models of order 4 up to isomorphism, not all quasigroups. The census’s non-existence answers are SAT results that we did not certify further.

6.4 A law that counts modulo 4

Theorem 6.2 [PROVEN — Lean: law167_card_mod_four; REDISCOVERY: Equational Theories Project]. Every finite magma satisfying x=(yx)(xy)x = (yx)(xy) has order n≡0n \equiv 0 or 1(mod4)1 \pmod 4.

Proof. Send an unordered pair {a,b}\{a, b\} to F{a,b}={ab,ba}F\{a, b\} = \{ab, ba\}. The law gives (ab)(ba)=b(ab)(ba) = b and (ba)(ab)=a(ba)(ab) = a, so FF is an involution. If a≠ba \ne b then ab≠baab \ne ba, since otherwise F(F{a,b})F(F\{a,b\}) would be a single element. So FF permutes the (n2)\binom n2 pairs of distinct elements. It fixes none of them: {ab,ba}={a,b}\{ab, ba\} = \{a, b\} forces a=ba = b through the law in both possible cases. A fixed-point-free involution on a finite set has an even number of points, so 4∣n(n−1)4 \mid n(n-1). ∎

Lean follows this argument via Mathlib’s Sym2 and its fixed-point congruence for maps of prime-power order, and checks models of orders 4, 5 (T4_law167, T5_law167) and the absence of order 2 (no_law167_order2). The converse is the ETP write-up’s construction: pair up the two-element subsets as {a,b},{c,d}\{a, b\}, \{c, d\} and set ab=cab = c, ba=dba = d, cd=bcd = b, dc=adc = a, xx=xxx = x. So the spectrum is exactly {n≡0,1(mod4)}\{n \equiv 0, 1 \pmod 4\}.

6.5 Pushing the open cases

Theorem 6.3 [PROVEN — Lean: left_mul_bijective_of_law; REDISCOVERY (standard)]. If a finite magma satisfies x=ys(x,y)x = y\,s(x, y) for some term ss, every left translation a↦yaa \mapsto ya is a bijection: each row of the table is a permutation.

Proof. y⋅s(x,y)=xy \cdot s(x, y) = x shows that a↦yaa \mapsto ya is onto, and an onto map from a finite set to itself is one-to-one. ∎

Theorem 6.3, and fixing 0*0∈{0,1}0 \ast 0 \in \{0, 1\} (relabelling always allows it), are sound extra constraints for the solver. With them we decided the 15 laws of unknown spectrum in the ETP notes for all n≤10n \le 10 but a few UNKNOWN sizes, with no disagreement with any size stated in the notes. What is new relative to the notes:

Theorem 6.4 [PROVEN — Lean decide certificates; POTENTIALLY NOVEL relative to the ETP notes]. Law 677, x=y(x((yx)y))x = y(x((yx)y)), has models of orders 5, 7 and 9. Laws 1286, x=y(((xy)x)y)x = y(((xy)x)y); 1313, x=y(((yx)x)y)x = y(((yx)x)y); and 1516, x=(yy)(x(xy))x = (yy)(x(xy)), each have a model of order 9.

Proof. We give explicit Cayley tables found by SAT. Lean’s kernel checks each law on each table (E677_order5_law, E677_order7_law, E677_order9_law, E1286_order9_law, E1313_order9_law, E1516_order9_law). ∎

The same pipeline certified an order-9 model of law 467. That size is not new: the notes construct models of 467 on ℤ[i]/(k+il)\mathbb{Z}[i]/(k + il) whenever k2+l2k^2 + l^2 is odd, and 9=32+029 = 3^2 + 0^2. We first missed this and recorded it as new; a second reading corrected it.

Theorem 6.5 [PROVEN — computer-assisted, drat-trim-verified; POTENTIALLY NOVEL for n≥5n \ge 5]. The following laws have no model of the given orders: law 501, x=y(y(x(xy)))x = y(y(x(xy))), orders 6 and 7; law 677, order 6; law 883, x=y((xy)(yy))x = y((xy)(yy)), order 6; law 1076, x=y((x(xy))y)x = y((x(xy))y), order 8; law 1083, x=y((x(yx))y)x = y((x(yx))y), orders 5 and 6. The same holds for 501 at order 3 and 677 at orders 3 and 4. These are new relative to the notes but small enough that ETP searches probably settle them.

Proof. For each case Glucose 4 logs a DRUP refutation of the CNF formula, and the independent checker drat-trim verifies it (all 48 frontier refutations passed). What remains trusted is drat-trim, the CNF generator and the soundness of the two extra constraints. ∎

Conjecture 6.6 [REFUTED — by the Skeptic’s review; see Theorem 6.10]. “Laws 677 and 1313 both have spectrum {1}∪{n odd:n≥5}\{1\} \cup \{n \text{ odd} : n \ge 5\}.”

Both laws have models of orders 5, 7, 9 and none of orders 2, 3, 4, 6, all certified except orders 5 and 7 of law 1313 (stated in the notes, found again by SAT, tables re-checked). Orders 8 and 10 were undecided; the order-5 models are idempotent quasigroups. The first version of this book stated the conjecture on this evidence. It is false: both laws have models of order 16 (Theorem 6.10). The evidence was too thin — four odd sizes and four small gaps say little about larger even sizes — and the search space had a blind spot: the lab looked for affine models only over ℤn\mathbb{Z}_n, whose additive group is cyclic, never over the fields GF(2k)\mathrm{GF}(2^k).

6.6 Three bold hypotheses fail

Hypothesis 6.7 (H-C05-1) [REFUTED]. “A single law x=tx = t has spectrum {1}\{1\} or all of ℕ\mathbb{N}: one identity cannot restrict sizes.” Law 167 is a counterexample (Theorem 6.2), and 61 of the 115 census laws are others.

Hypothesis 6.8 (H-C05-Q) [REFUTED]. “Every nontrivial law forces its finite models to be quasigroups.” Counterexample: this model of law 167, whose row 1 repeats the value 2 (T4_law167, T4_not_latin):

*\ast 0 1 2 3
0 0 3 1 0
1 0 2 2 2
2 2 0 1 3
3 1 3 1 3

Eighteen census laws already have non-quasigroup models of order at most 3.

Hypothesis 6.9 (H-C05-A) [REFUTED]. “A law has models of every order n≤6n \le 6 iff it has an affine model over every ℤ/n\mathbb{Z}/n, n≤6n \le 6.” Counterexample: x=(yx)(x(yy))x = (yx)(x(yy)). It has models of every order up to 6, and of order 2 it is satisfied by NAND, xy=¬(x∧y)xy = \lnot(x \land y): yy=¬yyy = \lnot y, x(yy)=¬x∨yx(yy) = \lnot x \lor y, yx=¬x∨¬yyx = \lnot x \lor \lnot y, so (yx)(x(yy))=¬((¬x∨¬y)∧(¬x∨y))=x(yx)(x(yy)) = \lnot\big((\lnot x \lor \lnot y) \land (\lnot x \lor y)\big) = x (nand2_law). Yet no affine operation over ℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2 satisfies it (no_affine_model_mod2); for n≤6n \le 6 its affine models exist only at n=1n = 1 and n=5n = 5. Three more laws (two mirror pairs in all) behave the same way, and in 39 restricted laws some sizes of the spectrum have no affine model.

6.7 After the review: linear models over finite fields

The Skeptic found the counterexample to Conjecture 6.6 by looking at linear operations over a field with 16 elements. We then searched every prime power q≤128q \le 128 systematically (EXP-C05-07). For x*y=ax+byx \ast y = ax + by a term t(x,y)t(x, y) evaluates to αx+βy\alpha x + \beta y, where α\alpha and β\beta are polynomials in aa and bb; the law x=tx = t holds exactly when α=1\alpha = 1 and β=0\beta = 0 in GF(q)\mathrm{GF}(q).

Theorem 6.10 [PROVEN — computer-assisted, three tables Lean-checked; NOT ASSESSED: linear models are a standard tool of the ETP]. For the 15 open laws of this chapter, the prime powers q≤128q \le 128 admitting a linear model over GF(q)\mathrm{GF}(q) are those listed in EXP-C05-07. In particular:

  • laws 677 and 1313 have models of order 16 — e.g. x*y=(1+t3)x+t3yx \ast y = (1 + t^3)x + t^3 y for 677 and x*y=t3x+(1+t3)yx \ast y = t^3 x + (1 + t^3) y for 1313, in GF(16)=GF(2)[t]/(t4+t+1)\mathrm{GF}(16) = \mathrm{GF}(2)[t]/(t^4 + t + 1) (the Skeptic’s examples); our search finds other pairs (a,b)(a, b) as well;
  • laws 670, 1076 and 1286 also have models of order 16, and 1286 one of order 32;
  • law 670 has a model of order 9, a size the SAT search had left UNKNOWN;
  • no linear model exists at any order that the SAT search, the DRUP certificates or the ETP notes exclude (0 conflicts).

Proof. The search is exhaustive over GF(q)2\mathrm{GF}(q)^2, and GF(q)\mathrm{GF}(q) is unique up to isomorphism, so “no linear model of order qq” does not depend on the representation. One model per law and order was re-checked on its full table (the Skeptic’s independent computation re-checked all of them, with a different representation of the fields), and Lean’s kernel checks the tables E677_order16_law, E1313_order16_law and E670_order9_law. ∎

Because direct products of models are models, the spectra of 677 and 1313 now contain every product of the orders 5, 7, 9, 16, … — including all 16⋅m16 \cdot m with mm in the spectrum. Which even orders below 16 occur was left open; the next section settles 8 and 10.

6.8 Orders 8 and 10 (version 1.1)

A direct SAT search with Latin rows and the symmetry break 0⋆0∈{0,1}0 \star 0 \in \{0, 1\} (EXP-C05-03) could not decide the two laws at n=8n = 8 and 1010. Two observations make the search small enough to finish and to certify.

Lemma 6.11a [PROVEN — Lean: C05_Law1313.lean]. Every finite model of law 1313 is a quasigroup. Proof. Rows are permutations because the law has the form x=y⋆Tx = y \star T. Given ww, put x=y⋆wx = y \star w; the law gives y⋆T=y⋆wy \star T = y \star w with T=((y⋆x)⋆x)⋆yT = ((y \star x) \star x) \star y, so T=wT = w and the column of yy takes the value ww. ∎ For 677 only the rows are known to be permutations; finite models of 677 that are not right-cancellative exist (ETP blueprint), so no column constraint may be used there.

A case split guided by the hard cases. Relabel so that some element is 0 and row 0 is a fixed permutation of a given cycle type. A first split on the cycle type alone left 41 of the 97 order-10 cases of 677 undecided at 300,000 conflicts each — almost all of them cases where 0 is idempotent. So we split first on idempotency: either every element is idempotent, or 0 can be chosen non-idempotent; in both cases 0 is chosen with the fewest fixed points in its row (among the non-idempotent elements in the second case), which gives every competing row at least as many fixed points. Implied “division” clauses (if y⋆a=xy \star a = x then the inner term TT equals aa) shorten the search further. The split is complete (Lemma 8 of proofs/paper/C05.md): 45 cases for n=8n = 8 and 97 for n=10n = 10.

Theorem 6.11 [PROVEN — computer-assisted; 677 at order 8: REDISCOVERY (Mace4, ETP paper); the rest POTENTIALLY NOVEL]. Neither law 677 nor law 1313 has a model of order 8 or 10. Consequently the orders n≤10n \le 10 of their models are exactly 1, 5, 7 and 9, for both laws.

Proof. All 284 case CNFs are unsatisfiable (EXP-C05-08). Each refutation is a DRAT proof from CaDiCaL that drat-trim verified — 40.5 million proof lines in all, the hardest case of 677 needing 1.33 million conflicts. The same pipeline finds models at orders 5 and 7 and none at 6, as the direct search did. ∎

Law 677 matters beyond this chapter: whether it implies x=((x⋆x)⋆x)⋆xx = ((x \star x) \star x) \star x (law 255) in finite magmas is the last implication between laws of order at most 4 that the Equational Theories Project could not settle. A finite counterexample would have to be a model of 677, so Theorem 6.11 rules out orders 8 and 10 — a small, certified data point, nothing more.

6.9 Experiments

6.10 Comparison with existing mathematics

Classical spectra. Steiner triple systems exist exactly for n≡1,3(mod6)n \equiv 1, 3 \pmod 6 (Kirkman, 1847). Mendelsohn triple systems exist for n≡0,1(mod3)n \equiv 0, 1 \pmod 3, n≠6n \ne 6 (N. S. Mendelsohn, 1971). Pairs of orthogonal Latin squares exist for all n≠2,6n \ne 2, 6 (Bose, Shrikhande and Parker, 1959–60; Euler’s 36 officers). Spectra of quasigroup identities have a long literature, for example Bennett and Mendelsohn on Stein quasigroups.

The Equational Theories Project (T. Tao et al., https://teorth.github.io/equational_theories/; paper arXiv:2512.07087, https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.07087) settled the implications between the 4694 laws of order at most 4. Two notes on the project’s Zulip treat spectra:

  • “Spectra of order-3 equations” (https://leanprover.zulipchat.com/user_uploads/3121/C8gCuSGHJXw9lUPe23ouRkVM/Order_3_spectra.pdf) proves that law 66 has the Mendelsohn spectrum and law 167 the spectrum {0,1mod4}\{0, 1 \bmod 4\}. B. Le Floch’s notes cite “Ben Gunby-Mann’s notes” for law 167, which we take to be this write-up.
  • B. Le Floch, “Spectra of equational laws” (July 10, 2025, https://leanprover.zulipchat.com/user_uploads/3121/apvdoKH3jwd1mrnPxZcvlXL_/spectrum.pdf) lists the 48 laws of order at most 4 that may have a non-full spectrum. Of these, 31 have no model of order 2 and 14 have one of order 2 but none of order 3. For many of them it gives only “unknown spectrum starting with …”.

Not new here: Observation 6.1 as a whole; Theorem 6.2 (our addition is the Lean proof); Theorem 6.3; the Mendelsohn spectrum of law 66; all gaps at order 2 and law 883’s gap at order 3, which the notes list; law 467 at order 9. New relative to the notes: the memberships in Theorem 6.4 and the gaps in Theorem 6.5, of which those at n≥5n \ge 5 are the plausible additions. We retrieved the ETP paper only partly, and its model searches may already contain these facts, so the novelty is relative to the sources we read. The three refuted hypotheses were our own.

6.11 Applications and limitations

The reusable output is a certification pipeline for finite algebra: a model becomes a table checked by Lean’s decide, a non-existence result a DRUP proof checked by drat-trim, so neither direction rests on trusting the SAT solver. Laws such as 66 or 907 also turn a solver into a generator of triple-system-like designs, a well-known use of quasigroups; we ran no comparison with dedicated design algorithms.

Limitations. Only two-variable laws x=tx = t of order at most 4 were studied; the census stops at n≤6n \le 6 (n≤8n \le 8 and 1010 for subsets); solver budgets left UNKNOWN entries. Most non-existence results of the census are trusted from the solver — only the 48 frontier instances are independently checked. An isomorphism-grouping step of EXP-C05-02 compared non-canonical tables, so its count is not used.

6.12 Open questions

  1. Decide laws 677 and 1313 at orders 12 and 14 (8 and 10 are excluded by Theorem 6.11, 16 occurs), and law 1286 at orders 8 and 10, with the case split of Theorem 6.11.
  2. Is the spectrum of law 115 all n≠2,6n \ne 2, 6, as the notes conjecture? Our data agree up to n=8n = 8.
  3. Direct products of models are models, so every spectrum is closed under multiplication. Which multiplicatively closed sets arise from a single law of small order?
  4. Which laws admit a finite-order “pair map” like FF in Theorem 6.2, and which congruences do such maps force?
  5. Extend the census to laws of order 5 and to three variables, where the central groupoid law (spectrum: the squares, by Knuth) already appears.

7 Anchored operations and arithmetic spectra

Volume 2 — Invented operations and their properties. Research cycle C06.

7.1 Motivation

An invented operation with no laws is just a table. A law such as associativity cuts the tables down, and Chapter 6 asks which sizes admit any model. Here we add one more ingredient, the simplest link between an operation and counting: we anchor the operation to the successor, x⋆0=x+1(modN),x \star 0 = x + 1 \pmod N, and ask, for a single composition law, for which sizes NN a model exists and how many there are. The answers turn out to be pieces of arithmetic.

7.2 Definitions

A successor-anchored magma of size NN is a binary operation ⋆\star on ℤ/N\mathbb{Z}/N with x⋆0=x+1x \star 0 = x + 1. The spectrum of a law is the set of NN admitting an anchored model. An isomorphism of anchored structures must fix 0 and commute with x↦x⋆0x \mapsto x \star 0, so it is the identity; we therefore count tables. A table is affine if x⋆y=x+ay+1x \star y = x + a y + 1 for some a∈ℤ/Na \in \mathbb{Z}/N.

7.3 The classical case: left-distributivity

For the left-distributive law x⋆(y⋆z)=(x⋆y)⋆(x⋆z)x \star (y \star z) = (x \star y) \star (x \star z) the anchor produces the famous Laver tables.

Theorem 7.1 [PROVEN — published theorem (Laver; Dehornoy, Prop. X.1.6); REDISCOVERY]. An anchored left-distributive magma of size NN exists iff NN is a power of 2, and it is then unique.

Our evidence is independent of Laver’s recursion: a complete SAT enumeration finds exactly one model for N=1,2,4,8N = 1, 2, 4, 8 and none for the other N≤10N \le 10 (EXP-C06-02); Laver’s recursion gives left-distributive tables exactly at the powers of two up to 64 (EXP-C06-01). Lean checks the table A3A_3 and the non-existence for N=3N = 3. The general theorem is published (Laver; elementary proof by Drápal; Dehornoy’s book) and we do not re-prove it; we read its statement in Biane’s refereed survey (Sém. Lothar. Combin. 80, 2019, §2.2), whose published version restricts the recursion to q<Nq < N — the correction of the arXiv version that Chapter 19 rediscovers.

7.4 When the anchor pins everything

Theorem 7.2 [PROVEN — Lean: anchored_assoc_unique]. An anchored associative operation is x⋆y=x+y+1x \star y = x + y + 1, for every NN.

Theorem 7.3 [PROVEN — Lean: anchored_rd_unique]. An anchored right-distributive operation, (x⋆y)⋆z=(x⋆z)⋆(y⋆z)(x \star y) \star z = (x \star z) \star (y \star z), is x⋆y=x+1x \star y = x + 1, for every NN.

In both proofs, putting z=0z = 0 moves the anchor along the law and pins the whole table. Left-distributivity instead sends the anchor through other rows, p⋆(q+1)=(p⋆q)⋆(p+1)p \star (q+1) = (p \star q) \star (p+1), and the consistency of that recursion is a genuine arithmetic constraint on NN.

7.5 A bold hypothesis fails

H-C06-2 [REFUTED]. “Restricted spectra are special to left-distributivity: every other law gives either every NN or only N=1N = 1.”

We tested all 382 laws x(yz)=tx(yz) = t and (xy)z=t(xy)z = t whose right side has three or four leaves using exactly x,y,zx, y, z (EXP-C06-03): 77 have models for every N≤6N \le 6, 283 only for N=1N = 1, and 22 have a restricted spectrum — only one of them left-distributivity:

spectrum (N ≤ 8) number of laws example
{1,2}\{1, 2\} 10 x(yz)=((xx)y)zx(yz) = ((xx)y)z
{1,3}\{1, 3\} 7 x(yz)=((zx)x)yx(yz) = ((zx)x)y
{1,5}\{1, 5\} 2 (xy)z=x(y(zz))(xy)z = x(y(zz))
odd NN 2 (xy)z=x((yz)z)(xy)z = x((yz)z)

7.6 The odd law and the golden law

Theorem 7.4 [PROVEN — Lean: odd_law_even_impossible, odd_law_formula, odd_law_model; POTENTIALLY NOVEL (small)]. The law (x⋆y)⋆z=x⋆((y⋆z)⋆z)(x \star y) \star z = x \star ((y \star z) \star z) has an anchored model iff NN is odd, and then exactly one: x⋆y=x+1+y/2x \star y = x + 1 + y/2.

Proof. Put z=0z = 0: (x⋆y)+1=x⋆(y+2)(x \star y) + 1 = x \star (y + 2). Going N/2N/2 times around an even cycle would give x⋆y=x⋆y+N/2x \star y = x \star y + N/2; for odd NN every yy is 2k2k and x⋆y=x+1+kx \star y = x + 1 + k. ∎

Theorem 7.5 [PROVEN — written, Skeptic-reviewed; Lean (Chapter 8): golden_law_models; POTENTIALLY NOVEL (small)]. The anchored models of (x⋆y)⋆z=x⋆(y⋆(z⋆z))(x \star y) \star z = x \star (y \star (z \star z)) are exactly the tables x⋆y=x+1+ayx \star y = x + 1 + a y with a2+a≡1(modN).a^2 + a \equiv 1 \pmod N . So the spectrum is the set of moduli in which the golden ratio exists: N=5emN = 5^e m with e≤1e \le 1 and every prime factor of mm congruent to ±1mod5\pm 1 \bmod 5 — 1,5,11,19,29,31,41,55,…1, 5, 11, 19, 29, 31, 41, 55, \ldots (OEIS A089270) — and the number of models is the number of roots (OEIS A087781).

Proof idea. Putting z=0z = 0 gives x⋆(y⋆1)=(x⋆y)+1x \star (y \star 1) = (x \star y) + 1. Iterating σ(y)=y⋆1\sigma(y) = y \star 1 from 0 visits every element (the values 0⋆σk(0)=1+k0 \star \sigma^k(0) = 1 + k are distinct), so the table is x⋆y=x+1+κ(y)x \star y = x + 1 + \kappa(y) for a bijection κ\kappa; the law then forces κ(y+c)=κ(y)+1\kappa(y + c) = \kappa(y) + 1 with cc a unit, so κ\kappa is linear, κ(y)=ay\kappa(y) = a y, and the law reduces to a2+a=1a^2 + a = 1. Quadratic reciprocity and Hensel’s lemma count the roots. ∎

The predictions were tested before they were proved: the SAT solver finds exactly 1, 1 and 2 models at N=1,5,11N = 1, 5, 11 and none at the other N≤12N \le 12 (EXP-C06-04).

A second hypothesis fails. H-C06-3 guessed that every spectrum other than Laver’s is explained by affine tables. Six laws refute it — e.g. x(yz)=y(x(yz))x(yz) = y(x(yz)) has models for every NN, none of them affine — but for 20 of the 22 restricted laws every model found (N≤9N \le 9) is affine.

7.7 Two counting theorems

Theorem 7.6 [PROVEN — written]. The anchored magmas satisfying (xy)z=(xz)y(xy)z = (xz)y are exactly x⋆y=x+c(y)x \star y = x + c(y) with c(0)=1c(0) = 1; there are NN−1N^{N-1} of them. (The law says that right translations commute; they all commute with the successor, an NN-cycle, whose centraliser consists of its powers.)

Theorem 7.7 [PROVEN — Lean core: centralizer_eq, comp_eq, id_eq, leftPerm_assoc, leftPerm_comm; REDISCOVERY in substance — a two-line lemma on left-permutable groupoids with a right identity]. Anchored magmas satisfying x(yz)=y(xz)x(yz) = y(xz) correspond bijectively to commutative monoid structures on ℤ/N\mathbb{Z}/N with identity 0, via a⋅b=(a−1)⋆ba \cdot b = (a-1) \star b. Their numbers 1,2,9,94,1486,…1, 2, 9, 94, 1486, \ldots are OEIS A058156.

Proof idea. The maps fm=Lm−1f_m = L_{m-1} commute and satisfy fm(0)=mf_m(0) = m. Any map gg commuting with all of them satisfies g(m)=g(fm(0))=fm(g(0))=fg(0)(m)g(m) = g(f_m(0)) = f_m(g(0)) = f_{g(0)}(m): the family is its own centraliser. So it is closed under composition and contains the identity — it is the regular representation of a commutative monoid. ∎

The OEIS match came first; the explanation followed from asking why a count of magmas should equal a count of monoids.

7.8 Which sets can be spectra?

Theorem 7.8 [PROVEN — written]. If a set of laws has anchored models of coprime sizes NN and MM, it has one of size NMNM.

Proof. Take the direct product of the two models and transport it to ℤ/NM\mathbb{Z}/NM along the Chinese remainder isomorphism, which sends x+1x + 1 to (x+1,x+1)(x + 1, x + 1) and therefore preserves the anchor. ∎

Every spectrum contains N=1N = 1; Theorem 7.8 adds that a spectrum containing two primes contains their product, so it can never be {1}\{1\} together with an infinite set of primes. Every spectrum we have seen is also closed under divisors; whether that is always so is the first question of the next cycle.

7.9 Experiments

Experiment Question Result
EXP-C06-01 Laver’s recursion LD exactly at powers of 2 (N ≤ 64); periods A098820
EXP-C06-02 model counts for 12 laws LD: 1 at 1, 2, 4, 8; NN−1N^{N-1}; A058156
EXP-C06-03 spectra of 382 laws 22 restricted; H-C06-2 refuted
EXP-C06-04 affine models golden and odd predictions exact; H-C06-3 refuted

7.10 Comparison with existing mathematics

Theorem 7.1 is Laver’s; the number theory in Theorem 7.5 is classical and the moduli are catalogued in the OEIS. Left-permutable groupoids are studied in the literature on groupoid identities, and the centraliser argument is the regular representation. We found no prior statement of the odd law, the golden law or the left-permutable correspondence for anchored operations; each is small.

7.11 Applications and limitations

No application is claimed. The data cover laws with at most four leaves on the right and sizes up to 8–12; spectra of larger laws are unexplored.

7.12 Open questions

  1. Prove that the laws with spectra {1,2}\{1, 2\} and {1,3}\{1, 3\} have no other models. Chapter 8 proves it for 16 of the 17 laws (two of them in Lean); only x(yz)=y((yx)z)x(yz) = y((yx)z) remains.
  2. Which sets of integers are anchored spectra of a single law? They are closed under coprime products (Theorem 7.8); are they closed under divisors?
  3. Are there laws other than left-distributivity with non-affine, Laver-like spectra?

Volume 3 — Emergent Mathematical Structures

Laws imply other laws. This volume studies the structure that emerges among the invented laws themselves: which identities follow from which, and what the resulting order looks like.

8 Which sets of integers are spectra?

Volume 3 — Emergent mathematical structures. Research cycle C13.

8.1 Motivation

Chapter 7 anchored an invented operation to the successor, x⋆0=x+1x \star 0 = x + 1 on ℤ/N\mathbb{Z}/N, and found that single laws cut out arithmetic sets of sizes: the powers of two (Laver), the odd numbers, the moduli in which the golden ratio exists. Every such spectrum is closed under products of coprime sizes (Theorem 7.8). This chapter asks the converse question: which sets of integers are spectra? Two answers emerge. Through the affine models a single law can encode a remarkable range of classical number theory — quadratic, cubic and cyclotomic conditions modulo NN. But spectra in general are wilder than the examples suggested: they need not even be closed under divisors.

8.2 Definitions

An anchored table is affine if x⋆y=x+ay+1x \star y = x + a y + 1 for some a∈ℤ/Na \in \mathbb{Z}/N (under the anchor these are the only tables of the form αx+βy+γ\alpha x + \beta y + \gamma). Evaluating both sides of a law in the affine table turns it into a finite set of polynomials in ℤ[a]\mathbb{Z}[a], its affine system; the affine spectrum Aff(E)\mathrm{Aff}(E) is the set of NN for which the system has a common root modulo NN. Always Aff(E)⊆Spec(E)\mathrm{Aff}(E) \subseteq \mathrm{Spec}(E); a law is affinely rigid if every anchored model is affine, so that the two coincide.

8.3 Affine spectra are arithmetic

Theorem 8.1 [PROVEN — Lean: C13_AffineStructure.lean]. Affine spectra are closed under divisors and under coprime products, so membership of NN depends only on the prime powers dividing NN. If the polynomials of the affine system have no common factor over ℚ\mathbb{Q}, the affine spectrum is the set of divisors of a single integer LL; otherwise it is infinite (by Schur’s theorem).

The laboratory computed the affine spectra of all 18,581 laws with at most four leaves per side over x,y,zx, y, z, and of all 75,625 laws over x,yx, y with at most five leaves per side (EXP-C13-01). They fall into 27 and 125 distinct spectra. Every finite one is a divisor set Div(L)\mathrm{Div}(L), with LL ranging over 80 values up to 328. The infinite ones read like a table of contents of elementary number theory:

affine condition spectrum OEIS example law
2a+1=02a + 1 = 0 odd numbers A005408 x=x((xx)x)x = x((xx)x)
3a+1=03a + 1 = 0 3∤N3 \nmid N A001651 ((xx)x)x=((yy)y)y((xx)x)x = ((yy)y)y
a2+1=0a^2 + 1 = 0 −1-1 is a square mod NN A008784 x(xx)=y(xy)x(xx) = y(xy)
a2−2a−1=0a^2 - 2a - 1 = 0 22 is a square mod NN A057126 (xx)(xy)=(yy)(yx)(xx)(xy) = (yy)(yx)

Of the 45 infinite affine spectra found (one of them all of ℕ), 29 have no match in the OEIS at all; most come from cubic and quartic conditions — the plastic number (a3−a−1a^3 - a - 1), the tribonacci and tetranacci polynomials, the fifth cyclotomic polynomial. Spot checks against the direct definitions up to N=1000N = 1000 (for example “−1-1 is a square modulo NN”) found no discrepancy (EXP-C13-09).

8.4 …but most laws are not rigid

H-C13-2 [REFUTED]. “Every law with an infinite, non-trivial affine spectrum is affinely rigid.” The laboratory blocked all affine tables and asked a SAT solver for any other model (EXP-C13-02). Of the 147 laws tested from infinite classes — the three shortest of every class — 146 have non-affine models, all of them at every size from 6 to 8. (The first run reported 198 of 199: a faulty filter had also admitted 52 laws from finite classes. The Skeptic found it, and the experiment was corrected and re-run.) The shortest laws in each class are weak (for example x=x(x(xx))x = x(x(xx)) only constrains each element’s own diagonal), and weak laws have many models: the affine spectrum of x=x(x(xx))x = x(x(xx)) is the Eisenstein set A034017, but its true spectrum turns out to be every NN except 2 (Theorem 8.6 below).

So affine spectra are lower bounds. To realise an arithmetic set as a true spectrum, a law must be shown to be rigid, and the laboratory found a large family where this can be proved.

Theorem 8.2 [PROVEN — Lean: right_normed_translation]. For any term V(y,z)V(y, z), every anchored model of (x⋆y)⋆z=x⋆V(y,z)(x \star y) \star z = x \star V(y, z) is a translation table x⋆y=x+1+κ(y)x \star y = x + 1 + \kappa(y), with κ\kappa a permutation fixing 0.

Proof idea. Putting z=0z = 0 gives x⋆σ(y)=(x⋆y)+1x \star \sigma(y) = (x \star y) + 1 with σ(y)=V(y,0)\sigma(y) = V(y, 0). Iterating σ\sigma from 0 therefore adds one each time, so it visits every element exactly once, and κ\kappa counts the steps. ∎

Theorem 8.3 (the W-family) [PROVEN — Lean: w_family_models, w_family_model_of_root]. For every term W(z)W(z) in one variable, the anchored models of (x⋆y)⋆z=x⋆(y⋆W(z))(x \star y) \star z = x \star (y \star W(z)) are exactly the affine tables with a⋅cW(a)≡1(modN)a \cdot c_W(a) \equiv 1 \pmod N, where cW(a)=∑leavesa(right depth)c_W(a) = \sum_{\text{leaves}} a^{\text{(right depth)}}. The polynomials cWc_W that occur are exactly the polynomials with non-negative integer coefficients, constant term 1 and no gaps [PROVEN — written].

For the right comb W=z(z(⋯z))W = z(z(\cdots z)) with nn leaves the condition is that the nn-bonacci polynomial tn−tn−1−⋯−1t^n - t^{n-1} - \cdots - 1 has a root modulo NN: the golden law of Chapter 7 is n=2n = 2, and n=3n = 3 gives a “tribonacci law” whose spectrum 1,2,7,11,13,14,17,…1, 2, 7, 11, 13, 14, 17, \ldots is not in the OEIS. For the left comb with k+1k+1 leaves the condition is ka2+a−1≡0k a^2 + a - 1 \equiv 0, of discriminant 1+4k1 + 4k. The theorem was tested before it was proved: for all 23 terms with at most five leaves and all N≤9N \le 9, the number of models equals the number of roots (EXP-C13-03).

8.5 Finite spectra, proved by machine

Chapter 7 conjectured that seventeen laws have spectrum exactly {1,3}\{1, 3\} or {1,2}\{1, 2\} (CLM-C06-10). All seventeen agree with the conjecture for every N≤12N \le 12 (EXP-C13-04).

Theorem 8.4 [PROVEN — Lean: law13_size_dvd_three, law12_size_dvd_two]. The laws x(yz)=((zx)x)yx(yz) = ((zx)x)y and x(yz)=((xy)z)xx(yz) = ((xy)z)x have spectra exactly {1,3}\{1, 3\} and {1,2}\{1, 2\}.

Proof idea for the first. With x=0x = 0 the law says 0⋆(y⋆z)=(z+2)⋆y0 \star (y \star z) = (z + 2) \star y, and with y=0y = 0 as well, 0⋆(0⋆z)=z+30 \star (0 \star z) = z + 3. Together: w⋆y=(w−2)⋆(y−2)+3w \star y = (w - 2) \star (y - 2) + 3. Walking N/2N/2 steps around an even cycle gives N/2≡0N/2 \equiv 0, impossible; for odd NN the walk shows that the table is affine with a=1/2a = 1/2, and then the law forces 3≡03 \equiv 0. ∎

Such arguments have a common shape — instances of the law at small elements, the anchor, and the fact that the table is a function — and they can be automated.

Theorem 8.5 [PROVEN — written; computer-assisted applications]. The offset closure procedure of the laboratory, which records equalities of the form “entry = element + integer” and merges them, is sound: whenever it derives N∣gN \mid g, every anchored model has size dividing gg.

Combined with the affine models, it proves the exact spectrum of 16 of the 17 laws of the conjecture in milliseconds each (EXP-C13-08); the seventeenth, x(yz)=y((yx)z)x(yz) = y((yx)z), is the one law with a non-affine model (at N=2N = 2), and it remains supported only up to N=12N = 12. Run over all 18,581 laws with at most four leaves per side, the procedure proves an exact finite spectrum Div(g)\mathrm{Div}(g) for 6,912 of them (6,045 have only the trivial model). Its trust base is the closure code; checked against the SAT solver on 1,245 sizes of 235 laws, it was never contradicted.

8.6 A bold hypothesis fails: spectra are not closed under divisors

Every spectrum seen in Chapter 7 was closed under divisors, and so are all affine spectra (Theorem 8.1). H-C13-1 conjectured that this always holds.

Theorem 8.6 [PROVEN — Lean: cube_law_no_two, cubeOp_model]. The one-variable law x=x⋆(x⋆(x⋆x))x = x \star (x \star (x \star x)) has anchored models of every size except 2. In particular it has a model of size 4 but none of size 2.

Proof. At N=2N = 2 the anchor forces 0⋆0=10 \star 0 = 1, and neither choice of 0⋆10 \star 1 returns 0 after three steps. For N≥3N \ge 3 let 0⋆1=20 \star 1 = 2, 0⋆2=00 \star 2 = 0 and x⋆x=xx \star x = x for x≠0x \ne 0. ∎

Divisor-closure fails often. Among all 253 one-variable laws with at most five leaves per side, 24 violate it for N≤12N \le 12, with spectra missing exactly {2}\{2\}, {3}\{3\} or {2,3}\{2, 3\} (EXP-C13-07). In random samples the proportion is higher: 81 of 300 two-variable laws (sizes up to 9), 29 of 150 three-variable laws and 17 of 80 pairs of laws (sizes up to 8) violate it (EXP-C13-05, EXP-C13-06). The law ((xy)x)y=(y(xx))x((xy)x)y = (y(xx))x, for instance, has models of sizes 6 and 8 but none of sizes 2, 3, 4 or 5. The same survey refutes a second guess, that one-variable laws have either only the trivial model or models of all large sizes: x=(xx)xx = (xx)x has spectrum exactly {1,2}\{1, 2\}.

What is true is the weaker structure: spectra are closed under coprime products (Theorem 7.8), and divisor-closed whenever the law is affinely rigid.

8.7 Experiments

Experiment Question Result
EXP-C13-01 affine spectra of 94,206 laws 27 + 125 spectra; finite ones are Div(L)\mathrm{Div}(L); classical sets
EXP-C13-02 are infinite affine classes rigid? 146 of 147 laws from infinite classes have non-affine models (H-C13-2 refuted)
EXP-C13-03 the W-family 23 terms, N≤9N \le 9: model counts = root counts
