8.8 Comparison with existing mathematics

The arithmetic sets of the atlas are classical: they are the sets of moduli where a polynomial has a root, or equivalently (for quadratics) the numbers primitively represented by a binary quadratic form, and their OEIS entries say so. What we did not find in the literature is their appearance as spectra of single magma laws with a successor anchor, nor the W-family theorem. The reduction of identities to polynomial conditions on the coefficients of affine (linear) models is the classical method of linear representations of quasigroups and medial magmas; here the anchor removes all freedom except one parameter. Laws whose finite models are counted or constrained by sizes are studied in universal algebra as spectra of varieties; our anchored spectra are a variant with a distinguished successor, and we make no claim about the general spectrum problem.

8.9 Applications and limitations

No application is claimed. The atlas covers laws with at most four leaves (three variables) or five leaves (two variables); the rigidity and divisor-closure searches cover sizes up to 8–12 only; the offset-closure prover is sound but incomplete.

8.10 Open questions

  1. Prove the spectrum {1,2}\{1, 2\} of x(yz)=y((yx)z)x(yz) = y((yx)z), the last case of CLM-C06-10.
  2. Which arithmetic sets are true spectra of rigid laws? Can “−1-1 is a square modulo NN” be realised by a rigid law?
  3. Is every spectrum of a one-variable law finite or cofinite?
  4. Are there laws besides left-distributivity whose spectrum is infinite but of density zero and comes from non-affine models?

Volume 4 — Alternative Representations of Quantities

Once numbers exist, they can be written in many ways. This volume studies operations that live on representations — multiplying digit positions (Knuth’s circle product and its relatives), the arithmetic of the resulting monoids, the cost of counting in different numeration systems, and integer codes.

9 Multiplying digit positions

Volume 4 — Alternative representations of quantities. Research cycle C08.

9.1 Motivation

In base 10, multiplying 10i10^i by 10j10^j gives 10i+j10^{i+j}: digit positions add. What happens if we define a multiplication by adding digit positions in a numeration system where that is not automatically consistent? Let GG be an increasing base sequence (G0=1G_0 = 1), let n=∑idi(n)Gin = \sum_i d_i(n) G_i be the greedy expansion, and set a∘sb=∑i,jdi(a)dj(b)Gi+j+s.a \circ_s b = \sum_{i,j} d_i(a)\, d_j(b)\, G_{i+j+s}. For Fibonacci weights G=1,2,3,5,8,…G = 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, \ldots and s=2s = 2 this is Knuth’s circle multiplication (1988), famous for being associative for no visible reason. Our implementation reproduces its OEIS table A101330 (EXP-C08-05).

9.2 The associativity atlas

We tested 46 base sequences × shifts s=0..5s = 0..5 exhaustively on [1,22]3[1,22]^3, then every candidate on random triples up to 10610^6 (EXP-C08-01..03). The pattern is governed by the roots of the sequence’s minimal recurrence:

class of GG sequences associative shifts
geometric (Gk=bkG_k = b^k) 5 all ss
Pisot (other roots |β|<1|\beta| < 1) 31 all ss beyond a threshold (most cases; see the correction below)
non-Pisot (some |β|≥1|\beta| \ge 1) 10 none (s≤8s \le 8)

Thresholds grow as |β|→1|\beta| \to 1: Pell (|β|=0.41|\beta| = 0.41) from s=1s = 1, Fibonacci (0.62) from 2, tribonacci (0.74) not before 4, 4-bonacci not before 8, Gk+1=Gk+Gk−2G_{k+1} = G_k + G_{k-2} (0.83) not before 11, Gk+1=2Gk+2Gk−1G_{k+1} = 2G_k + 2G_{k-1} not before 7 and Gk+1=3Gk+3Gk−1G_{k+1} = 3G_k + 3G_{k-1} (with G1=5G_1 = 5) not before 12; Gk+1=Gk+Gk−3G_{k+1} = G_k + G_{k-3} (|β|≈0.94|\beta| \approx 0.94) fails at every shift tested (s≤16s \le 16 in EXP-C08-07, s≤24s \le 24 in the Skeptic’s search).

Correction (2026-10-18). The first version of this chapter gave the thresholds 3 (tribonacci), 7 (4-bonacci), 10 (Gk+Gk−2G_k + G_{k-2}) and 5 (Gk+1=2Gk+2Gk−1G_{k+1} = 2G_k + 2G_{k-1}). All four are wrong. The laboratory’s Historian found the tribonacci counterexample in the literature — (10016∘310016)∘31=3414681926≠3414681924=10016∘3(10016∘31)(10016 \circ_3 10016) \circ_3 1 = 3414681926 \ne 3414681924 = 10016 \circ_3 (10016 \circ_3 1), the example by which Ambrož, Frougny, Masáková and Pelantová (2003) refuted a conjecture of Arnoux — and one more by computation. A certificate-producing re-test (EXP-C08-06) found the other two. The original tests sampled random triples up to 10610^6, and such sampling misses rare failures. Every failure quoted above is now certified by an explicit triple; every “from ss” is a search result, proved only where the literature proves it (Fibonacci: Lean; Pell: Messaoudi 2000; tribonacci s≥6s \ge 6 and 4-bonacci s≥10s \ge 10: Messaoudi).

Second correction (2026-10-18). The re-test was still not good enough. The Skeptic’s review found certified failures at shifts the re-test had passed: Gk+1=2Gk+2Gk−1G_{k+1} = 2G_k + 2G_{k-1} fails at s=6s = 6 and Gk+1=3Gk+3Gk−1G_{k+1} = 3G_k + 3G_{k-1} (G1=5G_1 = 5) at s=10s = 10 and 1111, e.g. (173938∘173938)∘1≠173938∘(173938∘1)(173938 \circ 173938) \circ 1 \ne 173938 \circ (173938 \circ 1). Both the random triples and the small pairs of the re-test avoid the inputs where failures live: numbers whose conjugate coordinates are extreme, so that the conjugate of a product leaves the window. A third search aimed there (EXP-C08-07) reproduces every failure the Skeptic found; the values in the paragraph above are its results, with the same caveat: lower bounds are certified, “not before” is all we can say.

9.3 Why: a twisted embedding into an algebraic number field

Theorem 9.1 [PROVEN — Lean: circ_assoc, circ_comm, key, window; REDISCOVERY (Knuth, Arnoux)]. For every t≥0t \ge 0 the shifted Knuth product a∘tb=∑k∈Z(a),l∈Z(b)Fk+l+ta \circ_t b = \sum_{k \in Z(a),\, l \in Z(b)} F_{k+l+t} (Zeckendorf indices ≥2\ge 2) is associative and commutative. For the two smaller shifts the product is not associative.

Proof. Put E(n)=∑k∈Z(n)φkE(n) = \sum_{k \in Z(n)} \varphi^k and E′(n)=∑k∈Z(n)ψkE'(n) = \sum_{k\in Z(n)} \psi^k (ψ=−1/φ\psi = -1/\varphi). Since φk=Fkφ+Fk−1\varphi^k = F_k\varphi + F_{k-1}, E(n)=nφ+n*E(n) = n\varphi + n^* and E′(n)=nψ+n*E'(n) = n\psi + n^* for an integer n*n^*. The conjugates of Zeckendorf sums lie in a window of length 1: |E′(n)|<−ψ|E'(n)| < -\psi (the Lean proof) or, sharper, −ψ2<E′(n)<−ψ-\psi^2 < E'(n) < -\psi. For a product, φtE(a)E(b)=Pφ+Q\varphi^t E(a)E(b) = P\varphi + Q and ψtE′(a)E′(b)=Pψ+Q\psi^t E'(a)E'(b) = P\psi + Q with P=a∘tbP = a\circ_t b; the second number has absolute value <ψ2< \psi^2, so |Q−P*|<ψ2−ψ=1|Q - P^*| < \psi^2 - \psi = 1, forcing Q=P*Q = P^*. Hence E(a∘tb)=φtE(a)E(b)E(a \circ_t b) = \varphi^t E(a)E(b) — the product becomes multiplication in ℤ[φ]\mathbb{Z}[\varphi] twisted by φt\varphi^t — and associativity follows because nn is recovered from (E(n),E′(n))(E(n), E'(n)). ∎

The same picture explains the atlas. The conjugate coordinates of a product are multiplied by βs\beta^s; if |β|<1|\beta| < 1 large shifts push them into the window of greedy expansions, if |β|≥1|\beta| \ge 1 nothing can.

9.4 A surprise: non-monotone thresholds

For G=1,3,4,7,11,…G = 1, 3, 4, 7, 11, \ldots (a Lucas-type sequence) no failure is found for s=2s = 2, s=3s = 3 fails (certified), and no failure is found again for s≥4s \ge 4 — even under the targeted search of EXP-C08-07. Our explanation: βs\beta^s alternates in sign when β<0\beta < 0, and the window is asymmetric about 0 (for Fibonacci (−0.382,0.618)(-0.382, 0.618)), so an odd shift can push a product’s conjugate out.

Conjecture 9.2 [COMPUTATIONALLY SUPPORTED; POTENTIALLY NOVEL (low)]. For quadratic Pisot sequences with positive conjugate root the associative shifts form an upward-closed set; non-monotone sets occur only for negative conjugates — and, as the Historian pointed out, only for non-canonical initial values: for canonical sequences Messaoudi’s criterion implies upward-closure whatever the sign of β\beta. Evidence (after the targeted search EXP-C08-07, shifts 0…120 \ldots 12): 0 of 30 positive-β\beta sequences non-monotone; 8 non-monotone sequences, all non-canonical with β<0\beta < 0. Four of the original ten examples were artefacts of random testing. The Skeptic’s independent search agrees: 0 of 231 positive-β\beta sequences non-monotone (s≤16s \le 16), 60 of 396 negative-β\beta ones, none canonical.

Conjecture 9.3 [COMPUTATIONALLY SUPPORTED]. For non-Pisot sequences whose dominant root exceeds 1 the circle product is non-associative at every shift. (The first version omitted “dominant root exceeds 1”; the Skeptic noted that Gk=k+1G_k = k + 1, with minimal polynomial (x−1)2(x - 1)^2, gives a∘sb=a+b+s−1a \circ_s b = a + b + s - 1, associative at every shift.)

9.5 Comparison with existing mathematics

Knuth (1988) proved associativity for Fibonacci; Arnoux (1989) found the ℤ[φ]\mathbb{Z}[\varphi] embedding; Grabner, Pethő, Tichy and Woeginger (1994) proved eventual associativity for recurrences with non-increasing coefficients and canonical initial values — our Pisot observations are a rediscovery of their theorem in that range (the first version of this chapter overstated it as “all Pisot sequences”). A. Messaoudi (Math. Slovaca 2000) characterises associativity by the stability of the conjugate window — exactly our mechanism — and proves the Fibonacci case for all s≥2s \ge 2; Ambrož, Frougny, Masáková and Pelantová (2003) bound the fractional digits of products of tribonacci integers. We did not find the non-Pisot statement or the sign phenomenon.

9.6 Open questions

  1. Prove Conjectures 9.2 and 9.3 by computing the greedy windows (Rauzy-type fractals for higher degree).
  2. Determine the exact threshold s0(G)s_0(G) from the geometry of the window.

10 Primes of an invented multiplication

Volume 4 — Alternative representations. Research cycle C09.

10.1 Motivation

Chapter 9 produced a new associative, commutative operation on the positive integers. Every such operation has an arithmetic: which numbers are irreducible (not a∘ba \circ b), how many are there, and is factorisation unique?

10.2 Exact formulas

From the embedding ν(n)=nφ+n*\nu(n) = n\varphi + n^* with n*=⌊(n+1)/φ⌋n^* = \lfloor (n+1)/\varphi \rfloor: a∘b=ab+ab*+a*b,(a∘b)*=ab+a*b*,a \circ b = ab + a b^* + a^* b,\qquad (a\circ b)^* = ab + a^* b^*, validated against the digit definition on 89,401 pairs.

10.3 Structure

Theorem 10.1 [PROVEN — written, resting on Theorem 9.1]. (ℕ≥1,∘)(\mathbb{N}_{\ge 1}, \circ) is commutative and cancellative, has no identity, and embeds into the multiplicative monoid of ℤ[φ]\mathbb{Z}[\varphi]. The Fibonacci numbers are exactly the elements mapped to units (ν(Fk)=φk\nu(F_k) = \varphi^k), and Fj∘Fk=Fj+kF_j \circ F_k = F_{j+k}. The norm N(n)=n*2+nn*−n2N(n) = n^{*2} + n n^* - n^2 is multiplicative.

Refuted hypothesis. Factorisation into irreducibles is not unique: 8=1∘1∘1=2∘28 = 1 \circ 1 \circ 1 = 2 \circ 2 (EXP-C09-02). The reason is pretty: 1 and 2 are F2F_2 and F3F_3, so factorisations of FkF_k are factorisations of kk in the numerical semigroup ⟨2,3⟩\langle 2, 3\rangle.

10.4 Circle primes are common

We conjectured (H-C09-1) that circle irreducibles thin out like primes. The data (EXP-C09-01) disagree: up to 10610^6 more than half of all integers are irreducible (density 0.600, 0.577, 0.562, 0.549, 0.539 at 102..10610^2..10^6). A proof explains why.

Theorem 10.2 (window obstruction) [PROVEN — written; POTENTIALLY NOVEL]. If n=a∘bn = a\circ b then the conjugate ν′(n)=ν′(a)ν′(b)\nu'(n) = \nu'(a)\nu'(b) lies in (ψ3,ψ2)(\psi^3, \psi^2), i.e. the fractional part of (n+1)/φ(n+1)/\varphi lies in (0.2360…,0.8541…)(0.2360\ldots, 0.8541\ldots), an interval of length 1/φ1/\varphi. Hence every nn outside it is irreducible, and by Weyl equidistribution these nn have density exactly 1/φ2≈0.3819661/\varphi^2 \approx 0.381966.

Numerically: not a single reducible number up to 10610^6 lies outside the zone, and the forced-irreducible density at 10610^6 is 0.381966 (EXP-C09-04).

Theorem 10.3 [PROVEN — written]. Every factorisation of nn into rr irreducibles satisfies |ν′(n)|<|ψ|r|\nu'(n)| < |\psi|^r; factorisation lengths are bounded.

Conjecture 10.4 [CONJECTURE]. The circle irreducibles have density exactly 1/φ21/\varphi^2: almost every nn in the product zone is reducible. The irreducible fraction inside the zone falls slowly — 0.316, 0.291, 0.270, 0.255 at 103..10610^3..10^6.

10.5 A lesson in reading the OEIS

The list of irreducibles matched OEIS A026273 for 24 terms. Reading the entry term by term showed the sequences diverge at 40 (=4∘4= 4 \circ 4): for n<40n < 40 every product has the form 1∘b1 \circ b, a Beatty-like set related to the Wythoff sequences, which explains the coincidence. The irreducibles of Knuth’s product appear not to be catalogued.

10.6 Open questions

  1. Prove Conjecture 10.4.
  2. Describe the irreducibles through prime factorisation in ℤ[φ]\mathbb{Z}[\varphi] plus a unit-window condition.
  3. The same questions for other associative circle products.

11 The cost of counting

Volume 4 — Alternative representations. Research cycle C10.

11.1 Motivation

The successor n↦n+1n \mapsto n+1 is the most basic operation on quantities. Its cost in a representation — the number of digit positions that change — is a natural measure of how well the representation suits counting, and it matters in practice (counters in data structures and hardware).

Which measure. Throughout this chapter the cost is the Hamming distance between the representations of nn and n+1n+1. Berthé, Frougny, Rigo and Sakarovitch (Adv. Appl. Math., 2020) study the related carry propagation measure systematically; under theirs the Zeckendorf value is φ2\varphi^2, not the 2 we obtain below. The two measures agree for ordinary bases.

11.2 Classical constants

Theorem 11.1 [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY]. The average cost per increment is b/(b−1)b/(b-1) in base bb (2 in binary, 10/910/9 in decimal), the same in negabinary and balanced ternary, e−1≈1.71828e - 1 \approx 1.71828 in the factorial base, and exactly 1 for the Gray code. (Digit kk changes with frequency b−kb^{-k}, respectively 1/k!1/k!.)

Measured over n<106n < 10^6 (EXP-C10-01): binary 1.999988, factorial 1.7182767, Gray 1.0, skew binary 1.4999955 with worst case 2. For skew binary both numbers are proved in Lean (C10_SkewBinary.lean): every increment changes at most two digits, and the average over the first NN increments is within (⌊log2(N+1)⌋+1)/(2N)(\lfloor \log_2(N+1) \rfloor + 1)/(2N) of 3/23/2.

11.3 Zeckendorf counts like binary

The Inventor guessed that the sparse Zeckendorf system (no two adjacent ones) would be cheaper to count in than binary. It is not.

Theorem 11.2 [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY in substance: OEIS A215005]. Let TmT_m be the total cost of counting from 0 to Gm−1G_m - 1 in the Zeckendorf system (G=1,2,3,5,…G = 1, 2, 3, 5, \ldots). Then Tm+1=Tm+Tm−1+⌈m/2⌉+1,Tm=2Gm−⌈m/2⌉−2,T_{m+1} = T_m + T_{m-1} + \lceil m/2\rceil + 1,\qquad T_m = 2G_m - \lceil m/2\rceil - 2, so the average cost tends to exactly 2, the same as binary.

Proof idea. The numbers below Gm+1G_{m+1} split into [0,Gm)[0, G_m) and a block of Gm−1G_{m-1} numbers beginning “10”; the step from Gm−1=1010⋯G_m - 1 = 1010\cdots to Gm=10⋯0G_m = 10\cdots0 costs ⌈m/2⌉+1\lceil m/2 \rceil + 1. ∎

The sequence TmT_m is OEIS A215005; the per-increment cost sequence is not catalogued.

11.4 Exchange systems

For the exchange numerations of Chapter 3 two clean laws hold.

Theorem 11.3 [PROVEN — written]. A terminating carry rule m[k]→∑bj[k+j]m[k] \to \sum b_j[k+j] fires on average 1/(m−∑bj)1/(m - \sum b_j) times per increment (the abelian property makes one-at-a-time counting fire exactly as often as stabilising nn tokens at once).

Theorem 11.4 [PROVEN — written]. If only one bg=qb_g = q is nonzero, every increment changes exactly (firings +1+ 1) digits, so the average cost is 1+1/(m−q)1 + 1/(m - q): 2 for base 3/23/2, 3/23/2 for base 3.

With two or more nonzero bjb_j a level can receive several tokens in one increment, and the cost carries a rule-specific excess — 0.500 for 3[k]→[k+1]+[k+2]3[k] \to [k+1] + [k+2], 0.333 for 4[k]→[k+1]+[k+2]4[k] \to [k+1] + [k+2], 0.831 for 4[k]→[k+1]+[k+2]+[k+3]4[k] \to [k+1]+[k+2]+[k+3] (EXP-C10-03). Conjecture: these are expectations under the invariant measure of the digit process; we have no formula.

11.5 Application note

As counters, none of the invented exchange representations beats the classical choices: Gray code (cost 1 at every step) or skew binary (average 1.5, worst case 2, used in purely functional random-access lists). We record this as a negative result.

12 Codes from forbidden patterns

Volume 4 — Alternative representations of quantities. Research cycle C11.

12.1 Motivation

A universal code writes every positive integer as a self-delimiting bit string, so that a stream of integers of unknown size can be decoded without separators. The Fibonacci code writes the Zeckendorf digits of nn (weights 1,2,3,5,…1, 2, 3, 5, \ldots, least significant first) and appends a 1; as Zeckendorf digits never contain 1111, the codeword ends where 1111 first appears. The code is defined by a forbidden pattern, and 1111 is one choice among many. Replacing it by an arbitrary terminator pp, we ask which terminator is best, and whether any of the resulting codes is useful against honest baselines, including an improved Fibonacci code from the literature and Golomb–Rice codes tuned to the data. The counting is governed by the autocorrelation polynomial of Chapter 2.

12.2 Definitions

Let pp be a binary pattern of length kk. The code TpT_p has as codewords the binary words in which pp occurs for the first time at the very end, assigned to n=1,2,3,…n = 1, 2, 3, \ldots in order of length and then lexicographically (enumerative coding with a Knuth–Morris–Pratt automaton): T11={11,011,0011,1011,…},T011={011,0011,1011,00011,01011,10011,11011,…}.T_{11} = \{11,\ 011,\ 0011,\ 1011,\ \ldots\}, \qquad T_{011} = \{011,\ 0011,\ 1011,\ 00011,\ 01011,\ 10011,\ 11011,\ \ldots\}. fp(L)f_p(L) is the number of codewords of length LL, cump(L)=∑l≤Lfp(l)\mathrm{cum}_p(L) = \sum_{l \le L} f_p(l), and the codeword of nn has length Lp(n)=min{L:cump(L)≥n}L_p(n) = \min\{L : \mathrm{cum}_p(L) \ge n\}. As in Chapter 2, cp(x)=1+∑bxk−|b|c_p(x) = 1 + \sum_b x^{k - |b|}, summed over the borders bb of pp, is the autocorrelation polynomial; pp is unbordered if cp=1c_p = 1. The growth rate is λp=1/ρp\lambda_p = 1/\rho_p, with ρp\rho_p the smallest positive root of Dp(x)=xk+(1−2x)cp(x),D_p(x) = x^k + (1 - 2x)\,c_p(x), and Lp(n)/log2n→1/log2λpL_p(n)/\log_2 n \to 1/\log_2 \lambda_p. The redundancy of a code on a source is its expected codeword length minus the entropy.

12.3 The pattern-terminated family

Theorem 12.1 [PROVEN — Lean: prefix_free, unique_parse_head; REDISCOVERY]. For every pattern pp of length kk: (a) TpT_p is a prefix code; (b) its Kraft sum ∑Lfp(L)2−L\sum_L f_p(L)\,2^{-L} equals 1; (c) its counts have the generating function Fp(x)=∑Lfp(L)xL=xkxk+(1−2x)cp(x);F_p(x) = \sum_L f_p(L)\,x^L = \frac{x^k}{x^k + (1 - 2x)\,c_p(x)}; (d) T11T_{11} has exactly the codewords of the Fibonacci code, and T1kT_{1^k} those of its order-kk generalisation.

Proof idea. (a) A codeword that is a proper prefix of another would put pp before the other’s end (Lean also checks unique parsing). (b) The sum is the probability that a random infinite bit sequence contains pp. (c) If A(x)A(x) counts the words avoiding pp, appending a letter gives 1+2xA=A+Fp1 + 2xA = A + F_p, and appending pp (whose first occurrence then ends after a period of pp) gives xkA=cpFpx^k A = c_p F_p. (d) A word whose first 1111 is at its end is a Zeckendorf digit string followed by the extra 1. ∎ By (c), TpT_p depends on pp only through kk and cpc_p. The 61 implemented pattern codes passed round trips, a prefix check and Kraft partial sums ≥0.999999999993\ge 0.999999999993 (EXP-C11-01).

Theorem 12.2 [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY: Guibas–Odlyzko correlation ordering]. Among the binary patterns of length kk, 1k1^k and 0k0^k have the largest growth rate, and every other pattern has a strictly smaller one.

Proof idea. Dp(1/2)=2−kD_p(1/2) = 2^{-k} for every pp. Since 1k1^k has every period, c1kc_{1^k} dominates cpc_p coefficientwise, and for x>1/2x > 1/2 D1k(x)−Dp(x)=(1−2x)(c1k(x)−cp(x))≤0,D_{1^k}(x) - D_p(x) = (1 - 2x)\bigl(c_{1^k}(x) - c_p(x)\bigr) \le 0, strictly unless cp=c1kc_p = c_{1^k}. So D1kD_{1^k} vanishes no later than DpD_p. ∎ All 2044 patterns of length 2 to 10 agree (EXP-C11-02); for example λ11=1.618\lambda_{11} = 1.618, λ101=1.755\lambda_{101} = 1.755, λ111=1.839\lambda_{111} = 1.839, λ0101=1.883\lambda_{0101} = 1.883 and λ1111=1.928\lambda_{1111} = 1.928.

The bold hypothesis fails. H-C11-2 [REFUTED]: “hence 1k1^k is the best length-kk terminator beyond small nn.” At n=1000n = 1000 the terminators 10001000, 10011001, 10101010 give codewords one bit shorter than 11111111. Among the sampled n≥1000n \ge 1000 (all n≤2048n \le 2048 and quarter-powers of 2 up to 2642^{64}), rivals beat 1k1^k at 762, 972 and 1127 values for k=4,5,6k = 4, 5, 6, the last as late as n≈1.3⋅1019n \approx 1.3 \cdot 10^{19} (EXP-C11-02). Theorem 12.2 is about the limit; at practical sizes the constants decide.

12.4 Unbordered terminators

Theorem 12.3 [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY]. If uu is unbordered and |u|=k+1|u| = k + 1, then Fu(x)=x1−xF1k(x),i.e.fu(L)=cum1k(L−1).F_u(x) = \frac{x}{1 - x}\,F_{1^k}(x), \qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad f_u(L) = \mathrm{cum}_{1^k}(L - 1). Hence λu=λ1k\lambda_u = \lambda_{1^k} is the kk-bonacci constant λ\lambda, and cumu(L)/cum1k(L)→1/(λ−1)\mathrm{cum}_u(L)/\mathrm{cum}_{1^k}(L) \to 1/(\lambda - 1).

Proof. c1k=(1−xk)/(1−x)c_{1^k} = (1 - x^k)/(1 - x) gives F1k=xk(1−x)/(1−2x+xk+1)F_{1^k} = x^k(1 - x)/(1 - 2x + x^{k+1}), and cu=1c_u = 1 gives Fu=xk+1/(1−2x+xk+1)F_u = x^{k+1}/(1 - 2x + x^{k+1}). ∎ The identity holds exactly for every unbordered pattern of length 3 to 9 up to length 200, and the count ratios at length 300 are 1.6180339891.618033989, 1.1914878841.191487884, 1.0780950781.078095078 for k=2,3,4k = 2, 3, 4 (EXP-C11-02, EXP-C11-06).

Corollary 12.4 [PROVEN — written; partly REDISCOVERY: T011T_{011} is Capocelli’s code C(3)]. With F1=F2=1F_1 = F_2 = 1, cum11(L)=FL+1−1\mathrm{cum}_{11}(L) = F_{L+1} - 1 and cum011(L)=FL+2−L−1\mathrm{cum}_{011}(L) = F_{L+2} - L - 1. If the Fibonacci codeword of nn has length L+1L + 1, the T011T_{011} codeword is one bit shorter when n≤FL+2−L−1n \le F_{L+2} - L - 1 and equally long for the last LL values of the level; T011T_{011} is longer than the Fibonacci code only for n∈{1,2,4}n \in \{1, 2, 4\}. The same holds for every unbordered 3-letter terminator.

Proof idea. Theorem 12.3 with k=2k = 2 gives f011(l)=Fl−1f_{011}(l) = F_l - 1; compare the cumulative counts level by level. ∎ For n≤2⋅106n \le 2 \cdot 10^6 the T011T_{011} codeword is one bit shorter for 1,999,568 values, equally long for 429 and longer for 3; block-wise checks up to L=90L = 90 find no exception (EXP-C11-06).

Two predictions of the average gain fail [REFUTED]. For k≥3k \ge 3, uu wins only on part of each level. The guess that the average of L1k(n)−Lu(n)L_{1^k}(n) - L_u(n) over n≤Nn \le N tends to logλ(1/(λ−1))\log_\lambda(1/(\lambda - 1)) predicts 0.2875 for k=3k = 3; the average over n≤240n \le 2^{40} is 0.2282 (EXP-C11-06). The corrected guess, convergence to (2−λ)/(λ−1)2=0.228155(2 - \lambda)/(\lambda - 1)^2 = 0.228155, fails too: the running average is 0.228181, 0.309709, 0.228548 at N=240,280,2120N = 2^{40}, 2^{80}, 2^{120} (EXP-C11-07).

Proposition 12.5 [PROVEN — written; POTENTIALLY NOVEL (low confidence)]. Let k≥2k \ge 2, λ\lambda the kk-bonacci constant, uu unbordered of length k+1k + 1 and G(N)=1N∑n≤N(L1k(n)−Lu(n))G(N) = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{n \le N}\bigl(L_{1^k}(n) - L_u(n)\bigr). Then liminfN→∞G(N)=2−λ(λ−1)2,limsupN→∞G(N)=λ(2−λ)λ−1.\liminf_{N\to\infty} G(N) = \frac{2 - \lambda}{(\lambda - 1)^2}, \qquad \limsup_{N\to\infty} G(N) = \frac{\lambda(2 - \lambda)}{\lambda - 1}.

Proof idea. Put r=1/(λ−1)r = 1/(\lambda - 1). For k≥3k \ge 3, on the level of T1kT_{1^k} with codeword length LL, TuT_u is one bit shorter exactly on the first cumu(L−1)−cum1k(L−1)≈(r−1)cum1k(L−1)\mathrm{cum}_u(L-1) - \mathrm{cum}_{1^k}(L-1) \approx (r - 1)\,\mathrm{cum}_{1^k}(L-1) values and equally long on the rest; GG rises along this segment and falls after it, and geometric sums at the two turning points give the constants. For k=2k = 2 use Corollary 12.4. ∎ The pair (liminf, limsup) is (1,1)(1, 1), (0.228155,0.352201)(0.228155, 0.352201), (0.084194,0.150533)(0.084194, 0.150533), (0.036495,0.069304)(0.036495, 0.069304), (0.016970,0.033108)(0.016970, 0.033108) for k=2,…,6k = 2, \ldots, 6; exact averages agree to six digits (EXP-C11-08).

12.5 The improved Fibonacci code C3

Fraenkel and Klein (1996) improved C1 by a code C3: delete the final 1 of each C1 codeword, take each length block twice and prefix the copies with 1010 and 1111 (101,111,1001,1101,10001,…101, 111, 1001, 1101, 10001, \ldots). C3 is uniquely decipherable and complete but not prefix-free: its decoder searches for 011011 and reads one bit of the next codeword.

Corollary 12.6 [PROVEN — written; partly REDISCOVERY: Fraenkel–Klein]. C3 has 2Fr−22F_{r-2} codewords of length r≥3r \ge 3, so cumC3(L)=2FL−2\mathrm{cum}_{C3}(L) = 2F_L - 2. C3 is never longer than C1 except at n=1n = 1, and T011T_{011} is never longer than C3 except at n∈{2,4,8}n \in \{2, 4, 8\}; cum011/cumC3→φ2/2≈1.309\mathrm{cum}_{011}/\mathrm{cum}_{C3} \to \varphi^2/2 \approx 1.309 and cumC3/cumC1→2/φ≈1.236\mathrm{cum}_{C3}/\mathrm{cum}_{C1} \to 2/\varphi \approx 1.236. (The count and the comparison with C1 are Fraenkel and Klein’s; the comparison with T011T_{011} was not found in the sources we read.)

Proof. As FL+2−2FL=FL−1F_{L+2} - 2F_L = F_{L-1}, cum011(L)−cumC3(L)=FL−1−(L−1)\mathrm{cum}_{011}(L) - \mathrm{cum}_{C3}(L) = F_{L-1} - (L - 1), negative only for L=3,4,5L = 3, 4, 5; and cumC3(L)−cumC1(L)=FL−2−1≥0\mathrm{cum}_{C3}(L) - \mathrm{cum}_{C1}(L) = F_{L-2} - 1 \ge 0 for L≥3L \ge 3. ∎ Our C3 (its first 12 codewords match the paper’s list) confirms this for n≤2⋅106n \le 2 \cdot 10^6 (EXP-C11-09). The average of LC1−LC3L_{C1} - L_{C3} over n≤240,280,2120n \le 2^{40}, 2^{80}, 2^{120} is 0.462, 0.400, 0.481: C3 recovers less than half of the bit that T011T_{011} saves.

12.6 Benchmarks

Benchmark result 12.7 [COMPUTATIONALLY SUPPORTED — algorithmic; improvement MIXED; baselines: Elias γ\gamma, δ\delta, ω\omega, Levenshtein, Fibonacci C1, Fraenkel–Klein C3, LEB128, Exp-Golomb(0, 2, 4), and Golomb–Rice and Exp-Golomb codes tuned to each source]. Among untuned codes, a pattern code is best on 5 of 12 synthetic sources and on both posting lists, beating the best classical code there by 0.34–3.06 bits per value; on Zipf 1.1, log-uniform and the bursty posting list it also beats the tuned codes. It loses on light-tailed sources and to tuned codes on geometric and uniform sources, and is 4–10 times slower than the Elias and Exp-Golomb codes.

Expected lengths are exact, from the breakpoints of each length function (no sampling); codes defined on n≥0n \ge 0 are applied to n−1n - 1. Redundancy in bits per value (EXP-C11-03; best entries bold):

geometric 0.5 2.00 Levenshtein 0.19 T011T_{011} 1.63 0.00 0.27

geometric 0.01 8.08 Exp-Golomb(4) 0.70 T011T_{011} 0.93 0.03 0.28
geometric 0.001 11.41 C3 2.51 T0111T_{0111} 1.74 0.03 0.28
uniform 282^8 8 Exp-Golomb(4) 1.75 T011T_{011} 1.93 0.50 1.00
uniform 2162^{16} 16 C3 5.71 T0111T_{0111} 3.30 0.50 1.00
uniform 2322^{32} 32 LEB128 7.50 T00111T_{00111} 4.44 0.50 1.00
log-uniform 10610^6 13.41 C3 1.36 T0111T_{0111} 0.40 3.93 2.00

Bits per value on data (EXP-C11-04; H0H_0 is the zeroth-order entropy; the tuned codes are tuned on the same data, which favours them):

data H0H_0 best fixed classical best pattern code tuned Golomb tuned Exp-Golomb
prime gaps ≤107\le 10^7 4.17 Exp-Golomb(4) 5.72 T011T_{011} 6.14 5.27 5.58
prime gaps, halved 4.17 Exp-Golomb(2) 4.58 T011T_{011} 5.11 4.27 4.58
posting list, geometric gaps 11.36 C3 13.93 T0111T_{0111} 13.15 11.45 11.70
bursty posting list 10.71 C3 11.82 T101T_{101} 11.47 12.80 11.79

Where pattern codes lose. On light-tailed sources (Zipf ≥1.5\ge 1.5, geometric ≥0.01\ge 0.01, prime gaps) γ\gamma, Levenshtein, Fibonacci or Exp-Golomb are better; even C3 beats T011T_{011} on Zipf ≥1.5\ge 1.5, geometric 0.5 and 0.1 and the prime gaps, where its shorter codewords for n=2,4,8n = 2, 4, 8 carry much probability. Tuned codes are within 0.04 bits of the entropy on geometric sources and within 0.5–1 bit on uniform ones; no universal code comes close. Speed: in our string-based CPython implementations (EXP-C11-05), pattern codes take about 3 µs per value, against 0.4–0.7 µs for the Elias codes and 2.1 µs for the Fibonacci code (machine-dependent; no optimised decoder was attempted).

12.7 Comparison with existing mathematics

Most of the theory of TpT_p is classical. The Fibonacci code and its order-kk generalisation are Apostolico and Fraenkel’s and Fraenkel and Klein’s. The generating function of Theorem 12.1 comes from Guibas and Odlyzko’s theory of string overlaps, and Theorem 12.2 is in substance their comparison of patterns through correlations; every count sequence we used is in the OEIS. The closest prior art for T011T_{011} is C3, which already improves C1 by decoding with 011011 but gives up the prefix property. For prefix-synchronised (fixed-length) codes, unbordered markers 1⋯101\cdots10 are optimal at finite lengths (Gilbert; Guibas and Odlyzko), the phenomenon of Theorem 12.3 in another setting; the multi-delimiter codes of Anisimov and Zavadskyi are reported to beat Fibonacci codes. The Historian’s second audit found that T011T_{011} itself is Capocelli’s code C(3) (IEEE Trans. Inform. Theory 35, 1989: the words in which 011 occurs only as a suffix), that the codes TpT_p in general are Lakshmanan’s pattern codes (1981; Fraenkel and Klein 1996, §3), and that Lima and da Rocha (2013) tabulate C(3) against the Fibonacci code, one bit shorter at the tabulated n=2tn = 2^t. The exact comparisons of Corollaries 12.4 and 12.6 were not found (Capocelli’s paper could not be read), so both are recorded as partial rediscoveries; our confidence in the novelty of Proposition 12.5 is low, given the multi-delimiter codes and the generic nature of log-periodic oscillations.

  • P. Elias, “Universal codeword sets and representations of the integers”, IEEE Trans. Inform. Theory 21 (1975) 194–203.
  • A. Apostolico, A. S. Fraenkel, IEEE Trans. Inform. Theory 33 (1987) 238–245, https://docs.lib.purdue.edu/cstech/464.
  • A. S. Fraenkel, S. T. Klein, Discrete Appl. Math. 64 (1996) 31–55 (codes C1, C2, C3), https://u.cs.biu.ac.il/~tomi/Postscripts/robust.pdf.
  • L. J. Guibas, A. M. Odlyzko, J. Combin. Theory Ser. A 30 (1981) (string overlaps); SIAM J. Appl. Math. 35 (1978) 401–418 (prefix-synchronized codes). E. N. Gilbert, IRE Trans. Inform. Theory 6 (1960) 470–477.
  • A. V. Anisimov, I. O. Zavadskyi, IEEE Trans. Inform. Theory 63 (2017) 2885–2895, https://arxiv.org/abs/1508.01360.
  • https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fibonacci_coding; OEIS https://oeis.org/A000071 (T011T_{011}), https://oeis.org/A005314 (T101T_{101}), https://oeis.org/A008937 (T0111T_{0111}), https://oeis.org/A059633 (T1011T_{1011}), https://oeis.org/A112575 (T0101T_{0101}).

12.8 Applications and limitations

T011T_{011} is a drop-in prefix-free replacement for the Fibonacci code that saves one bit on almost every integer (Corollary 12.4). For heavy-tailed streams of unknown scale (gaps in posting lists with bursty terms, magnitudes over many orders), a fixed pattern code such as T0111T_{0111} or T101T_{101} is a strong universal choice; for geometric gaps use Golomb–Rice, for values of known range a tuned Golomb or Exp-Golomb code, for small values γ\gamma, Fibonacci or C3. These are empirical findings on 12 synthetic sources and 4 data sets (two simulated), comparing memoryless codes with zeroth-order entropy, with the tuned codes fitted to the evaluated data. No code is claimed optimal, and the 4–10-fold speed penalty of our implementation is unresolved.

12.9 Open questions

  1. Can table-driven (byte-wise) decoders make pattern codes as fast as optimised Elias and Fibonacci decoders?
  2. Which sets of terminators, as in multi-delimiter codes, suit a source?
  3. How well do unbordered terminators resynchronise after bit errors?
  4. What are the oscillation constants of the gain of T011T_{011} over C3?

Volume 5 — Mathematical Transformations and Relationships

Structures defined by relations rather than magnitudes: games given by their move relations, operations defined by minimal excluded values, and graphs treated as quantities under local products.

13 Games defined by relations

Volume 5 — Mathematical transformations and relationships. Research cycle C12.

13.1 Motivation

System D asks for structures in which only relations matter. In a one-heap game a move from nn goes to a smaller position, and who wins is decided by the move relation alone. In Chapter 5 games came from rewriting rules; here the move relation is an arithmetic relation — divides, is coprime to, is a submask of, differs by a square — and we ask which relations produce structured values. Nineteen games were solved exactly and compared.

13.2 Definitions

The Sprague–Grundy value of a position is g(n)=mex{g(m):m an option of n},g(n) = \operatorname{mex}\{\, g(m) : m \text{ an option of } n \,\}, where mexA\operatorname{mex} A is the least natural number not in AA. Options are smaller, so gg is unique (Lean: IsGrundy.unique), and nn is a P-position (the player to move loses) iff g(n)=0g(n) = 0 (Lean: IsGrundy.zero_pset). In a splitting game a heap nn is replaced by two nonempty heaps a+b=na + b = n with option value g(a)⊕g(b)g(a) \oplus g(b) (XOR). By the Sprague–Grundy theorem a sum of games has the XOR of the values, so a formula for gg solves all sums of heaps.

v2(n)v_2(n) is the exponent of 2 in nn, Ω(n)\Omega(n) the number of prime factors with multiplicity, popcount(n)\operatorname{popcount}(n) the number of 1-bits, and mm in n=2amn = 2^a m (mm odd) the odd part. A sequence is eventually (arithmetic-)periodic if g(n+p)=g(n)g(n + p) = g(n) (resp. g(n)+sg(n) + s, s≠0s \ne 0) for all large nn.

game move from nn relation
G01, G02 n→n−dn \to n - d, d∣nd \mid n, d<nd < n (G02: also d=nd = n) divisibility
G03 n→n−dn \to n - d, d∣nd \mid n, 1<d<n1 < d < n (“deleting divisors”) divisibility
G04 n→dn \to d, d∣nd \mid n, d<nd < n divisibility
G05, G06 n→mn \to m, 1≤m<n1 \le m < n, gcd(m,n)=1\gcd(m, n) = 1 (G06: >1> 1) coprimality
G08–G12 n→n−sn \to n - s, ss a square, Fibonacci number, power of 2, 3 or 4, prime, triangular number additive sets
G13 n→n \to a proper binary submask of nn bitwise
G16–G18 split n=a+bn = a + b with gcd(a,b)=1\gcd(a, b) = 1, a∣ba \mid b, or a≠ba \ne b (Grundy’s game) splitting
G19, G20 n→nmodkn \to n \bmod k (1≤k<n1 \le k < n), n→⌊n/k⌋n \to \lfloor n/k \rfloor (2≤k≤n2 \le k \le n) Euclidean division

13.3 Arithmetic relations give arithmetic values

Theorem 13.1 (CLM-C12-01) [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY]. G01 has g(n)=v2(n)g(n) = v_2(n), G02 has g(n)=v2(n)+1g(n) = v_2(n) + 1 (for n≥1n \ge 1), and G04 has g(n)=Ω(n)g(n) = \Omega(n).

Proof idea. In G01 let k=v2(n)k = v_2(n). The options n−2jn - 2^j (j<kj < k) have valuation jj and realise every smaller value. No option has valuation kk: if v2(d)<kv_2(d) < k then v2(n−d)=v2(d)v_2(n - d) = v_2(d), and if v2(d)=kv_2(d) = k then n−d=2k(a−b)n - d = 2^k(a - b) with a,ba, b odd, so the valuation exceeds kk. G02 adds the option 0, which shifts every value by one; for G04 a chain of divisors realises 0,…,Ω(n)−10, \ldots, \Omega(n) - 1. ∎

Theorem 13.2 (CLM-C12-02) [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY]. In G05, g(1)=0g(1) = 0 and g(n)=jg(n) = j when the smallest prime factor of nn is the jj-th prime; “subtract kk coprime to nn” is the same game, since gcd(n−m,n)=gcd(m,n)\gcd(n - m, n) = \gcd(m, n). In G06, g(2k)=k−1g(2k) = k - 1, g(m)≤(m−1)/2g(m) \le (m - 1)/2 for odd mm, and the P-positions n≥1n \ge 1 are exactly 1 and the primes.

Proof idea. In G05 the primes below the smallest prime factor pjp_j of nn are options with values 1,…,j−11, \ldots, j - 1, and 1 has value 0; an option of value jj would be divisible by pjp_j. In G06 a simultaneous induction proves the even formula and the odd bound: every odd option of 2k2k shares an odd prime qq with 2k2k, so it is at most 2k−32k - 3 and its value is at most k−2k - 2. ∎ We know no closed form for the odd values of G06; we observed g(m)/m<0.24985g(m)/m < 0.24985 for odd m≤5000m \le 5000.

Theorem 13.3 (CLM-C12-05) [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY]. G13 has g(n)=popcount(n)g(n) = \operatorname{popcount}(n); G19 has g(n)=⌊log2((n+1)/3)⌋+1g(n) = \lfloor \log_2((n + 1)/3) \rfloor + 1 for n≥2n \ge 2; G20 has g(n)=⌊log2n⌋g(n) = \lfloor \log_2 n \rfloor for n≥1n \ge 1.

Proof idea. Proper submasks realise every smaller popcount. The remainders nmodkn \bmod k are exactly the rr with 0≤r<n/20 \le r < n/2, so g(n)=mexg([0,⌈n/2⌉))g(n) = \operatorname{mex} g([0, \lceil n/2 \rceil)) and the value vv first appears at 3⋅2v−1−13 \cdot 2^{v-1} - 1. In G20 the quotients ⌊n/2j⌋\lfloor n/2^j \rfloor realise every smaller value and every option is at most n/2n/2. ∎

13.4 Periodic games and rigid splitting

Theorem 13.4 (CLM-C12-06) [PROVEN — Lean: residue_grundy, power_of_two_grundy; general kk written; REDISCOVERY]. (a) If no move is divisible by qq and 1,…,q−11, \ldots, q - 1 are moves, the subtraction game has g(n)=nmodqg(n) = n \bmod q; in particular subtract-a-power-of-2 has g(n)=nmod3g(n) = n \bmod 3. (b) For every k≥2k \ge 2, subtract-a-power-of-kk has the Grundy function of the finite game {1,k}\{1, k\}: nmod2n \bmod 2 for odd kk, and for even kk the pattern 0,1,0,1,…,1,20, 1, 0, 1, \ldots, 1, 2 of period k+1k + 1.

Proof idea. ki≡(−1)i(modk+1)k^i \equiv (-1)^i \pmod{k+1}, so every move changes the residue modulo k+1k + 1 by ±1\pm 1, exactly as the moves 1 and kk do; the mex equations are then checked residue by residue. ∎

Theorem 13.5 (CLM-C12-07) [PROVEN — Lean: split_parity; REDISCOVERY]. In every splitting game in which each heap n≥2n \ge 2 has at least one allowed split, g(n)=1g(n) = 1 for even nn and g(n)=0g(n) = 0 for odd nn: every play lasts exactly n−1n - 1 moves.

So the coprime and dividing splits (G16, G17) are pure parity games; Grundy’s game (G18) escapes only because a heap of size 2 cannot be split.

13.5 Deleting divisors

Theorem 13.6 (CLM-C12-03) [PROVEN — Lean: g03_pset, g03_P_positions; REDISCOVERY: Codeforces 1537D]. The P-positions of G03 are exactly 0, the odd numbers, and the powers 22j+12^{2j+1}.

Proof idea. From odd nn every option n−dn - d is even and divisible by the odd d≥3d \ge 3, hence neither odd nor a power of 2. An even nn with odd part m>1m > 1 moves to the odd number n−mn - m. The only power of 2 among the options of 2a2^a is 2a−12^{a-1}, so the powers of 2 alternate. ∎

The P-positions do not solve sums of heaps; the whole Grundy function does.

Theorem 13.7 (CLM-C12-04) [PROVEN — written + computer-assisted; Skeptic review pending; POTENTIALLY NOVEL]. Let n=2amn = 2^a m with mm odd. Then g(n)=0g(n) = 0 for odd nn, and for a≥1a \ge 1:

odd part mm g(2am)g(2^a m)
m≥17m \ge 17 aa
11 00 for odd aa; a−1a - 1 for even aa, except g(16)=2g(16) = 2
33 2,3,4,3,62, 3, 4, 3, 6 for a=1,…,5a = 1, \ldots, 5; a−2a - 2 for a≥6a \ge 6
55 1,3,4,3,61, 3, 4, 3, 6 for a=1,…,5a = 1, \ldots, 5; a+2a + 2 for a≥6a \ge 6
7,9,11,157, 9, 11, 15 aa for a≤2a \le 2; a+1a + 1 for a≥3a \ge 3
1313 aa for a≤3a \le 3; a+1a + 1 for a≥4a \ge 4

Consequently |g(n)−v2(n)|≤2\lvert g(n) - v_2(n) \rvert \le 2 unless nn is an odd power of 2, and the record values occur at n=5⋅2an = 5 \cdot 2^a for a≥6a \ge 6.

Proof idea. Call FF the tabulated function; we show g=Fg = F by strong induction. Up to 10510^5 the two agree by exact computation. Odd nn have value 0 because the zero set of FF is the P-set of Theorem 13.6. For odd part m≥17m \ge 17 the options n−2jn - 2^j realise 0,…,a−10, \ldots, a - 1; an option with v2<av_2 < a again has odd part ≥17\ge 17, and an option 2a(m−e)2^a(m - e) can only take the value aa when m∈{3,13,15}m \in \{3, 13, 15\}. For odd part m≤15m \le 15 (so a≥13a \ge 13 beyond 10510^5) every option value has the form a+δa + \delta with offsets that depend only on mm and the parity of aa, so the mex equation needs to be checked for one exponent of each parity; the certificate checks all m≤15m \le 15 and 13≤a≤10013 \le a \le 100. ∎ (EXP-C12-06; the written inductive steps have not yet been reviewed by the Skeptic.)

Compare G01, the same game with the move “subtract 1” allowed, where g=v2g = v_2 exactly. Forbidding one move perturbs the Grundy function only on the odd powers of 2 and on numbers whose odd part is at most 15; up to 10510^5 there are 96 such exceptions.

13.6 Sparse move sets

Theorem 13.8 (CLM-C12-08) [PROVEN — written, from the Furstenberg–Sárközy theorem; REDISCOVERY]. For subtract-a-square and subtract-a-triangular-number the P-positions form an infinite set of natural density 0. Hence neither Grundy sequence is eventually periodic or eventually arithmetic-periodic.

Proof idea. Two P-positions never differ by a move. So the P-set of subtract-a-square has no two elements differing by a square and has density 0 (Furstenberg 1977, Sárközy 1978); triangular numbers contain the values m(2m+1)m(2m + 1) of a polynomial vanishing at 0, and the polynomial version (Kamae and Mendès France 1978) applies. A finite P-set would force a tail of ℕ\mathbb{N} into finitely many translates of the move set, which has density 0. An infinite eventually periodic zero set has positive density, and an arithmetic-periodic sequence has only finitely many zeros. ∎

The data agree: from N=1.25⋅104N = 1.25 \cdot 10^4 to 2⋅1052 \cdot 10^5 the P-density falls 0.0538→0.02220.0538 \to 0.0222 (squares) and 0.0344→0.01160.0344 \to 0.0116 (triangular), while the maxima grow 36→9536 \to 95 and 50→14450 \to 144. Primes and Fibonacci numbers differ.

Conjecture 13.9 (CLM-C12-12) [COMPUTATIONALLY SUPPORTED; REDISCOVERY: OEIS A014589]. Subtract-a-prime satisfies g(n)≤11g(n) \le 11 for all nn. (Our check reaches 2⋅1052 \cdot 10^5; the OEIS entry records the bound up to 10910^9.)

Conjecture 13.10 (CLM-C12-13) [CONJECTURE]. Subtract-a-Fibonacci-number has bounded Grundy values (maximum 14 for n≤2⋅105n \le 2 \cdot 10^5; weak support, since it rose from 13 to 14 between 2.5⋅1042.5 \cdot 10^4 and 5⋅1045 \cdot 10^4).

13.7 Hypotheses that failed

H-C12-1 [REFUTED] (CLM-C12-10). “In each of the games G01–G06, g(n)g(n) depends only on the prime signature of nn.” Already n=2n = 2 and n=3n = 3 refute it in G01 (g=1g = 1 and 00). The first counterexamples elsewhere are (2,3)(2, 3) in G02, (4,9)(4, 9) in G03, (2,3)(2, 3) in G05 and (6,10)(6, 10) in G06 (values 2 and 4). Only G04 depends on the signature alone, as g=Ωg = \Omega predicts.

H-C12-2 [REFUTED] (CLM-C12-11). “Subtraction games whose move sets are the squares, the triangular numbers, the Fibonacci numbers or the primes keep a positive density of P-positions.” Squares and triangular numbers have density 0 (Theorem 13.8). For Fibonacci numbers (0.180) and primes (0.155) the question remains open.

H-C12-0 [INCONCLUSIVE] (CLM-C12-09). “Every game is eventually periodic, eventually arithmetic-periodic, or has a closed form in a number-theoretic function.” Thirteen of the 19 games conform. Subtract-a-square and subtract-a-triangular-number are provably not (arithmetic-)periodic, but “has no closed form” is not a theorem, so the hypothesis cannot be refuted as stated.

13.8 Experiments
