18.7 Comparison with existing mathematics

Feedback shift registers (Golomb, 1967), de Bruijn counts (van Aardenne-Ehrenfest and de Bruijn, 1951), periods of linear recurrences (Ward, 1933; Wall, 1960) and Fibonacci lengths of groups (Knox, 1992; Campbell and coauthors) cover the general theory. Tuscan squares (Etzion, Golomb and Taylor, 1989) impose similar distance-2 conditions on arrays rather than single cycles. We found no description of full-period quasigroups, their counts, the even-order isotope obstruction, or Fibonacci sequences in Laver tables.

18.8 Applications and limitations

The test against standard generators was negative: order-2 recurrences on nn symbols cannot have period longer than n2n^2, and affine versions modulo 2322^{32} inherit the known low-bit weaknesses of lagged Fibonacci generators. The test battery is a sanity check, not TestU01. The existence of full-period quasigroups beyond order 16 is unproved.

18.9 Open questions

  1. Do full-period quasigroups exist for every n≥5n \ge 5? A construction for even nn must avoid isotopes of groups (Theorem 18.7).
  2. How many are there of order 6, and is the count close to L(6)/36L(6)/36?
  3. Why do Fibonacci sequences in Laver tables reach the top within five steps?

19 Deciding laws of finite operations

Volume 7 — Computational experiments and algorithms. Research cycle C17.

19.1 Motivation

Every model found by the laboratory’s SAT searches had to be checked: is this table really associative, left-distributive, …? The obvious check examines all n3n^3 triples. This chapter measures how much the structure found in earlier chapters can save, against honest baselines.

19.2 Definitions

A finite magma is a table TT with T[a,b]=a⋆bT[a, b] = a \star b on nn elements. The cost of a test is its number of probes (table look-ups) — an exact, machine-independent measure. Wall-clock times were also recorded, on a shared machine, and are indicative only.

19.3 Three tests

Theorem 19.1 (Light’s test) [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY: Light, Clifford–Preston]. If SS generates the magma, then it is associative iff (x⋆s)⋆y=x⋆(s⋆y)(x \star s) \star y = x \star (s \star y) for all x,yx, y and all s∈Ss \in S.

Proof idea. The set of “good middles” ss is closed under ⋆\star: if aa and bb are good, four applications of the definition show that a⋆ba \star b is good. A submagma containing the generators is everything. ∎

Corollary 19.2 [PROVEN]. For an anchored magma (x⋆0=x+1x \star 0 = x + 1), associativity holds iff x⋆(y+1)=(x⋆y)+1x \star (y + 1) = (x \star y) + 1 for all x,yx, y — a check of 2n22n^2 probes. (Chapter 7: this identity alone forces x⋆y=x+y+1x \star y = x + y + 1.)

Theorem 19.3 [PROVEN (one-sidedness); detection bound cited — REDISCOVERY: Rajagopalan–Schulman 2000]. Comparing (u⋆v)⋆w(u \star v) \star w with u⋆(v⋆w)u \star (v \star w) for random subsets u,v,wu, v, w, computed in the magma algebra over GF(2), never rejects an associative table and rejects a non-associative one with probability at least 1/81/8 per round, at O(n2)O(n^2) probes per round.

19.4 The benchmark

Inputs: cyclic groups, anchored tables x+y+1x + y + 1, left-zero bands (x⋆y=xx \star y = x), random magmas, and single-entry corruptions of the first two — the hardest case for sampling, since one wrong entry spoils only about 4n4n of the n3n^3 triples.

family (n=512n = 512) exhaustive / Light probes exhaustive time Light + generators anchored test
cyclic group 383 1.7 s 6 ms —
anchored x+y+1x + y + 1 614 1.3 s 4 ms 3 ms
left-zero band 1.7 1.4 s 0.94 s —
one corrupted entry 0.15–1.1 0.16 s* 4–5 ms 3 ms
random 4⋅10−64 \cdot 10^{-6} (4 probes against ≈9⋅105\approx 9 \cdot 10^5) 0.19 s* 4–5 ms —

* Our vectorised implementation evaluates a whole slab of 64 rows; the probe column counts a sequential check that stops at the first violating triple.

The first three rows are certification: the input is associative and every exact test must do its full work. There Light’s test wins by two to three orders of magnitude whenever a small generating set exists. The last two rows are refutation, and there the exhaustive check wins: a random magma fails at the first triple it looks at. The first version of this table showed gains of 48–77 on these rows; the Skeptic traced them to an accounting error (the exhaustive check was charged a whole slab as soon as the slab contained a violation), and the numbers above are re-measured.

All exact tests and the randomised test (40 rounds) returned the correct verdict on all 90 instances; random-triple sampling with n2/16n^2/16 samples missed 27 of the 30 near-associative inputs.

H-C17-1 [REFUTED]. “Light’s test always wins by a factor of at least n/10n/10.” Left-zero bands need every element as a generator, and the gain collapses to 1.7×; on non-associative inputs there is no gain at all.

19.5 The anchor instance of a law

For anchored magmas, the instance z=0z = 0 of a three-variable law costs only O(n2)O(n^2) probes to check. For associativity it decides the law (Corollary 19.2). Does it always?

H-C17-2 [REFUTED]. Among the 382 laws of Chapter 7, the anchor instance decides the law for 339 (all anchored models with N≤5N \le 5) and is insufficient for 43 — e.g. x(yz)=y(y(xz))x(yz) = y(y(xz)) has 11 anchored models of its instance at N=4N = 4, two of which violate the law, and x(yz)=x(zy)x(yz) = x(zy) fails already at N=3N = 3. (The first version reported 333 / 18 and 31 undecided laws; a classification bug, found by the Skeptic, had counted laws with a violation at one size as undecided because the enumeration was capped at another.)

Observation 19.4 [PROVEN — computer-assisted for N≤4096N \le 4096, forcing argument in Lean; REDISCOVERY]. For every N≤4096N \le 4096, the single identity x⋆(y+1)=(x⋆y)⋆(x+1)x \star (y + 1) = (x \star y) \star (x + 1) (for all x,yx, y modulo NN) has an anchored solution exactly when NN is a power of 2, and then it is the Laver table. So left-distributivity of an anchored table is decided by an O(N2)O(N^2) check, at least for these sizes.

The first version checked only N≤10N \le 10, by SAT. The Skeptic then noticed why the search space is so small: the instances with y≤N−2y \le N - 2 already force the table. Row N−1N - 1 must be the identity, and going down, every entry x⋆(y+1)=v⋆(x+1)x \star (y+1) = v \star (x+1) with v=x⋆y>xv = x \star y > x refers to a row that is already determined — this is Laver’s recursion. So for every NN there is at most one candidate, and the question is only whether it satisfies the remaining wrap-around equations. Building the candidates for every N≤4096N \le 4096 (EXP-C17-03) shows they do exactly at the powers of 2. Lean checks the forcing argument, the existence of the candidate and the reduction to the wrap-around equations for every NN (C17_ForcedTable.lean).

The Historian’s second audit found the general case in the literature. Biane remarks, in the published version of his survey, that the full instance fails when NN is not a power of 2 (a correction credited to A. Lewandowski), and Aguilera and Iannella (2026) prove that the wrap-around equation at xx holds iff row xx ends in the top element; with Dehornoy’s criterion — the table is left-distributive iff every row ends in the top element — this settles every NN. The Historian also sketched a direct argument, and the Skeptic completed it and checked it in Lean:

Theorem 19.5 [PROVEN — Lean, from Laver’s theorem; REDISCOVERY: Biane, Aguilera–Iannella]. For every N≥1N \ge 1 the identity x⋆(y+1)=(x⋆y)⋆(x+1)x \star (y + 1) = (x \star y) \star (x + 1), wrap-around included, has an anchored solution iff NN is a power of 2.

Proof idea. Every row of the forced table is periodic, with period the first column at which it reaches the top element, and the wrap-around equation holds in row xx iff that period divides NN. Write N=2krN = 2^k r with r≥3r \ge 3 odd and s=N−2k+1s = N - 2^{k+1}. Using only that the Laver table of order 2k+12^{k+1} satisfies the identity (Laver’s theorem), the rows s,…,N−2s, \ldots, N - 2 of the forced table of order NN are shifted copies of rows of that Laver table, and row s−1s - 1 is s,s+1,…s, s + 1, \ldots with period 2k+12^{k+1}, which does not divide NN. ∎ The Lean file C17_RowPeriod.lean proves the equivalence for every NN with Laver’s theorem as its only hypothesis; the details are Theorem 5 of proofs/paper/C17.md.

This experiment also corrected the laboratory’s own citation of Laver’s theorem in Chapter 7: the classical recursion is imposed only for q<Nq < N; with the wrap-around instance there are no solutions at all for most NN.

19.6 Comparison with existing mathematics

Light’s test and the randomised test are classical; the anchored criteria are small corollaries of Chapter 7. The benchmark is ours.

19.7 Applications and limitations

Certifying a table found by a solver: compute a small generating set and run Light’s test — hundreds of times faster than the exhaustive check in our runs at n=512n = 512 — or, for anchored tables, a single identity. Limitations: Python implementations on a shared machine; no comparison with optimised C code or with model checkers.

19.8 Open questions

  1. (Answered in version 1.1 by Theorem 19.5.) What remains is to prove Laver’s theorem itself in Lean, which would make Theorem 19.5 unconditional.
  2. Which other laws admit a Light-type reduction to generators?

Volume 8 — Optimization and Practical Applications

Applications are claimed only with a baseline, a benchmark and an honest statement of limitations. Several candidate applications failed that test; they are reported here as well.

20 Quasigroups as mixers: an honest negative

Volume 8 — Optimization and practical applications. Research cycle C18.

20.1 Motivation

The laboratory invented and studied many finite operations: Latin squares, the nimber field of Chapter 14, the anchored structures of Chapter 7. A quasigroup — an operation whose every row and column is a permutation — is the natural candidate for a mixing primitive: Markovski, Gligoroski and Bakeva’s e-transformation b0=l⋆a0,bi=bi−1⋆aib_0 = l \star a_0, \qquad b_i = b_{i-1} \star a_i turns a byte string into another one, and iterating it is the core of published quasigroup hash designs. Can our operations serve as practical (non-cryptographic) hash functions, measured honestly against standard baselines? Nothing in this chapter is a security evaluation.

20.2 Definitions

We use seven quasigroups of order 256: a random Latin square (built row by row from random matchings), a random isotope of ℤ/256\mathbb{Z}/256, the nimber inversion quasigroup (x⊕y)−1(x \oplus y)^{-1} (the nim-product itself is not a quasigroup — its row 0 is zero), an affine operation 77x+141y+577x + 141y + 5, XOR, and the anchored quasigroups x+c(y)x + c(y) and (x+1)⊕y(x + 1) \oplus y of Chapter 7. A tail hash QG(TT, pp, rev) applies pp e-transformations to key ‖ length ‖ padding, optionally one more to the reversed string, and outputs the last 8 bytes. Baselines: FNV-1a-64, MX64 (multiply-xorshift with the splitmix64 finaliser), BLAKE2b-64 and CRC32.

20.3 What can be proved in advance

Theorem 20.1 [PROVEN — Lean: eT_injective, eT_length; REDISCOVERY: Markovski et al. 1999]. Every e-transformation is a bijection of strings of a given length; the d-transformation ai=bi−1∖bia_i = b_{i-1} \backslash b_i inverts it.

Theorem 20.2 [PROVEN — Lean: eT_ne_of_ne]. A difference at position ii changes exactly the outputs at positions ≥i\ge i. So a tail hash without a reverse pass cannot let a late input byte influence earlier tail bytes: its maximal avalanche bias is 1/21/2.

Theorem 20.3 [PROVEN — written; REDISCOVERY: Klimov–Shamir T-functions]. If the operation is a T-function — built from ++, ×\times, ⊕\oplus modulo 282^8, like the affine and the anchored (x+1)⊕y(x + 1) \oplus y quasigroups — then no hash built from it can move information from high input bits to low output bits.

Theorem 20.4 [PROVEN — written]. For XOR the whole hash is affine over GF(2): output differences do not depend on the key (as for CRC32). For the anchored x+c(y)x + c(y), differences between runs over the same inputs stay constant, so its one-pass hash cannot see the order of the message bytes.

20.4 The avalanche test: a misleading success

On 2000 random 16-byte keys, flipping each of the 128 input bits:

hash mean bias max bias bit independence (max corr.)
BLAKE2b-64 (reference) 0.0089 0.045 0.11
MX64 0.0090 0.045 0.11
QG random Latin square, 2 passes + reverse 0.0089 0.041 0.11
QG nimber inversion, 2 passes + reverse 0.0090 0.042 0.11
QG x+c(y)x + c(y), 2 passes + reverse 0.0091 0.043 1.00
QG random Latin square, forward only 0.090 0.5 1.00
FNV-1a-64 0.098 0.5 1.00
QG affine / (x+1)⊕y(x+1) \oplus y 0.39 / 0.34 0.5 1.00
QG XOR, CRC32 0.5 0.5 1.00

The bold hypothesis H-C18-1 — a random Latin square with two passes and a reverse pass reaches BLAKE2b’s avalanche — holds. The anchored x+c(y)x + c(y) has ideal per-bit flip rates but perfectly correlated bit changes, exactly as Theorem 20.4 predicts.

20.5 The distribution test: a collapse, and its theorem

On 65,536 decimal strings, near-duplicate keys and word-like strings, every reverse-pass quasigroup hash failed badly: up to 2.7 times the expected collisions in 2162^{16} buckets and 32,624 identical 64-bit hash values among the near-duplicates. The nimber hash, conjectured to be competitive with FNV-1a (H-C18-3), is refuted (chi-square z=1084z = 1084 on decimal strings). The explanation is not a bug:

Theorem 20.5 (tail-information bound) [PROVEN — Lean core: eT_append, eT_tail_factors]. The last 8 outputs of an e-transformation are a bijective function of one state byte and the last 7 inputs. Consequently a reverse-pass tail hash gives at most 256 different values to all keys sharing their first 7 bytes, and a pp-pass forward hash at most 256p256^p to keys sharing their last 7 bytes.

Proof idea. bjb_j is determined by bj−1b_{j-1} and aja_j, and conversely aj=bj−1∖bja_j = b_{j-1} \backslash b_j; the rest of the message enters only through bn−8b_{n-8}. ∎

The bound is attained exactly on data (256 values in every one of 100 test families), and it predicts the observed number of collisions exactly: 16×(2295−256)=32,62416 \times (2295 - 256) = 32{,}624. A byte-level e-transformation is a pipeline with an 8-bit state, however good its avalanche looks — which is why published quasigroup hashes used enormous quasigroups.

20.6 A repair that works — and does not pay

Theorem 20.5 suggests keeping a full 64-bit state: run eight e-transformations in parallel and output the final state of each (a cascade). Its state update is a bijection of the 8-byte state (Theorem 20.6 [PROVEN — written]), so the bound no longer applies.

hash max bias BIC flagged key sets MB/s (indicative)
cascade, random Latin square 0.048 0.11 none 13
cascade, ℤ/256\mathbb{Z}/256 isotope 0.046 0.11 none 10
cascade, nimber inversion 0.085 0.15 one marginal 10
cascade, x+c(y)x + c(y) 0.116 0.18 none 9
MX64 0.045 0.11 none 165
BLAKE2b-64 0.045 0.11 none 41

The cascades over random-like quasigroups pass every test we ran. The bold hypothesis that all three random-like cascades would pass (H-C18-8) is refuted by the nimber cascade, whose structure (x⋆x=0x \star x = 0, commutativity) leaves a measurable bias.

Result 20.7 [COMPUTATIONALLY SUPPORTED — improvement: NO IMPROVEMENT]. No quasigroup construction improves significantly on the best baseline in any measured dimension (some are numerically slightly better on a single statistic, within noise): the good cascades only match MX64 and BLAKE2b in quality and are several times slower in the same run. Throughput depends on machine load — the tail-output variants reached 11–52 MB/s in one run and 20–98 MB/s in the Skeptic’s re-run, against 640 and 428 MB/s for numpy-vectorised FNV-1a and MX64 — but the ordering is stable. Against FNV-1a alone the good cascades mix better, at a large speed cost. (FNV-1a’s z=−9.8z = -9.8 on sequential keys means more uniform than random; the first version of this chapter read it as a defect.)

20.7 Comparison with existing mathematics

The e-transformation and its bijectivity are Markovski, Gligoroski and Bakeva’s (1999); T-functions are Klimov and Shamir’s (2002). Quasigroup hashes submitted to the SHA-3 competition (Edon-R, NaSHA) used huge quasigroups in wide-pipe designs and were nevertheless attacked. We found no explicit statement of the byte-level tail-information bound, which is elementary.

20.8 Applications and limitations

The outcome is negative and is reported as such. Limitations: Python and numpy implementations on a shared machine (timings indicative); a small subset of standard hash tests; one random Latin square, not uniformly sampled; 16-byte keys for the avalanche test. None of this is a security evaluation.

20.9 Open questions

  1. Do cascades over larger quasigroups (order 2162^{16}), in optimised code, become competitive?
  2. Which properties of a quasigroup (commutativity, relations such as x⋆x=0x \star x = 0, its isotopy class) leave a bias in cascades?
  3. How does the avalanche bias of a cascade over a random Latin square decay with its depth?

Volume 9 — Failed Theories and Mathematical Counterexamples

The laboratory’s rule is that failures are never deleted.

Failed theories and counterexamples

Volume 9 — Failed theories and mathematical counterexamples. Generated from the claim database.

Every bold hypothesis the laboratory proposed and refuted is kept here with the counterexample that refuted it. Failures are part of the record: several of them (the Fibonacci pattern of Chapter 2, the non-rational complexity of Chapter 5, the odd and golden spectra of Chapter 7) led directly to the positive results of the book.

C01 — Quantity as the complete invariant of rewriting

  • CLM-C01-05 (refuted). Claimed: For every relation u <-> v over {a,b} with u != v and equal symbol counts, the generated congruence coincides with counting (Parikh) equivalence on all words of length >= |u|. Counterexample: u = aab, v = aba: the word baa is fixed by every rewrite, so its class {baa} is smaller than its counting class {aab, aba, baa}. All 33 relations of length 3-4 fail (EXP-C01-04).

C02 — Exchange systems: positional numeration as normal forms

  • CLM-C02-06 (refuted). Claimed: For every carry rule with sum b < m, each integer has exactly one stable configuration of that value. Counterexample: 4[k] -> 2[k+1] (base 2 with digits 0..3): the stable configurations ‘2’ and ‘10’ both have value 2. Also 4[k] -> [k+2] on all levels, 4[k] -> 2[k+2], 4[k] -> 2[k+3] (EXP-C02-04).

C03 — Termination of one-rule string rewriting 0^p 1^q -> 1^r 0^s

  • CLM-C03-07 (refuted). Claimed: The rule 0^p 1^q -> 1^r 0^s terminates iff s <= p or r <= q. Counterexample: 0011 -> 111000 (both counts grow) is terminating - a published theorem (A. Geser, ‘A Solution to Zantema’s Problem’, Passau MIP-9314, 1993; Zantema-Geser 2000; automated RFC match-bound proofs). Our own evidence agrees (all 3747 words of length <= 12 containing 0011 have finite derivation trees) but we have no independent proof; the refutation rests on the cited proof.
  • CLM-C03-08 (refuted). Claimed: The rule 0^p 1^q -> 1^r 0^s terminates iff r < 2q or s < 2p. Counterexample: 011 -> 11100 (r = 3 < 4 = 2q) loops: 001111 ->4 111 001111 00100 (Lean-verified, not_terminating_0_11). 40 counterexamples with parameters <= 5 (35 loops of EXP-C03-02 plus the 5 mirror loops of EXP-C03-03; count corrected after the skeptic review).
  • CLM-C03-09 (refuted). Claimed: The rule is non-terminating iff r > q, s > p and (r >= 2q or s >= 2p). Counterexample: 0011 -> 11100000 (p = q = 2, r = 3, s = 5): H-C03-3 predicts non-termination (r > q, s > p, s >= 2p), but the rule terminates by Zantema-Geser Theorem 1.1, case 4 (q < r < 2q and p does not divide s). The refutation rests on the published proof.

C04 — Derivation lengths, random descent and rewriting games

  • CLM-C04-02 (refuted). Claimed: A one-rule system l -> r with |l| <= 3 is non-terminating iff l is a factor of r. Counterexample: ab -> bbaa is non-terminating although ab is not a factor of bbaa: aab -> abbaa -> bbaabaa = bb.aab.aa. In total 18 such rules with |r| <= 5 (EXP-C04-02).
  • CLM-C04-07 (refuted). Claimed: A terminating one-rule system has random descent iff its left-hand side is unbordered. Counterexample: aa -> a has the bordered left-hand side aa but random descent (every derivation from w has sum over a-runs of (length - 1) steps and ends in the same word); 18 bordered rules with |l|, |r| <= 3 have random descent (EXP-C04-05). The ‘if’ direction is CLM-C04-05.
  • CLM-C04-11 (refuted). Claimed: For every terminating one-rule system over {a,b} with |l|, |r| <= 3, the derivational complexity D(n) satisfies a linear recurrence. Counterexample: aab -> ba (and its 3 symmetric images): D(n) = n - 1 - kappa(n) is not given by any linear recurrence (CLM-C04-09).
  • CLM-C04-14 (refuted). Claimed: Every rewriting game of a terminating one-rule system with |l|, |r| <= 3 has Sprague-Grundy values <= 3. Counterexample: aa -> b: the word a^14 has Grundy value 4 (computed from its own derivation graph); Dawson’s Kayles reaches 9, Officers 91 for n <= 3000 (EXP-C04-08).

C05 — Spectra of single-identity magma varieties

  • CLM-C05-01 (refuted). Claimed: Every identity t = x has spectrum {1} or the set of all positive integers. Counterexample: ETP law 167, x = (y*x)*(x*y): Lean-certified models of orders 4 and 5 (T4_law167, T5_law167) and no model of order 2, 3, 6 or 7 (law167_card_mod_four: every finite model has order 0 or 1 mod 4). The census EXP-C05-01 finds 61 of 115 nontrivial two-variable identities with spectra on n <= 6 that are neither {1} nor everything.
  • CLM-C05-03 (refuted). Claimed: If x occurs in t but is neither its leftmost nor its rightmost leaf, every finite model of t = x is a quasigroup. Counterexample: The 4-element model T4 of law 167, x = (y*x)*(x*y), has row 1 = (0, 2, 2, 2) (Lean: T4_law167, T4_not_latin). In the census, 18 of the 115 identities already have non-quasigroup models of order <= 3.
  • CLM-C05-06 (refuted). Claimed: A nontrivial identity t = x has models of all orders 1..6 iff it has an affine model x*y = a x + b y + c over Z_n for every n <= 6. Counterexample: x = (y*x)*(x*(y*y)) has models of every order 1..6 (EXP-C05-01); of order 2 it is satisfied by NAND (Lean: nand2_law), yet no affine operation over Z_2 satisfies it (Lean: no_affine_model_mod2); its affine models exist only for n in {1, 5}. Three further identities behave alike (EXP-C05-05).
  • CLM-C05-09 (refuted). Claimed: The finite models of x = y(x((yx)y)) (ETP 677) and of x = y(((yx)x)y) (ETP 1313) have exactly the orders 1 and the odd integers n >= 5. Counterexample: Linear models of order 16 (found by the Skeptic, reviews/skeptic_C05_C08_C12.md, Appendix B) - over GF(16) = GF(2)[t]/(t^4 + t + 1), x*y = (1 + t^3)x + t^3 y satisfies law 677 and x*y = t^3 x + (1 + t^3)y satisfies law 1313 (each has 8 such pairs (a, b)). Re-found by EXP-C05-07 and Lean-certified (E677_order16_law, E1313_order16_law, kernel decide on the 16 x 16 tables).

C06 — Successor-anchored magmas: spectra of composition laws

  • CLM-C06-04 (refuted). Claimed: Among the laws x(yz) = t and (xy)z = t with t a term of 3-4 leaves over exactly {x, y, z}, every law other than left-distributivity has anchored spectrum {1} or {1, …, 6}. Counterexample: 21 other laws have restricted spectra (EXP-C06-03): (xy)z = x((yz)z) - odd N; (xy)z = x(y(zz)) - N = 1, 5 (and 11, 19, … by Theorem 6 of proofs/paper/C06.md); x(yz) = ((zx)x)y - N = 1, 3; x(yz) = ((xx)y)z - N = 1, 2.
  • CLM-C06-09 (refuted). Claimed: For every law of EXP-C06-03 other than left-distributivity, the anchored spectrum for N <= 6 equals the set of N for which some affine table x*y = x + a*y + 1 satisfies the law. Counterexample: x(yz) = y(x(yz)), x(yz) = y(x(xz)), x(yz) = y(y(xz)) and x(yz) = (yx)(yz) have anchored models for every N <= 6 but no affine model for N > 1; x(yz) = y((yx)z) has a non-affine model at N = 2 (EXP-C06-04).

C07 — Arithmetic from the minimal excludant: a census of mex-defined operations

  • CLM-C07-02 (refuted). Claimed: Apart from trivial operations (0, projections), the only associative mex operations of the family are Conway’s nim-sum and nim-product. Counterexample: L+R+D[z] gives ordinary addition a + b and D[z] gives min(a, b) (Theorem 1); also the zero-absorbing shifts of XOR and +.
  • CLM-C07-04 (refuted). Claimed: Every commutative mex operation of the family is closed on [0, 2), [0, 4) and [0, 16), like the nimbers. Counterexample: D[x^y]: f(2,3) = 4, so [0,4) is not closed; 30 of the 34 commutative operations fail (EXP-C07-01).
  • CLM-C07-06 (refuted). Claimed: For every operation of the family and every a, the row b -> f(a,b) satisfies f(a, b + p) = f(a, b) + s for all large b, for some p >= 1 and s, as for Wythoff’s game. Counterexample: Row 2 of D[x^y] (Theorem 3): |f(2,b) - b| = J_k on the k-th block pair, which rules out every (p, s). Numerically only 222 of 670 rows (a = 1..5, b < 1500) have a period <= 375.
  • CLM-C07-07 (refuted). Claimed: For every commutative mex operation of the family, 0 is absorbing or an identity, and the diagonal f(n,n) is a sequence in the OEIS. Counterexample: The first part holds (Theorem 2); the second fails - e.g. the diagonal 0, 2, 1, 5, 7, 4, 3, 16, 13, 10, 15, 6, … of L+R+D[x^y^z] and 0, 1, 3, 6, 8, 11, 10, 19, … of D[z]+D[x^y^z] have no OEIS match (88 unmatched sequences, EXP-C07-02, OEIS state of 2026-10-18).

C08 — Circle multiplications on recurrence numeration systems

  • CLM-C08-05 (refuted). Claimed: Smallest shift s0 with o_s associative for all tested s0 <= s <= max tested: Fibonacci 2, Pell (1,2,5) 1, G(k+1)=2G(k)+2G(k-1) (1,2) 5, tribonacci 3, 4-bonacci 7, G(k+1)=G(k)+G(k-2) 10, G(k+1)=3G(k)+3G(k-1) (1,5) 10; G(k+1)=G(k)+G(k-3) none up to 20 (full table in results/EXP-C08-02.json and EXP-C08-03.json). Counterexample: Certified violations (EXP-C08-06) - tribonacci s = 3: (10016 o 10016) o 1 = 3414681926 != 3414681924 = 10016 o (10016 o 1) (the example of Ambroz-Frougny-Masakova-Pelantova 2003); 4-bonacci s = 7: triple (623729, 581581, 1); G(k+1) = 2G(k) + 2G(k-1) (1,2) s = 5: triple (29957, 280628, 1); G(k+1) = G(k) + G(k-2) s = 10: triple (78, 248, 1) (found by the Historian). The random tests behind the measurement (triples up to 10^6) missed these rare failures. The Skeptic’s review adds G(k+1) = 3G(k) + 3G(k-1) (1,5) at s = 10: (173938 o 173938) o 1 != 173938 o (173938 o 1) - the fifth wrong value. Thresholds after the targeted search in CLM-C08-08.
  • CLM-C08-06 (refuted). Claimed: Among digit-convolution products a o_s b = sum d_i d_j G_(i+j+s), only Fibonacci with s = 2 is associative. Counterexample: Fibonacci with s = 3 (t = 1) is associative by Theorem 1 (CLM-C08-01); geometric bases are associative for every s; many Pisot sequences are associative at larger shifts (EXP-C08-02).
  • CLM-C08-07 (refuted). Claimed: For the circle product o_s: Fibonacci (1,2) fails for s <= 1 and is associative for s >= 2 (Lean, CLM-C08-01); Pell (1,2,5) fails at s = 0, no failure found for 1 <= s <= 5 (associative for s >= 1 by Messaoudi 2000); G(k+1) = 2G(k) + 2G(k-1) (1,2) fails for s <= 5, no failure found for 6 <= s <= 8; tribonacci (1,2,4) fails for s <= 3, no failure found for 4 <= s <= 9; 4-bonacci (1,2,4,8) fails for s <= 7, no failure found for 8 <= s <= 10; G(k+1) = G(k) + G(k-2) (1,2,3) fails for s <= 10, no failure found for s = 11, 12; G(k+1) = 3G(k) + 3G(k-1) (1,5) fails for s <= 9, no failure found for 10 <= s <= 12. Every failure is certified by an explicit triple; every “no failure” is computational support only. Counterexample: Two of its ‘no failure found’ ranges were wrong (Skeptic review 2026-10-18, reviews/skeptic_C05_C08_C12.md, P2) - G(k+1) = 2G(k) + 2G(k-1) (1,2) fails at s = 6: (314965 o 314965) o 1 != 314965 o (314965 o 1); G(k+1) = 3G(k) + 3G(k-1) (1,5) fails at s = 10 and s = 11: (173938 o 173938) o 1 != 173938 o (173938 o 1). Both re-verified with the lab’s code and re-found by EXP-C08-07 (e.g. (5653, 42197, 1) at s = 6; (3192, 173938, 1) at s = 10; (12100, 12100, 1) at s = 11). The certified lower bounds of this claim stand; the thresholds it implied for these two sequences do not. Superseded by CLM-C08-08.

C09 — Arithmetic of Knuth’s circle semigroup

  • CLM-C09-02 (refuted). Claimed: Every n >= 1 has a unique multiset of circle-irreducible factors. Counterexample: 8 = 1 o 1 o 1 = 2 o 2 with 1, 2 irreducible (every product is >= 3); the factorisations even differ in length.
  • CLM-C09-04 (refuted). Claimed: The proportion of circle-irreducible numbers up to x tends to 0 as x tends to infinity. Counterexample: By CLM-C09-03 the irreducibles contain a set of natural density 1/phi^2 = 0.3819…; observed density is 0.539 at 1e6.

C10 — The cost of counting across representations

  • CLM-C10-03 (refuted). Claimed: The average number of digit changes per increment in the Zeckendorf system is smaller than in binary. Counterexample: Both averages tend to exactly 2 (Theorem 2 and the binary case of Theorem 1); at n < 1e6 the Zeckendorf average is 1.999992 vs binary 1.999988.

C11 — Pattern-terminated universal integer codes

  • CLM-C11-05 (refuted). Claimed: For every k, the all-ones terminator 1^k gives the shortest codeword among all length-k terminators for every n beyond a small threshold. Counterexample: k = 4, n = 1000: terminators 1000, 1001 and 1010 give codewords 1 bit shorter than 1111. Rivals beat 1^k at 762, 972 and 1127 sampled n >= 1000 (all n <= 2048 and quarter-powers of 2 up to 2^64) for k = 4, 5, 6, up to n = 311743, 2^45 and about 1.3e19 respectively (EXP-C11-02). By Theorem 2 (CLM-C11-02), 1^k does win eventually.
  • CLM-C11-06 (refuted). Claimed: (a) The average over n <= N of L_(1^k)(n) - L_u(n), u unbordered of length k + 1, tends to log_lambda(1/(lambda - 1)). (b) Corrected version: this average converges to (2 - lambda)/(lambda - 1)^2. Counterexample: k = 3 (111 vs 0111): the average over n <= 2^40 is 0.2282 against the prediction 0.2875 of (a) (EXP-C11-06); the running average is 0.228181, 0.309709 and 0.228548 at N = 2^40, 2^80 and 2^120, so it does not converge (EXP-C11-07). Both statements hold only for k = 2 (value 1).

C12 — Relational one-heap games

  • CLM-C12-09 (inconclusive). Claimed: Every game of the catalogue is eventually periodic, eventually arithmetic-periodic, or has a Grundy function given by a closed form in a number-theoretic function. Status: The “closed form” clause is not precisely defined, so the hypothesis cannot be refuted as stated; recorded as inconclusive rather than refuted.
  • CLM-C12-10 (refuted). Claimed: For each of the games G01-G06, g(n) depends only on the prime signature (sorted exponent vector) of n. Counterexample: G01: n = 2 and n = 3 have signature (1) but g = 1 and 0. Further first counterexamples: G02 (2, 3: g = 2, 1), G03 (4, 9: 1, 0), G05 (2, 3: 1, 2), G06 (6, 10: 2, 4). Only G04 (g = Omega) depends on the signature alone.
  • CLM-C12-11 (refuted). Claimed: Subtraction games whose move sets are the squares, the triangular numbers, the Fibonacci numbers or the primes have P-positions of positive natural density. Counterexample: Squares and triangular numbers: the P-set has density 0 (CLM-C12-08); measured P-density 0.0538 -> 0.0222 and 0.0344 -> 0.0116 as N grows from 1.25e4 to 2e5. For Fibonacci numbers (0.180) and primes (0.155) the question remains open.

C13 — Anchored spectra II: which sets of integers are spectra?

  • CLM-C13-07 (refuted). Claimed: If a set of laws has an anchored model of size N, it has one of every size d dividing N. Counterexample: x = x(x(xx)) has models of every size except 2 (CLM-C13-05, Lean). Violations are common - 24 of 253 one-variable laws (N <= 12), 81 of 300 random 2-variable laws (N <= 9), 29 of 150 random 3-variable laws and 17 of 80 random pairs of laws (N <= 8); e.g. ((xy)x)y = (y(xx))x has models at N = 6 and 8 but none at N = 2, 3, 4, 5.
  • CLM-C13-08 (refuted). Claimed: For every law with at most 4 leaves per side (3 variables) or 5 leaves per side (2 variables) whose affine spectrum is infinite and not all N, every anchored model is affine. Counterexample: 146 of the 147 laws tested from infinite non-trivial affine classes have non-affine models (EXP-C13-02, corrected run), all at every N = 6, 7, 8; e.g. x = x(x(xx)) (affine spectrum A034017, non-affine models for N = 3..8) and (xx)x = (yy)y (non-affine models for N = 2..8). (The first run also tested 52 laws from finite classes Div(L), L >= 61, through a faulty filter - they are all non-rigid too.)
  • CLM-C13-09 (refuted). Claimed: Every law t1(x) = t2(x) in one variable has anchored spectrum {1} or contains all sufficiently large N. Counterexample: x = (xx)x has spectrum exactly {1, 2}: at x = 0 the anchor gives 0 = 2, and x*y = x + 1 is a model of size 2; likewise x = ((xx)x)x has spectrum {1, 3} and x = (((xx)x)x)x has {1, 2, 4} (proofs/paper/C13.md, remark after Theorem 5).

C14 — Graphs as quantities: the 256 local graph products

  • CLM-C14-08 (refuted). Claimed: Each of the six commutative associative local products with unit K1 (Cartesian, strong, symmetric difference, and their complement-duals 221, co-normal, 141) has unique prime factorisation on connected graphs, or, for the complement-duals, on co-connected graphs. Counterexample: Symmetric difference: K2 xor K2 xor K2 is isomorphic to K2 xor K_{1,3}; K2 and K_{1,3} are connected and prime, so this connected graph of order 8 has the two factorisations {K2,K2,K2} and {K2,K_{1,3}} (proofs/paper/C14.md Theorem 8; explicit isomorphism in EXP-C14-06). In all graphs already C4 = K2 xor K2 = K2 xor 2K1. The other five products show no collision up to order 15 in the stated classes, consistent with Sabidussi-Vizing and Doerfler-Imrich/McKenzie.

C15 — Magma cellular automata: light cones, streaming width and fast-forwarding

  • CLM-C15-08 (refuted). Claimed: For every magma automaton of order <= 3 the streaming width lambda(t) is either bounded or grows geometrically; there is no intermediate growth. Counterexample: The fuse magma [[0,0,0],[0,0,1],[1,2,2]] has floor((floor(t/2)+1)/2) <= lambda(t) <= 3t + 3 (Theorem 4; lambda(t) = t + 2 for t <= 12); 104 order-3 classes show linear growth on t <= 9 (EXP-C15-02).
  • CLM-C15-09 (refuted). Claimed: An order-3 magma automaton has bounded streaming width iff the magma is a semigroup, is affine over Z_3, or its light cone depends on at most two cells. Counterexample: The ‘if’ direction holds for all 18 semigroup and 8 affine classes, but 685 bounded-width classes lie outside the three classes, e.g. the reversible automaton [[0,0,2],[2,2,0],[1,1,1]] whose light cone uses all t + 1 cells and whose width stays 3 (EXP-C15-02).
  • CLM-C15-10 (refuted). Claimed: A magma automaton admits a fast-forward (rows at time t computable in time polynomial in L and log t) if and only if its streaming width is bounded. Counterexample: The fuse magma has unbounded (linear) streaming width but its row at any time t is computable in O(L) operations (Theorem 4). The converse direction is open (CLM-C15-18).
  • CLM-C15-11 (refuted). Claimed: For at least half of the order-3 classes a random cyclic row of length 32 repeats within 5000 steps, and only classes with geometric streaming width have rows that do not. Counterexample: The first part holds (1590/1734 classes at L = 32), the second fails - the bounded-width affine quasigroups x + y, 2x + y, 2x + 2y, 2x + 2y + 1 (mod 3, up to relabelling) do not repeat within 5000 steps at L = 32 and 64 (EXP-C15-04). The cap matters (Skeptic) - at L = 32 they do repeat, with periods 6,560 or 19,680 (still long, so the refutation stands); at L = 64 none repeats within 200,000 steps.
  • CLM-C15-12 (refuted). Claimed: Each of the affine, commutative-semigroup and fuse fast-forwards is exact, beats direct numpy simulation by more than 100x at L = 1024, t = 10^5, and loses for t below a few hundred steps. Counterexample: Exactness holds (99/99), but the commutative-semigroup method reaches only x62 (mult mod 3) and x77 (min) at L = 1024, t = 10^5 (the Skeptic’s re-run - 79-97x). The clause ‘loses for small t’ is partly true - timed with the same protocol on both sides, the affine method loses for t below about 20 and the fuse method below about 20-70 (Skeptic review, Appendix C). The first version said the affine and fuse methods never lose; EXP-C15-03 had timed the fast method as the best of 3 runs and the simulation as a single run.

C16 — Fibonacci dynamics in invented finite algebras

  • CLM-C16-02 (refuted). Claimed: As for Pisano periods (where (0, 0) is fixed), the maximal period of the pair map on an n-element magma is at most n^2 - 1, and the maximum is attained only by quasigroups. Counterexample: x*y = 1 - y on {0,1} has the single cycle (0,0) -> (0,1) -> (1,1) -> (1,0) of length 4 = n^2 and is not a quasigroup; every n has full-period tables (Theorem 2); for n <= 4 none of them is a quasigroup (EXP-C16-01).
  • CLM-C16-06 (refuted). Claimed: For every n, no quasigroup of order n has a full-period pair map; the maximal period of a quasigroup of order n is n^2 - 1. Counterexample: True for n = 2, 3, 4, false for n = 5 - exactly 3360 of the 161280 Latin squares of order 5 are full-period (e.g. the cycle 0,0,1,0,2,2,0,3,3,1,2,1,4,0,4,4,2,3,2,4,1,1,3,4,3); examples exist for every order 6-16 (EXP-C16-02).

C17 — Deciding laws of finite magmas: generator, anchor and randomised tests against the exhaustive check

  • CLM-C17-06 (refuted). Claimed: Light’s test with a greedy generating set uses at most a fraction 10/n of the exhaustive check’s probes on every input family. Counterexample: Left-zero band x*y = x (associative; n generators needed) - exhaustive/Light probe ratio 1.71 at n = 512 (EXP-C17-01). The first version also cited one-entry corruptions of cyclic groups (ratio 47.9 at n = 512); that number was an artefact of the probe accounting (Skeptic review, P1). With correct accounting an early-exit exhaustive check is about as cheap as Light’s test on corruptions and far cheaper on random magmas, so the hypothesis fails on non-associative inputs as well.
  • CLM-C17-07 (refuted). Claimed: For every law of the C06 family, every anchored magma satisfying the instance z = 0 of the law satisfies the law (N <= 5). Counterexample: 43 of the 382 laws (first violating anchored model at N = 3 for 33 laws, at N = 4 for 10), e.g. x(yz) = x(zy) (54 violating models among 82 at N = 3), x(yz) = y(y(xz)) (two violating models among 11 at N = 4), x(yz) = x((xy)z), (xy)z = (zy)x, (xy)z = (x(zz))y (EXP-C17-02). For the other 339 laws the instance decides the law for N <= 5, among them associativity, and (N <= 10) left-distributivity. The first version reported 18 insufficient and 31 undecided laws because of a classification bug - a law whose enumeration was capped at one N was counted as undecided even when a violation had been found at another N; the Skeptic’s independent census gives the same 339 / 43 / 0 as the corrected experiment.

C18 — Invented operations as mixing primitives: quasigroup string transformations versus standard hashes

  • CLM-C18-09 (inconclusive). Claimed: Every structured quasigroup tested (affine, XOR, x + c(y), (x + 1) XOR y) gives a larger maximal avalanche bias than the random Latin square, in the reverse-pass (p2+rev) construction. (As first worded - ‘in the same construction’ - the hypothesis is false for the forward-only construction, where the random Latin square and x + c(y) both have bias exactly 1/2 by CLM-C18-02; the experiment compared every structured variant with QG-LS p2+rev.) Status: Proved (bias 1/2) for the T-function and GF(2)-affine quasigroups (CLM-C18-04, CLM-C18-05). For x + c(y) with a reverse pass the difference (0.043 versus 0.041) is within sampling noise; its defect shows in bit independence instead (CLM-C18-06), which the hypothesis did not measure.
  • CLM-C18-10 (refuted). Claimed: QG-NIMINV p2+rev (x*y = (x XOR y)^-1 in the nimber field of order 256) has avalanche at least as good as FNV-1a-64, |chi-square z| <= 3 and a bucket-collision ratio within 2% on all four key sets. Counterexample: Avalanche is better than FNV-1a’s (max bias 0.0415 vs 0.5), but on the decimal strings 0..65535 the 1024-bucket z-score is 1084 and the bucket-collision ratio 2.62, and near-duplicate keys give 32,624 full 64-bit collisions - the tail-information bound (CLM-C18-03). (FNV-1a itself has z = -9.8 there - more uniform than random, bucket-collision ratio 0.995 - and 0 full collisions; the first wording wrongly attributed |z| <= 3 on all four key sets to FNV-1a.)
  • CLM-C18-11 (refuted). Claimed: The cascades QC-LS, QC-ISO and QC-NIMINV (depth 8, 8 zero bytes of finalisation) have avalanche within 0.01 of BLAKE2b-64, |z| <= 3, collision ratios within 2% and no full collisions on all four key sets. Counterexample: QC-NIMINV d8 - maximal avalanche bias 0.0845 against 0.045 for BLAKE2b (about 7 standard deviations of a cell; the Skeptic’s replication with new keys - 0.0735, against a null maximum of 0.048; worst cell key byte 15, bit 0 -> top bit of s1). QC-LS d8 and QC-ISO d8 pass every test (EXP-C18-05). (A z = -3.21 on synthetic words was also cited at first; it is 1 of 48 z-scores and in the benign direction - more uniform than random - so the refutation rests on the avalanche bias alone.)
  • CLM-C18-12 (refuted). Claimed: The cascade QC-ARP d8 has a maximal bit-independence correlation of at least 0.5. Counterexample: QC-ARP d8 has maximal correlation 0.176 (noise level about 0.11) - the cascade removes the perfect correlation of QG-ARP p2+rev, although 5 avalanche cells still exceed bias 0.1 (EXP-C18-05).

C19 — The longest Zeckendorf game

  • CLM-C19-10 (refuted). Claimed: D(n) = sum_t floor(j_t^2/4) - sum_t R(j_t, j_{t+1}) over consecutive Zeckendorf levels j_1 > j_2 > … of n. Counterexample: n = 12 = F_5 + F_3 + F_1 (levels 4, 2, 0): D(12) = 0 but the model gives 1; 23,680 of the n < 30000 violate it (EXP-C19-03).
  • CLM-C19-11 (refuted). Claimed: D(n) - D(n - F_top) is a function of the two highest Zeckendorf levels of n. Counterexample: Levels (4, 2) occur with D(n) - D(n - F_5) in {0, 1} (n = 12 and n = 11); 87 such ambiguous pairs below 30000 (EXP-C19-03). The dependence is through the parity chain of lower levels, captured by rho in CLM-C19-05.
  • CLM-C19-12 (refuted). Claimed: There is a function phi(start, length) with D(n) = sum of phi over the maximal runs of ones of the lazy representation of n. Counterexample: For n < 160 the linear system D(n) = sum phi(run) has coefficient rank 46 and augmented rank 47 (exact rational elimination), so it has no solution (EXP-C19-03).
  • CLM-C19-13 (refuted). Claimed: L(n + 1) = L(n) + lambda(n), where lambda(n) is the longest game from the Zeckendorf representation of n plus one extra F_1. Counterexample: n = 3: L(4) = 3, L(3) = 2, lambda(3) = 0 (Zeckendorf(3) + F_1 is already Zeckendorf); fails for 288 of the first 300 n (EXP-C19-02). Longest games avoid early normalisation, so they do not pass through Zeckendorf(n).

Volume 10 — Open Problems and Future Mathematical Research

What remains open, stated precisely enough to be attacked.

21 Closing an open problem, and what remains open

Volume 10 — Open problems and future mathematical research. Research cycle C20.

21.1 Motivation

Chapter 5 classified 860 of the 868 one-rule systems l→rl \to r over {a,b}\{a, b\} with |l|≤3|l| \le 3, |r|≤5|r| \le 5. Eight rules — the images of aab→ababaaab \to ababa and aab→babaaaab \to babaa under reversal and the exchange of letters — resisted every certificate the laboratory tried: no loop, no interpretation. The last research cycle of the batch went back to them.

21.2 Two tools from earlier chapters

Random descent. The left side aabaab has no border, so by Theorem 5.4 all maximal derivations from a word have the same length; one terminating strategy settles termination for all of them.

Runs. Writing w=ax0bax1b⋯baxmw = a^{x_0} b\, a^{x_1} b \cdots b\, a^{x_m}, a redex is a run xk≥2x_k \ge 2 followed by a bb. For aab→ababaaab \to ababa a step turns (xk,xk+1)(x_k, x_{k+1}) into (xk−1,1,xk+1+1)(x_k - 1, 1, x_{k+1} + 1): one surplus aa moves one bb to the right and a new run of length 1 appears behind it. For aab→babaaaab \to babaa a pair moves: (xk−2,1,xk+1+2)(x_k - 2, 1, x_{k+1} + 2).

21.3 The results

Theorem 21.1 [PROVEN — written; termination REDISCOVERY (barren rules), complexity POTENTIALLY NOVEL]. aab→ababaaab \to ababa terminates, and its derivational complexity is exactly D(n)=2n−2−1(n≥2),D(n) = 2^{n-2} - 1 \qquad (n \ge 2), attained by an−1ba^{n-1} b.

Proof idea. Always rewrite the rightmost redex. A surplus aa then travels to the right — each step brings it one bb closer to the end — until it fills an empty run or reaches the last run: a finite journey. Journeys never create new surplus, so there are at most (number of aa’s) −1- 1 of them. Each step inserts one bb, and a journey has at most as many steps as there are bb’s, so every journey at most doubles the number of bb’s. For an−1ba^{n-1} b every journey has exactly that many steps: 1+2+⋯+2n−31 + 2 + \cdots + 2^{n-3}. ∎

Theorem 21.2 [PROVEN — written; POTENTIALLY NOVEL]. aab→babaaaab \to babaa terminates. Every maximal derivation from ww has length D(w)=∑k=m−10dk(2⌊xk/2⌋−1),D(w) = \sum_{k=m-1}^{0} d_k \bigl(2^{\lfloor x_k/2 \rfloor} - 1\bigr), where dkd_k is the number of bb’s to the right of run kk when its turn comes (the original m−km - k plus one for every step already made), and D(n)=max1≤b≤nb(2⌊(n−b)/2⌋−1)=Θ(2n/2),D(n) = \max_{1 \le b \le n} b\,\bigl(2^{\lfloor (n-b)/2 \rfloor} - 1\bigr) = \Theta(2^{n/2}), explicitly D(n)=2n/2−2D(n) = 2^{n/2} - 2 for even n≥2n \ge 2 and 3⋅2(n−3)/2−33 \cdot 2^{(n-3)/2} - 3 for odd n≥5n \ge 5 (the Skeptic’s simplification).

Proof idea. A pair of aa’s, once released, always travels all the way to the last run, so a run releases its pairs one after another, each journey twice as long as the previous one. ∎

Corollary 21.3 [PROVEN]. All 868 rules of the extended census are classified: 480 terminate and 388 do not.

All three statements were checked on every word of length ≤12\le 12 for all eight rules; the rightmost and leftmost strategies agree on every word, as random descent predicts (EXP-C20-01). The independent Skeptic re-checked the written proofs step by step and extended the checks to all words of length ≤18\le 18 and ≤21\le 21.

What the literature already knew. Both rules are right barren in McNaughton’s sense, and barren one-rule systems are known to terminate (McNaughton; Geser, ICASE Report 2002-33). So the termination half is a rediscovery. Geser’s report proves linear derivation lengths for its non-barren rules; for barren rules, as these examples show, the lengths can be exponential — and here they are known exactly. (The report’s introduction announces linear lengths for all terminating rules with one pair of overlaps; aab→ababaaab \to ababa has one pair of overlaps and exponential length, so only the body’s narrower statement is correct — a point the Skeptic made.)

21.4 A literature question, settled by the Historian

Chapter 4 conjectured a divisibility criterion for when 0p1q→1r0s0^p 1^q \to 1^r 0^s does not terminate. Zantema and Geser published a complete characterisation of these rules (tech. report 1994, journal 2000). The Director’s attempts to obtain the full text failed (HTTP 403, a bot challenge, HTTP 500); the Historian then found the scanned report on an older archive host. Their Theorem 1.1 is equivalent to our conjecture (it characterises termination, ours non-termination, with the same cases) — now Theorem 4.6. The 24 rules we had left open all terminate.

21.5 The open problems of batch 1

Volume 10’s catalogue (next chapter) lists every unproven claim of the database. The most interesting ones, in the laboratory’s judgement:

  1. Common borders (Chapter 2): the class-count formula with two or more common borders — and, in the one-border case, the injectivity lemma that would finish the proof.
  2. Finite spectra (Chapters 7–8): of the 17 laws whose anchored models seemed to exist only for N∈{1,2}N \in \{1, 2\} or {1,3}\{1, 3\}, 16 are now proved (Chapter 8); x(yz)=y((yx)z)x(yz) = y((yx)z) remains open (spectrum {1,2}\{1, 2\} supported up to N=12N = 12).
  3. The anchor instance of left-distributivity (Chapter 19): the instance x⋆(y+1)=(x⋆y)⋆(x+1)x \star (y+1) = (x \star y) \star (x+1) forces at most one table for every NN, and for N≤4096N \le 4096 a model exists exactly at the powers of 2. Prove that it never exists otherwise.
  4. Rows of mex operations (Chapter 14): the structure beyond the Jacobsthal row.
  5. Octal games (Chapter 5): the periodicity of 0.06 and Officers (famous and hard).
  6. Circle-product thresholds (Chapter 9): the exact associativity thresholds, e.g. whether tribonacci is associative at shift 4 or 5. The first version of the book got five of them wrong, and the laboratory’s first correction two more; only lower bounds are certified.
  7. Spectra of ETP laws 677 and 1313 (Chapter 6): which even orders below 16 occur, and which odd ones — the conjectured answer of the first version was refuted by models of order 16.
  8. The longest Zeckendorf game (Chapter 16): prove the recursion that predicts L(n)L(n) for every nn.

Open problems

Volume 10 — Open problems and future research. Generated from the claim database and the cycle records.

Unproven claims

Each of these is supported by the evidence cited in its record but has no proof. A proof or a counterexample would settle it.

  • CLM-C01-08 (computationally supported): For all words u != v of equal length k over a q-letter alphabet, the number of congruence classes of length-n words modulo u = v has generating function c(x) / (x^k + (1 - q x) c(x)), where c(x) = 1 + sum over words b that are borders of both u and v of x^(k - |b|). Evidence: EXP-C01-05, EXP-C01-06, EXP-C01-07.
  • CLM-C01-10 (computationally supported): If u != v of length k share exactly one common border z (neither border set containing the other), the class counts have generating function c/(x^k + (1 - q x) c) with c = 1 + x^(k - |z|). Evidence: EXP-C01-06, EXP-C01-07, EXP-C01-08.
  • CLM-C01-11 (computationally supported): For words u != v of equal length, the one-rule system {u -> v} is confluent if and only if every border of u is a border of v. Evidence: EXP-C01-08.
  • CLM-C03-11 (computationally supported): For 0 1^q -> 1^(q+1) 0^k the rightmost strategy from 0 1^(q(q+1)) reaches a self-embedding after 16, 32, 64 steps (k = 2) and 260, 1556, 9332 steps (k = 6) for q = 4, 5, 6. Evidence: EXP-C03-04.
  • CLM-C04-12 (computationally supported): The 162 terminating rules (55 symmetry classes) have derivational complexity linear (144 rules, of which 4 are n - Theta(log n)), quadratic (14: floor(n^2/4), floor(n(n-1)/6), floor(n^2/8)) or exponential (4: 2^(n-1) - 1); for 54 of the 55 classes D(n) = floor((a n^2 + b n + c)/d) or 2^(n-1) - 1 on all computed terms. Evidence: EXP-C04-03.
  • CLM-C04-16 (conjecture): The Grundy sequences of the rewriting games of aa -> ab, aaa -> b and aaa -> ab on the words a^n are ultimately periodic. Evidence: EXP-C04-08, EXP-C04-09.
  • CLM-C05-02 (computationally supported): Of the 115 identities t = x (x occurring in t, neither leftmost nor rightmost), 52 have models of every order 1..6, 2 have none of orders 2..6, and 61 have restricted spectra; the 63 non-full ones fall into 15 patterns on n = 1..8 listed in research/cycles/C05_magma_spectra/README.md (e.g. 10111011 for 16 identities including the Dupont law, 10110010 = Mendelsohn pattern for law 66 and three equivalents, 1010001? for law 907). Evidence: EXP-C05-01, EXP-C05-02.
  • CLM-C06-10 (computationally supported): The seven laws x(yz) = ((zx)x)y, x(yz) = ((zx)y)x, x(yz) = ((zy)x)x, (xy)z = y((zx)y), (xy)z = y((zy)x), (xy)z = z((yx)z), (xy)z = z((yz)x) have anchored models exactly for N in {1, 3}; the ten laws x(yz) = y((yx)z), x(yz) = ((xx)y)z, x(yz) = ((xx)z)y, x(yz) = ((xy)x)z, x(yz) = ((xy)y)z, x(yz) = ((xy)z)x, x(yz) = ((xy)z)y, x(yz) = ((xz)x)y, x(yz) = ((xz)y)x, x(yz) = ((xz)y)y have anchored models exactly for N in {1, 2}. Evidence: EXP-C06-03, EXP-C06-04.
  • CLM-C06-11 (computationally supported): (xy)z = x(z(yy)) has the same anchored model counts as the golden law (xy)z = x(y(zz)), and (xy)z = x((zy)y) the same as the odd law (xy)z = x((yz)z), for N <= 9; their affine spectra coincide for N <= 40. Evidence: EXP-C06-04.
  • CLM-C07-08 (computationally supported): Among the 134 distinct mex operations, 22 have additively periodic rows a = 1..5 (period <= 375 within b < 1500), 40 have none, and the rest are mixed. Evidence: EXP-C07-03.
  • CLM-C08-02 (computationally supported): If the minimal linear recurrence of an increasing base sequence G (G_0 = 1) has a dominant root alpha > 1 and another characteristic root beta with |beta| >= 1 (counted with multiplicity: a repeated dominant root counts as such a beta), then the circle product o_s is non-associative for every s >= 0. Evidence: EXP-C08-02, EXP-C08-07.
  • CLM-C08-03 (computationally supported): For base sequences G_k = a_1 G_(k-1) + … + a_d G_(k-d) with a_1 >= a_2 >= … >= a_d >= 1 and canonical initial values (the conditions of Grabner-Petho-Tichy-Woeginger 1994), the circle product o_s is associative once the shift s is large enough. (The earlier title, “Pisot base sequences …”, overstated the published theorem.) Evidence: EXP-C08-02, EXP-C08-03, EXP-C08-06, EXP-C08-07.
  • CLM-C08-04 (computationally supported): For quadratic Pisot base sequences G(k+1) = pG(k) + qG(k-1) with non-canonical initial values (for canonical ones the associative shifts are upward closed whatever the sign of beta, by Messaoudi’s Thm 2 as derived by the Historian): if the conjugate root beta is positive, the set of shifts s for which o_s is associative is upward-closed; sets that are not upward-closed (e.g. {2} and s >= 4 for G = 1,3,4,7,11,…) occur only when beta < 0. Evidence: EXP-C08-07, EXP-C08-06, EXP-C08-04, EXP-C08-03.
  • CLM-C08-08 (computationally supported): For the circle product o_s: Fibonacci (1,2) fails for s <= 1, associative for s >= 2 (Lean, CLM-C08-01); Pell (1,2,5) fails at s = 0 (associative for s >= 1, Messaoudi 2000); G(k+1) = 2G(k) + 2G(k-1) (1,2) fails for s <= 6; G(k+1) = 3G(k) + 3G(k-1) (1,5) fails for s <= 11; tribonacci (1,2,4) fails for s <= 3; 4-bonacci (1,2,4,8) fails for s <= 7; G(k+1) = G(k) + G(k-2) (1,2,3) fails for s <= 10; G(k+1) = G(k) + G(k-3) (1,2,3,4) fails for every s <= 16 (the Skeptic - every s <= 24). Every failure is certified by an explicit triple. Above these bounds no failure was found up to s = 16 by the targeted search of EXP-C08-07 and by the Skeptic’s independent extreme-conjugate search - computational support only. Evidence: EXP-C08-07, EXP-C08-06.
  • CLM-C09-05 (conjecture): Almost every n whose conjugate lies in the product zone is a circle product, so the circle irreducibles have natural density 1/phi^2. Evidence: EXP-C09-04, EXP-C09-01.
  • CLM-C10-06 (computationally supported): For carry rules with two or more nonzero b_j, the average digit-change cost per increment converges to a constant exceeding 1 + 1/(m - B) (measured excess: 0.500 for 3->(1,1), 0.333 for 4->(1,1), 0.643 for 3->(0,1,1), 0.714 for 3->(1,0,1), 0.831 for 4->(1,1,1)), equal to an expectation under the invariant measure of the digit process. Evidence: EXP-C10-03.
  • CLM-C11-08 (computationally supported): Among fixed (untuned) universal codes, a pattern-terminated code (T_101, T_0111 or T_00111) has the smallest expected length on 5 of 12 synthetic sources (Zipf 1.1, geometric 0.001, uniform 2^16, uniform 2^32, log-uniform on [1, 1e6]) and on both posting-list data sets, beating the best classical universal code (Elias, Levenshtein, Fibonacci, Fraenkel-Klein C3, LEB128, fixed Exp-Golomb) by 0.34-3.06 bits per value; on Zipf 1.1, log-uniform and the bursty posting list it also beats Golomb and Exp-Golomb codes tuned to the source. It loses on light-tailed sources (Zipf >= 1.5, geometric >= 0.01, prime gaps) and to tuned Golomb/Exp-Golomb codes on geometric and uniform sources, and the reference implementation is 4-10 times slower than the Elias and Exp-Golomb codes. Evidence: EXP-C11-03, EXP-C11-04, EXP-C11-05, EXP-C11-09.
  • CLM-C12-12 (computationally supported): The Grundy values of the subtraction game whose move set is the set of primes satisfy g(n) <= 11 for all n. Evidence: EXP-C12-03, EXP-C12-01.
  • CLM-C12-13 (conjecture): The Grundy values of the subtraction game with move set {1, 2, 3, 5, 8, …} (Fibonacci numbers) are bounded; the observed maximum is 14 for n <= 2e5. Evidence: EXP-C12-03, EXP-C12-01.
  • CLM-C13-10 (computationally supported): Every one-variable law t1(x) = t2(x) has an anchored spectrum that is either finite or contains all sufficiently large N. Evidence: EXP-C13-07.
  • CLM-C13-12 (computationally supported): All 146 non-rigid laws from infinite affine classes in EXP-C13-02 have non-affine anchored models at every N = 6, 7, 8; no law other than left-distributivity was found whose non-affine models occur only at sparse sizes such as powers of two. Evidence: EXP-C13-02, EXP-C13-05, EXP-C13-06.
  • CLM-C14-12 (computationally supported): Every connected graph has exactly one factorisation into connected primes with respect to the product (g,h) ~ (g’,h’) iff (EA, AE, AA or NN); equivalently every graph with connected complement factors uniquely under the symmetric-difference product into primes with connected complement. Evidence: EXP-C14-04.
  • CLM-C14-13 (computationally supported): Every switching class of finite graphs has a unique factorisation into prime switching classes with respect to the product induced by the symmetric difference (equivalently, symmetric +-1 matrices with unit diagonal up to signed permutation similarity, under the Kronecker product). Evidence: EXP-C14-05.
  • CLM-C15-07 (computationally supported): Of the 1734 order-3 magma automata (up to isomorphism and mirror image), on t <= 9 the streaming width is bounded for 808 classes, grows linearly for 104, geometrically for 300 (largest lambda(9) = 649) and irregularly for 522; 43 are surjective, 7 injective and 34 nilpotent by t = 9. Evidence: EXP-C15-02.
  • CLM-C15-13 (computationally supported): Computing the row at time t of an affine magma automaton by binary exponentiation of P = a + bS modulo S^L - 1 (FFT products) is exact and, at L = 1024, faster than direct numpy simulation by about one order of magnitude at t = 10^3, two orders at 10^4 (138-314x in two runs) and three orders at 10^5 (1,459-2,500x); t = 10^9 takes milliseconds for L <= 4096. With the same timing protocol on both sides it loses for t below about 20. Evidence: EXP-C15-03.
  • CLM-C15-14 (computationally supported): The exponent-arithmetic fast-forward (Theorem 3) is exact; at L = 1024 it is about two orders of magnitude faster than direct simulation at t = 10^5 (62-100x; the Skeptic’s re-run 79-97x) but no faster around t = 10^3 (0.5-1.0x) for L >= 1024, and equal for t < 2L (where it falls back to simulation). Evidence: EXP-C15-03.
  • CLM-C15-15 (computationally supported): The burn-time fast-forward of the fuse magma (Theorem 4) is exact and takes O(L) time for every t - about three orders of magnitude faster than direct simulation at t = 10^5 (L = 1024; 4,021x in the first run, 1,363x on the Skeptic’s re-run), with t = 10^9 in about a millisecond; because the O(L) method is a Python loop it loses to simulation for t below about 20-70. Evidence: EXP-C15-03.
  • CLM-C15-16 (computationally supported): Storing visited rows answers a query t in mu + lambda simulated steps; for random rows of length 32 this is at most 5000 steps for 1590 of 1734 order-3 classes (median transient 5, median period 2), but it fails for the affine quasigroups and for 128 of 300 geometric-width classes. Evidence: EXP-C15-04.
  • CLM-C15-17 (computationally supported): The Laver tables A_2 and A_3 give automata whose light cones depend on at most 3 resp. 4 cells (t <= 8 resp. 5) with streaming (forward) widths 4 and 8 (backward widths 5 and 13-14); GF(4) multiplication and XOR on [0,4) have streaming width 4; the anchored golden- and odd-law models of C06 are permutive (hence surjective) affine automata. Evidence: EXP-C15-05.
  • CLM-C15-18 (conjecture): Every magma automaton with bounded streaming width admits an algorithm computing a cyclic row of length L at time t in time polynomial in L and log t. Evidence: EXP-C15-02, EXP-C15-03.
  • CLM-C16-10 (computationally supported): For every n >= 5 there is a quasigroup of order n whose Fibonacci pair map is a single cycle through all n^2 pairs. Evidence: EXP-C16-02, EXP-C16-05.
  • CLM-C16-13 (computationally supported): For every affine magma x*y = alpha x + beta y + gamma on Z/N with beta a unit, the maximal period of the pair map equals the order of [[0,1,0],[beta,alpha,gamma],[0,0,1]] mod N. Evidence: EXP-C16-03.
  • CLM-C16-16 (computationally supported): The longest transient of the Laver pair map (steps until the state contains the top element) is 1, 3, 3 for n = 1, 2, 3 and exactly 5 for every n = 4, …, 10 (the Skeptic - every n = 4, …, 13). Evidence: EXP-C16-03.
  • CLM-C16-17 (computationally supported): On a simple battery (byte chi-square, serial pairs, lowest-bit period, lag-1 correlation; 2^20 outputs) the anchored Fibonacci and affine magma generators on Z/2^32 fail low-byte uniformity and have lowest-bit period 3, and the de Bruijn byte magma (maximal possible period 65536) is too regular; PCG64 passes every test. Evidence: EXP-C16-04.
  • CLM-C16-19 (computationally supported): With fixed budgets, lexicographic DFS (3e6 nodes) and a SAT encoding (3e5 conflicts) find full-period quasigroups only up to n = 8, while seeded sampling of random Latin squares finds them for every n = 5..16 at rates close to 1/n^2 (H-C16-11). Evidence: EXP-C16-05, EXP-C16-02.
  • CLM-C17-04 (computationally supported): Certification (associative inputs, n = 32..512) - Light’s test with a greedy generating set uses 23-383 times (cyclic groups) and 38-614 times (anchored tables) fewer table probes than the exhaustive n^3 check (generating-set cost included), the anchored test 2n^2 probes; on left-zero bands the gain is only 1.7x. Refutation (one-entry corruptions, random magmas) - an exhaustive check that stops at the first violating triple is about as cheap as Light’s test or much cheaper (4 probes on random magmas against about 9e5; 0.1-1.1x Light’s probes on corruptions), so the gains concern certification only. The randomised test (40 rounds) made no error; random-triple sampling with n^2/16 samples missed 27 of 30 near-associative inputs. Evidence: EXP-C17-01.
  • CLM-C18-08 (computationally supported): QG-LS p2+rev has mean and maximal avalanche bias within 0.01 of BLAKE2b-64 on 2000 random 16-byte keys. Evidence: EXP-C18-02.
  • CLM-C18-13 (computationally supported): Among 13 tail-output quasigroup hashes and 4 cascades, measured against FNV-1a-64, MX64 (multiply-xorshift with the splitmix64 finaliser), BLAKE2b-64 and CRC32 on avalanche, bit independence, chi-square, collisions and throughput, no quasigroup construction improves significantly on the best baseline in any measured dimension (some have numerically smaller maximal bias or bit-independence values, within noise). Evidence: EXP-C18-02, EXP-C18-03, EXP-C18-04, EXP-C18-05.
  • CLM-C19-05 (computationally supported): Let h(k) = floor(k^2/4) and define rho_p (p = 0, 1) by rho_p(0) = 0 and, for m = F_{i+1} + t with Zeckendorf top level i (0 <= t < F_i): rho_p(m) = r_p(i) + [i = p mod 2] * max(0, rho_p(t) - h(i-2)), where r_p(0) = 1, r_p(i) = h(i) + 2 for i >= 1, i = p mod 2, and r_p(i) = h(i+1) + 1 otherwise. Then D(0) = 0 and D(F_{j+1} + m) = h(j) + D(m) - rho_{j mod 2}(m) for 0 <= m < F_j, and L(n) = B(n) - D(n). Evidence: EXP-C19-03, EXP-C19-04, EXP-C19-05.
  • CLM-C19-06 (computationally supported): For levels j >= i + 2, D(F_{j+1} + F_{i+1}) = floor(j^2/4) + floor(i^2/4) - R(j,i) with R(j,0) = 1, R(j,i) = floor((i+1)^2/4) + 1 if j - i is odd and R(j,i) = floor(i^2/4) + 2 if j - i is even (i >= 1). Evidence: EXP-C19-03.
  • CLM-C19-09 (computationally supported): For 1 <= n < 30000, the deficit satisfies D(n) <= floor(j^2/4) where j is the top Zeckendorf level of n (equality at the Fibonacci numbers), and L(n+1) > L(n). Evidence: EXP-C19-03.

Directions proposed by the research cycles

C01 — Quantity as the complete invariant of rewriting.

  • C01: prove the injectivity lemma that completes the one-common-border case (CLM-C01-10), then attack two or more common borders
  • C01: full Lean formalisation of the border-containment theorem
  • C01: Lean formalisation of the min/gcd theorem (Bezout over N)
  • C01: common-border formula for pairs of relations?

C02 — Exchange systems: positional numeration as normal forms.

  • C02: explicit bound n0 for critical rules; Lean formalisation of Theorem 10 (Perron-Frobenius argument)
  • C02: longest Zeckendorf game - closed form or asymptotics
  • C02: which carry rules have regular representation languages?

C03 — Termination of one-rule string rewriting 0^p 1^q -> 1^r 0^s.

  • C03: retrieve the Zantema-Geser theorem statement and compare with H-C03-4
  • C03: implement RFC match-bounds to prove termination of the 24 open rules (start with 0011 -> 111000 and 0011 -> 11100000)
  • C03: prove that every base rule 0 1^q -> 1^r 0^k (q < r < 2q, k >= 2) loops from 0 1^(q(q+1)); explain k^q loop lengths
  • C03: Lean formalisation of the conservation theorem (s = p)

C04 — Derivation lengths, random descent and rewriting games.

  • C04: decide termination of aab -> ababa and aab -> babaa
  • C04: games of aba -> u and bab -> u (alternating runs)
  • C04: complexity census for |l| = 4 - new non-rational classes? cubic growth?
  • C04: OEIS submissions (0.06 Grundy values; formula for A247983) - human decision

C05 — Spectra of single-identity magma varieties.

  • C05: decide laws 677 and 1313 at orders 12 and 14, and law 1286 at 8 and 10, with the case split of EXP-C05-08 (Lemma 8); certify
  • C05: a certified search for finite models of 677 that violate 255 (x = ((xx)x)x), the ETP’s open finite implication, at the next orders with models
  • C05: which odd orders are in the spectra of 677 and 1313? (677 has no linear model at 17, 23, 29; 1313 none at 11, 29; 15 is not a prime power)
  • C05: extend the census to order-5 laws and three-variable identities
  • C05: Lean-formalise orbit-counting arguments for other laws with a finite-order pair map

C06 — Successor-anchored magmas: spectra of composition laws.

  • C06: prove the finite spectra {1,2} and {1,3} (CLM-C06-10) by showing every anchored model of those laws is affine
  • C06: which subsets of N arise as anchored spectra? They are closed under coprime products (CLM-C06-12), so never the primes; are they always closed under divisors? (bold)
  • C06: larger terms - are there non-LD laws with power-of-two (Laver-like, non-affine) spectra?
  • C06: Lean formalisation of the golden-law correspondence (Theorem 6)

C07 — Arithmetic from the minimal excludant: a census of mex-defined operations.

  • C07: rows a >= 3 of D[x^y] - an exploratory look suggested row 3 is injective and rows 4-6 are not; is there a recursive block structure?
  • C07: prove (or refute) that the 33 remaining specs equal their associative formulas for all a, b
  • C07: submit the Jacobsthal row and selected census sequences to the OEIS (human decision)
  • C07: which mex operations are automatic sequences (2-automatic tables)? A Walnut-style decision procedure would turn observations into proofs

C08 — Circle multiplications on recurrence numeration systems.

  • C08: complete the Lean proof of the shifted Knuth theorem
  • C08: prove non-Pisot never-associativity (CLM-C08-02, dominant root > 1)
  • C08: compute greedy windows exactly (Rauzy fractal boundaries) to turn the threshold searches of EXP-C08-07 into proofs
  • C08: compute greedy windows to prove the sign-of-beta conjecture (CLM-C08-04)
  • C09: arithmetic of the circle semigroup - irreducibles and factorisation

C09 — Arithmetic of Knuth’s circle semigroup.

  • C09: prove the density-1/phi^2 conjecture
  • C09: characterise circle irreducibles via prime factorisation in Z[phi] plus a unit-window condition
  • C09: arithmetic of other associative circle products (Pell, tribonacci)

C10 — The cost of counting across representations.

  • C10: exact multi-offset counting constants via the invariant measure of the digit process

C11 — Pattern-terminated universal integer codes.

  • C11: table-driven (byte-wise) decoders for T_p and a fair speed comparison with optimised Elias and Fibonacci decoders
  • C11: multi-pattern terminators (sets of delimiters, as in multi-delimiter codes) and the best set per source
  • C11: synchronisation and robustness after bit errors for unbordered terminators (the classical selling point of Fibonacci codes)
  • C11: exact oscillation constants for the average gain of T_011 over C3 (as Proposition 5 does for 1^k)

C12 — Relational one-heap games.

  • C12: boundedness of the Grundy values of subtract-a-prime (11) and subtract-a-Fibonacci (14); convergence of the Fibonacci P-density (<= 1/5)
  • C12: forbidden-move perturbations - for which d0 does forbidding ‘subtract d0’ in G01 give finitely many exceptional odd parts as in Theorem 8?
  • C12: prove g(m) < m/4 for odd m in the non-coprime game G06 (max ratio 0.24985 up to 5000)
  • C12: formalise Theorem 8 in Lean (finite certificate plus the shift-invariance lemma)

C13 — Anchored spectra II: which sets of integers are spectra?.

  • C13: prove the last case of CLM-C06-10, x(yz) = y((yx)z) (spectrum {1,2}, with a non-affine model at N = 2)
  • C13: which arithmetic sets are TRUE spectra of rigid laws? Find a rigid law realising ‘-1 is a square mod N’ (A008784)
  • C13: prove that spectra of one-variable laws are finite or cofinite (CLM-C13-10)
  • C13: search larger laws for a non-affine sparse (Laver-like) spectrum other than left-distributivity
  • C13: submit the tribonacci-law and plastic-number spectra to the OEIS (human decision)

C14 — Graphs as quantities: the 256 local graph products.

  • C14: prove or refute that two-graphs are a free commutative monoid under the symmetric-difference product (twin reducts; analogue of McKenzie’s theorem for Kronecker products of +-1 matrices)
  • C14: unique factorisation of product 141 on connected graphs (no collision to order 15)
  • C14: classify the graphs G that absorb switching (G xor X = G xor X^Z for all X); K_{a,a} do
  • C14: characters for directed graphs and for products of graphs with loops

C15 — Magma cellular automata: light cones, streaming width and fast-forwarding.

  • C15: characterise bounded streaming width - what do the 685 unexplained bounded-width order-3 classes have in common?
  • C15: prove or refute CLM-C15-18 (bounded width => polylog-time fast-forward); find fast-forwards for non-commutative semigroups via the popcount word
  • C15: are the ‘geometric’ widths really exponential? Lower-bound proofs for one chaotic order-3 magma
  • C15: light cones of Laver tables - is the dependence always bounded by the order’s 2-adic size?

C16 — Fibonacci dynamics in invented finite algebras.

  • C16: prove that full-period quasigroups exist for every n >= 5 (CLM-C16-10) - a recursive or algebraic construction for even n is needed, since isotopes of groups of even order are excluded
  • C16: count full-period quasigroups of order 6 (a partial DFS found 1136 normalised cycles within 2e7 nodes without finishing) and compare with L(6)/36
  • C16: prove CLM-C16-13 (affine maximal period = order of the 3x3 matrix) and explain the Laver transient bound 5
  • C16: which laws force short periods? (idempotency gives n fixed points; left-distributivity with an anchor forces period <= 2)

C17 — Deciding laws of finite magmas: generator, anchor and randomised tests against the exhaustive check.

  • C17: formalise the existence of the Laver tables (Laver’s theorem) in Lean, which would make CLM-C17-08 and CLM-C17-05 unconditional
  • C17: generator-based tests for other laws (left-distributivity, mediality) - which laws admit a Light-type reduction?
  • C17: benchmark against optimised C implementations and against SAT-based model checkers

C18 — Invented operations as mixing primitives: quasigroup string transformations versus standard hashes.

  • C18: wide-state quasigroup cascades with larger quasigroups (e.g. order 2^16) and an optimised C implementation, run through a full SMHasher-style suite before any claim of usefulness
  • C18: explain the residual bias of the nimber-inversion cascade (x*x = 0 and commutativity) - which quasigroup properties hurt cascades?
  • C18: a probabilistic model of the cascade over a random Latin square - how does the avalanche bias decay with the depth?

C19 — The longest Zeckendorf game.

  • C19: prove the recursion CLM-C19-05 by analysing the normalisation phase from the lazy representation (carries through alternating blocks)
  • C19: prove D(n) <= floor(j^2/4) and the strict monotonicity of L
  • C19: generalise the lazy-phase theorem and the Fibonacci-input formula to the generalised Zeckendorf games of Baird-Smith et al. (other linear recurrences)
  • C19: submit L(n) and L(F_k) to the OEIS (human decision)

C20 — Open problems revisited: the last one-rule systems of C04, and a literature check for C03.

  • C20: derivational complexity of all terminating barren one-rule systems with |l| <= 4 - is it always n - O(1), polynomial or 2^Theta(n)?
  • C20: formalise Theorems 1-2 of proofs/paper/C20.md in Lean (run encoding + journeys)

Corrections recorded in the journal

  • C03-14 (C03): Correction of journal entry C03-11 - the larger matrix-interpretation tiers it mentions (upper-triangular d = 3 entries <= 5, d = 4 entries <= 2, full d = 3 entries <= 1) were searched outside the committed experiments and are not reproducible from the repository; only the tiers listed in the README are recorded evidence. The claim records do not rely on the unrecorded searches.
  • C06-11 (C06): EXP-C06-03 and EXP-C06-04 stored a wall-clock runtime inside the hashed results, so every re-run looked like a changed result; the audit-log entry ‘reproduced: false’ for EXP-C06-03 (skeptic re-run) reflects only that time stamp. The runtime now lives under ‘timings’ and both experiments were re-run.
